引言

第 01 章 · 章节速读

这本书讨论一个核心问题：如何把仿真、优化和 AI 结合起来，在不确定的环境中做出更好的决策。

仿真：先试一试
在计算机中重现系统，观察不同方案可能产生的结果。
优化：选一个更好的方案
根据仿真结果比较方案，并合理分配有限的计算预算。
AI：从经验中学习
学习预测模型或决策策略，同时用仿真检验效果。

阅读顺序：仿真优化基础 → 机器学习 → 两者结合 → 实际应用。

展开完整原文（英文）

Simulation and artificial intelligence (AI) provide essential tools for the analysis and optimization of complex dynamic systems found in critical application domains including manufacturing, supply chains, transportation, healthcare, telecommunications, call centers, and finance. Simulation emulates natural and man-made processes with the objective of evaluating system performance, enabling practitioners to inform their decision-making by predicting outcomes and assessing different strategies without costly real-world experiments. However, simulating large-scale stochastic systems necessitates substantial computational resources, as accurate performance assessments require a sufficient number of simulation replications to achieve desired statistical precision. Thus, early “optimization” of simulation models were primarily trial-and-error approaches and heuristic algorithms, which did not scale well and usually had no guarantees of optimality. Over the past few decades, significant strides have been made in the integration of simulation and optimization methods.

Historically, simulation and optimization are centuries-old fields, both tracing their roots back to Newton and Leibniz, whereas AI and the Monte Carlo (MC) method, which serves as the basis of stochastic simulation, the focus of this review, both originated in the mid-20th century (with MC preceding AI by about a decade), and simulation optimization (SO) did not become a common term until even later in the 20th century. Since its birth, major advances in AI have appeared to come in waves, with progress driven by advances in computational power, algorithmic innovation, and the exponential growth of data.

Early AI research focused on symbolic reasoning and rule-based systems, following pioneers such as Alan Turing, who proposed the concept of a universal machine capable of performing any computation, and John McCarthy, who coined the term “artificial intelligence” and introduced the programming language LISP, and also exemplified by the development of the Logic Theorist, which aimed to emulate human problem-solving abilities. Over its relatively brief lifetime, AI has experienced periods of rapid progress followed by significant setbacks often referred to as “AI winters,” due to unmet expectations and limited computational resources. The resurgence of AI in recent decades, culminating in the development of large language models (LLMs), can be attributed to breakthroughs in machine learning (ML), particularly deep learning (DL), which have enabled the development of models capable of processing and learning from vast amounts of data with unprecedented accuracy and efficiency.

The current “golden age” of AI reflects a paradigm shift from traditional symbolic reasoning to modern data-driven approaches. Contemporary techniques harness advanced ML and deep artificial neural network (ANN) architectures, as exemplified by LLMs, which significantly enhance natural language understanding and generative capabilities. ML, a pillar of modern AI, focuses on developing algorithms that allow systems to learn from and make predictions based on data. Supervised learning, unsupervised learning, and reinforcement learning (RL) are core methodologies of ML, each catering to different types of problems and data structures. DL, a specialized branch of ML, utilizes multi-layered ANNs to model complex patterns and representations in data, leading to breakthroughs in fields such as computer vision, natural language processing, and speech recognition. Additionally, advances in RL have empowered AI systems to excel in decision-making tasks through interactions with their environment, as demonstrated by the success of AlphaGo and its successors.

The impact of AI can be found everywhere. In healthcare, AI-driven models assist in disease diagnosis, personalized treatment planning, and predictive analytics for patient outcomes, thereby enhancing the quality and efficiency of medical services. In the automotive industry, AI underpins the development of autonomous vehicles, enabling real-time navigation, obstacle detection, and decision-making to ensure safe and efficient transportation. The financial sector leverages AI for algorithmic trading, risk assessment, and fraud detection, utilizing sophisticated models to analyze market trends and detect anomalous activities with high precision.

However, the most conspicuous and ubiquitous presence of AI in modern life may be generative LLM-based agents such as OpenAI’s line of generative pre-trained transformers (GPTs) and Google DeepMind’s Gemini (formerly, Bard). These LLM-enabled AI agents possess advanced language understanding and generative capabilities, allowing them to perform complex tasks, engage in natural language dialogues, and adapt to new situations with minimal human intervention. Their learning ability represents a huge leap from traditional agent-based simulation (ABS) environments, which typically consist of agents with predefined rules. The integration of LLMs enhances the cognitive flexibility of AI agents, enabling more dynamic interactions and decision-making processes. The relationship between LLM-enabled AI agents and traditional ABS lies in their shared goal of modeling intelligent behavior; however, LLMs introduce a level of linguistic and contextual understanding that can enrich agent-based models by facilitating more realistic simulations of communication and learning among agents.

To give a preview of the interplay between research in AI and SO, advances in AI/ML have led to significant enhancements in simulation modeling and analysis, such as the use of ANNs as surrogate models to approximate the outputs of a complex stochastic simulation model. By training ANNs on a subset of simulation output data, accurate predictions for new input parameters can be rapidly generated, significantly reducing the computational burden for performance evaluation or estimating the objective function in an optimization problem. Thus, using surrogate models leads to orders of magnitudes reduction in computation in the optimization aspect, enabling real-time integrated simulation and optimization, providing a path to the digital twin (DT) technology supporting decision-making under uncertainty in dynamic environments.

Furthermore, AI-driven optimization algorithms, such as RL, have been effectively applied within simulation frameworks, such as agent-based systems. RL agents interact with simulated environments to learn optimal decision-making strategies through trial-and-error, navigating complex decision spaces to maximize cumulative rewards. Using simulations as training grounds allows for extensive experimentation without incurring the risks and costs of real-world trials, a strategy that has proven valuable in the development of autonomous systems such as self-driving cars and intelligent robotics. In addition, variance reduction techniques and adaptive sampling strategies further refine simulation processes by allocating computational resources to the most critical regions of the input space, thereby enhancing both the reliability and precision of simulation outputs.

Conversely, the incorporation of SO techniques into AI applications has also led to notable real-world success stories, such as AlphaGo and its successors and progeny (AlphaGo Zero, AlphaZero, MuZero, and AlphaFold), which employ ANNs and Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). AlphaGo exemplifies the fusion of AI and SO in addressing complex strategic decision-making tasks. Developed by DeepMind, AlphaGo utilizes deep ANNs to evaluate board positions and predict potential moves, thereby effectively capturing the intricate patterns and strategies inherent in the game of Go. Central to its remarkable performance is the implementation of MCTS, a simulation-based optimization technique that systematically explores possible future game states by generating and assessing numerous potential move sequences. This synergy between AI-driven learning and simulation-based search enables AlphaGo to balance the exploration of novel strategies with the exploitation of known high-probability moves, resulting in superior performance against human champions. Additionally, AlphaGo incorporates RL to continuously refine its policy and value networks through extensive self-play, thereby enhancing its decision-making capabilities over time. The confluence of these AI methodologies with advanced SO not only facilitated AlphaGo’s unprecedented victories in Go but also demonstrated the potential of hybrid approaches in solving other complex, high-dimensional problems across various domains. Furthermore, the training of these systems through deep reinforcement learning (DRL) to approximate value and policy functions has been instrumental in achieving superhuman performance, highlighting the important role that SO methods play in AI technologies.

AI’s relevance in the context of SO stems from its capacity to learn from vast datasets, identify intricate patterns, and make accurate predictions. ML facilitates the development of predictive models that can be seamlessly incorporated into simulation frameworks, thereby enhancing their accuracy and efficiency. For instance, DL models are capable of approximating complex input-output relationships within simulations, which allows for more precise optimization and reduces the computational burden associated with traditional simulation methods. This synergy between AI and SO is increasingly being leveraged to address high-dimensional and non-linear problems that are otherwise challenging to solve using conventional optimization techniques. By harnessing the predictive power of AI, SO methods can achieve superior performance in terms of both speed and accuracy.

One of the most significant advances in the integration of AI and SO is the application of RL for SO. RL algorithms, such as Q-learning and policy gradient methods, have demonstrated remarkable promise in optimizing sequential decision-making problems within simulated environments. These algorithms are able to learn optimal policies through a “trial-and-error” paradigm (sampling and feedback), making them exceptionally well-suited for dynamic and complex systems where traditional optimization techniques often fall short. The application of RL in SO is evident in various domains, including robotic control, autonomous systems, and smart grid management. In robotic control, for example, RL algorithms enable robots to learn optimal movement strategies through interaction with their environment, thereby enhancing their adaptability and efficiency. Similarly, in autonomous systems, RL facilitates real-time decision-making and optimization, which are crucial for the safe and efficient operation of self-driving vehicles and other autonomous technologies.

ANNs form the backbone of modern AI, with their training processes inherently formulated as SO problems. The training of ANNs, particularly in DL and RL contexts, relies heavily on stochastic gradient estimation, which is crucial for adjusting network parameters to minimize loss functions. Techniques such as stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and its variants are fundamental to the effective training of ANNs, enabling them to learn complex patterns and representations from large datasets. The upper confidence bound algorithm for trees (UCT), derived from the multi-armed bandit (MAB) problem, is a popular node selection policy in MCTS for balancing exploration and exploitation. Variance reduction techniques for stochastic gradient estimation play a critical role in mitigating the oscillations of parameter search trajectories, thereby accelerating the training process of ANNs. Moreover, reducing the variance of the estimated action-value function through simulations enhances the accuracy of node selection policies in MCTS, leading to more reliable and effective decision-making processes. These advances highlight the synergistic interplay between AI and SO, where optimization techniques enhance the performance and reliability of AI models, and AI-driven methods, in turn, optimize complex simulation-based systems.

Moreover, the integration of AI into SO methodologies addresses several inherent limitations of traditional optimization techniques, particularly in dealing with high-dimensional and non-linear problems. Traditional optimization methods often struggle with scalability and computational efficiency when applied to large-scale, complex systems. AI techniques such as DL and RL offer scalable solutions that can efficiently handle the complexities associated with high-dimensional data and non-linear relationships. DL models can effectively approximate non-linear functions and capture intricate dependencies within data, enabling more accurate and efficient optimization. Similarly, RL algorithms can dynamically adapt to changing environments and optimize decision-making processes in real-time, providing significant advantages over static optimization methods. This capability is particularly valuable in applications such as autonomous systems and real-time resource management, where the ability to adapt and optimize on the fly is essential for maintaining optimal performance. For example, integrating DL models with SO techniques in manufacturing systems leads to more precise optimization of production schedules, reducing lead times and minimizing downtime. Similarly, in supply chain management, AI and SO approaches have optimized inventory levels and distribution logistics, resulting in enhanced responsiveness and reduced operational costs.

AI techniques significantly enhance the computational efficiency of SO by automating and accelerating the search for optimal solutions. Traditional SO methods often require extensive computational resources due to the need for numerous simulation runs to explore different scenarios. AlphaRank is an advanced AI methodology designed to enhance SO by addressing Ranking and Selection (R\(\&\)S) problems prevalent in management, engineering, and finance. R\(\&\)S tasks involve identifying the optimal option among numerous alternatives under uncertain conditions, often constrained by limited simulation budgets. AlphaRank integrates DRL with Monte Carlo simulations, modeling the sampling decision-making process as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) to effectively capture sequential decisions for optimal selection. A key innovation is the use of pre-trained neural networks, developed offline, which enable rapid and efficient real-time decision-making by striking the right balance between exploration – investigating less-tested alternatives to reduce uncertainty, and exploitation – focusing resources on options with high estimated performance. Moreover, AlphaRank employs a parallelizable computing framework with “divide-and-conquer” and recursion strategies, allowing it to efficiently manage large-scale R\(\&\)S problems involving thousands to millions of alternatives. This integration of AI and SO methodologies positions AlphaRank as a transformative tool, driving significant innovations and enabling superior operational excellence across various industries by resolving previously intractable problems.

The convergence of AI and SO will drive significant advances through interdisciplinary collaboration and technological innovation. The ongoing refinement of advanced AI algorithms such as DL and RL, in conjunction with progressive enhancements in simulation modeling and optimization techniques, will significantly broaden the capabilities and applications of these integrated methodologies. The expansion of high-performance computing infrastructure and the increasing availability of extensive datasets will facilitate the management and analysis of larger, more detailed simulation models, enabling more comprehensive and efficient optimization processes. Furthermore, collaborative efforts across diverse fields such as operations research, computer science, and engineering are expected to generate novel approaches and solutions, fostering the development of robust frameworks that synergize the complementary strengths of AI and SO. This multidisciplinary synergy will enhance the ability to model complex systems, improve predictive accuracy, and optimize decision-making processes across various domains, including autonomous systems, healthcare, and sustainable energy.

Scope and Organization of this Review

AI is a vast field, as are simulation and optimization, so any review must necessarily make choices on coverage. As indicated in this introduction, we will focus on the interaction and synergy between RL and SO for stochastic dynamic systems, perhaps the most natural and well-developed synergy between AI/ML and SO. The interaction goes both ways, as exemplified by methodologies such as policy gradient search, Q-learning, Monte Carlo tree search, and the training of neural networks. To enhance accessibility to an audience from diverse fields, we begin with background material on basic simulation modeling and analysis, and then focus on the simulation and stochastic optimization topics most relevant to the later discussion of research between SO and AI/ML: discrete optimization with noisy observations from a set of given alternatives, gradient-based search for continuous-valued decision variables, Markov decision processes, estimation of gradients for noisy output functions, and variance reduction techniques for stochastic simulation models. This is followed by an overview of a broad range of AI/ML topics, including linear and logistic regression, support vectors machines, and decision trees, as a prelude to the main topics of ANNs (also including gradient estimation) and RL. Because our own research in SO is ultimately driven by making decisions (prescriptive behavior), as opposed to descriptive or predictive models, RL aligns well, since it addresses sequential decision making under uncertainty.

Thus, the review is structured to present the specific required background on simulation and optimization first in Chapter 2, followed by basic AI/ML topics in Chapter 3, and the synergies between the two in Chapter 4, followed by applications and case studies in Chapter 5. The material should be accessible and of interest (we hope!) to researchers and graduate students from computer science, applied mathematics, operations research & management science, and most engineering fields (e.g., systems & industrial engineering, electrical & computer engineering, mechanical engineering, aeronautical engineering, civil engineering, and chemical engineering). Perhaps the only prerequisite is an undergraduate-level course in calculus-based probability (integration and differentiation but no measure theory required). The choice of placing the simulation and optimization material before the AI/ML material was deliberate. Although we hope to reach a wide audience, the dichotomy between those with an AI/ML background and those without any such training is such that the former can easily skip Chapter 3, whereas most others except the far smaller community of researchers whose primary focus is SO, would benefit from reading both background chapters. In particular, many graduate operations research and management science programs do not even require a stochastic simulation course, whereas industrial engineering courses may not cover topics like stochastic gradient estimation (or only at the universities where that select set of researchers resides). Furthermore, the topics that are reviewed in the optimization sections of Chapter 2 narrowly focus on the specific topics of statistical ranking & selection (for stochastic optimization over a discrete set) and stochastic gradient-based search (for stochastic optimization over variables taking values from a continuous set), which lie outside the standard OR/MS graduate curriculum in mathematical programming/optimization (linear programming, convex programming, integer programming, nonlinear programming, stochastic programming).

To be specific, the rest of this review is organized as follows. In Chapter 2, we overview the main stochastic simulation approaches (Monte Carlo, discrete-event, and agent-based), introduce the most relevant areas of discrete and continuous stochastic optimization for our discussion, cover the basic stochastic gradient estimation methods, and summarize several variance reduction techniques useful for AI. Chapter 3 summarizes the AI methodologies relevant to our discussions, namely traditional regression and classification methods, DL, RL, computer vision, and natural language processing. Chapter 4 delves into advanced topics that draw from both SO and AI/ML, such as forward learning for neural network training, policy gradient methods, Q-learning exploration, and MCTS, alongside AI-enhanced SO. Chapter 5 applies these methodologies to operations and supply chain management and presents several case studies and recent developments.

Notes and References

Except for Chapter 5, references are deferred to the end of each chapter, to avoid disrupting the narrative flow. In addition to providing some historical context and original references, we also recommend further reading, both textbooks and research monographs.

The renowned Turing test was introduced by Alan Turing in his 1950 paper (Turing 1950). The term “artificial intelligence” was coined at the 1956 Dartmouth Summer Research Project (Moor 2006) by John McCarthy, who also introduced in 1958 at MIT the programming language LISP (LISP 1996), which was to dominate AI research for decades.

Also introduced in 1956 was the Logic Theorist of Newell and Simon (1956).

Highly recommended graduate-level stochastic simulation textbooks (from the operations research, management science, industrial & systems engineering community) include Law and Kelton (2000; Glasserman 2004; Asmussen and Glynn 2007; Nelson and Pei 2021).

Books focusing on SO include Chen and Lee (2011; Fu 2015) and the older Pflug (1996; Fu and Hu 1997; Spall 2003). Mohamed et al. (2020) and Fu, Hu, and Scheinberg (2025) provide background on stochastic gradient estimation from the AI/ML and SO perspectives, respectively,

and a recent tutorial on SO and stochastic gradients can be found in Fu, Hu, Ryzhov, et al. (2025).

Monte Carlo tree search was introduced by Rémi Coulom (Coulom 2006), with its seeds contained in the adaptive multistage sampling algorithm of Chang et al. (2005), which used the upper confidence bound (UCB) algorithm of Auer et al. (2002) for MDPs. The UCT algorithm was introduced by Kocsis and Szepesvári (2006). A history of MCTS is described in Chang et al. (2016), and a video recounting the successful journey of AlphaGo can be found at AlphaGo-Movie (2017).

A classical RL textbook from the AI/ML community is Sutton and Barto (2018). For those interested in perspectives from outside the CS community, Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis (1996) is recommended, with Chang et al. (2007) focusing on simulation-based algorithms. An area that appears to be another good candidate for exploiting synergies between SO and DRL is meta-RL, for which a recent tutorial can be found in Beck et al. (2025), which also includes a substantial set of references on DRL.

A textbook introducing a wide range of topics in deep learning is Goodfellow et al. (2016).

下一章 · 仿真与优化

仿真与优化

第 02 章 · 章节速读

仿真优化的基本流程是：建立随机模型，重复模拟，比较方案，再用新样本验证最终选择。

建立模型
蒙特卡洛、离散事件和智能体仿真分别适合不同的系统。
搜索方案
离散问题比较候选方案；连续问题可用随机近似、梯度估计或代理模型。
处理连续决策
用马尔可夫决策过程描述状态、动作与长期收益。
判断差异是否可信
用置信区间量化不确定性，用共同随机数等方法降低比较噪声。
连接工业应用
智能体可以协调数据、仿真与优化工具，支持生产规划。

不要只看一次仿真的最小成本；还要看不确定性和独立验证结果。

本章动手实验

生产单元的产能规划离散事件仿真、置信区间与共同随机数。
展开完整原文（英文）

Entering the 21st century, the field of simulation optimization experienced significant progress through the integration of stochastic simulation techniques and advanced optimization algorithms. Researchers demonstrated that this combination could effectively address complex challenges posed by systems characterized by high levels of uncertainty. Stochastic simulation emerged as a powerful tool to manage randomness and variability, expanding the scope and applicability of stochastic optimization methods across various disciplines, and new optimization methodologies were developed that were tailored to the stochastic simulation setting.

Advances in computational capabilities and algorithmic developments have considerably transformed the landscape of SO. Enhanced techniques for estimating gradients in stochastic contexts allow optimization algorithms to operate more efficiently by generating precise gradient information from fewer simulation iterations. Such innovations have proven especially valuable in addressing high-dimensional optimization scenarios, where conventional gradient estimation techniques tend to be computationally burdensome. Furthermore, researchers introduced improved simulation sampling allocation strategies, designed to strategically distribute computational resources across different alternatives, thereby reducing computational overhead while preserving solution accuracy. These strategies have been pivotal in managing computational demands, facilitating more effective exploration of possible solutions. Moreover, variance reduction methods have become essential components in SO, significantly improving the accuracy and reliability of results by systematically reducing variability inherent in stochastic simulations. Such advances have contributed greatly to the robustness of optimization outcomes.

This chapter reviews the fundamental methodologies essential for stochastic simulation and optimization. Section 2.1 reviews the three main approaches for stochastic simulation models: Monte Carlo (MC) simulation, discrete event simulation (DES), and agent-based simulation (ABS). Discrete and continuous stochastic optimization are reviewed in Sections 2.2 and 2.3, respectively, where the treatment focuses on non-search methods for the former (statistical ranking & selection, and multi-armed bandit models) and gradient-based search methods for the latter, and the basics of Markov decision processes are summarized in Section 2.4, in preparation for reinforcement learning in Chapter 3. Stochastic gradient estimation, the linchpin of gradient-based search for continuous optimization algorithms, is presented in Section 2.5. Finally, the most effective variance reduction techniques are briefly summarized in Section 2.6, emphasizing their role in improving the reliability and precision of simulation outcomes. This overview sets the stage for the algorithms and applications integrating simulation and optimization with AI presented in Section 4.

2.1 Stochastic Simulation

Stochastic simulation enables the analysis of complex systems characterized by inherent randomness and uncertainty. We review three principal methodologies: MC simulation, DES, and ABS. MC simulation leverages repeated random sampling to approximate solutions to intricate deterministic problems. DES models systems through a sequence of discrete, time-ordered events, providing detailed insights into operational dynamics and performance metrics like flow time and resource utilization, making it essential for optimizing manufacturing, logistics, and service systems. ABS extends the DES paradigm by incorporating autonomous agents with individual behaviors and decision-making capabilities, facilitating the simulation of complex adaptive systems and the emergence of macro-level phenomena from micro-level interactions. The integration of object-oriented programming and AI has further enhanced the sophistication and applicability of ABS, allowing for more realistic and adaptive models. These stochastic simulation techniques offer robust and versatile tools for researchers and practitioners to model, analyze, and optimize systems across diverse domains, with ongoing advances ensuring their continued relevance and efficacy in addressing multifaceted challenges in uncertain environments.

The Monte Carlo Method

The MC method employs statistical sampling for estimating quantities such as integrals, which in our setting usually represent expected values. To introduce the main idea of the MC method, consider calculating \[\int_0^1 g(x) dx,\] where direct integration of the function \(g\) is numerically challenging.

Then the MC estimator based on a sample of size \(n\) is given by

\[\begin{equation} \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} g(X_i), \end{equation}\]

where \(X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n,\) are simply random numbers, i.e., random variables uniformly distributed on the interval \([0,1]\), written as \(U(0,1)\). For a given \(n\), the MC estimator is unbiased, and if \(\{ X_i \}\) are uncorrelated, then the law of large numbers (LLN) ensures that it is also consistent, i.e., converges to the target value as the sample size \(n\) goes to infinity.

Application of the MC method for stochastic simulation views integrals as expected value output performance measures from a simulation model, viz.,

\[\begin{equation} \mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \int g(x) f(x) dx, \end{equation}\]

where \(g\) is the output performance measure, and \(X \sim f\) is the simulation input, with \(f\) the input probability density function (p.d.f.). For the simple example, the input p.d.f. is the \(U(0,1)\) density given by

\[f(x) = {\bf 1}\{0 \leq x \leq 1\}.\]

The MC estimator (2.1) can be used to estimate expectations of the form given in Equation (2.2) for multi-dimensional integrals with general \(f\), in which case \(f\) would represent a joint p.d.f. and each \(X_i\) would be a vector of input random variables generated according to \(f\). The computational advantages of the MC method over standard (deterministic) numerical integration methods become apparent for high-dimensional problems, because its computational requirements grow linearly with the dimension, in contrast to the exponential increase in computation required by methods such as Simpson’s rule or Gaussian quadrature. Furthermore, its statistical nature allows straightforward measures of precision for the estimators not always available for deterministic procedures, and the central limit theorem (CLT) provides a mathematically rigorous basis for constructing (asymptotically valid) confidence intervals.

An example to be utilized in more detail later for discrete-event simulation, stochastic gradient estimation (Section 2.5), and variance reduction techniques (Section 2.6) is a single-server queue, where the input random variables would drive both the customer arrival process (interarrival times) and the customer service times, and output performance measures of interest include queueing times (e.g., average time spent by customers in the system) and queue lengths.

Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

Implementing the MC method assumes the availability of generating from the input distribution \(f\), which would just be a (pseudo-)random number generator for the simple example where the input distribution is \(U(0,1)\). Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) considers the situation where generating from such a distribution is itself challenging, such as when it is of high dimensions. Here, we illustrate the classical Metropolis-Hastings algorithm in the setting where the target distribution is discrete and finite, and hence denote the target distribution by \(\pi:= \{ \pi_i \}\), following the usual notational convention. The main idea behind MCMC is to construct an appropriate Markov chain whose stationary distribution is \(\pi\). Assuming stationarity of the underlying transition dynamics, i.e., a time-homogeneous Markov chain, denote the one-step transition probabilities of the Markov chain by \(\{ p_{ij} \}\).

The Metropolis-Hastings algorithm constructs a Markov chain with the desired stationary distribution \(\pi\) by specifying \(\{ p_{ij} \}\) using an acceptance-rejection procedure based on a proposal set of one-step transition probabilities \(\{ q_{ij} \}\). Specifically, given the current state \(X_n = i\), a new state \(j\) is proposed according to \(q_{ij}\), and the proposed state transition is accepted with probability (w.p.) \[\alpha_{ij} = \min\left( \frac{\pi_j q_{ji}}{\pi_i q_{ij}}, 1 \right).\] If the proposal is rejected, the chain remains in the current state \(i\). If we let \(\{ p_{ij} \}\) denote the resulting one-step transition probabilities for the Markov chain generated using this “acceptance-rejection” procedure for state transitions, then it can be shown that \(\{ p_{ij} \}\) satisfy the detailed balance conditions \[\pi_i p_{ij} = \pi_j p_{ji}, \quad \forall i, j,\] thereby guaranteeing that \(\pi\) is the stationary distribution of the chain.

It is critical to note that MCMC generates correlated samples, so that more care is needed in applying classical statistical theorems such as the LLN and CLT. Also, for many applications, it is easier to generate from high-dimensional distributions by using conditional distributions on lower-dimensional spaces rather than trying to use the full joint distribution. Gibbs sampling is perhaps the most prominent example of this approach. Another useful generalization for stochastic processes is sequential Monte Carlo, also known as particle filtering.

Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) or Particle Filtering

Sequential Monte Carlo extends the principles of MCMC to dynamical systems where the target distribution evolves over time, making them particularly suitable for state-space models. The objective of SMC is to estimate a sequence of posterior distributions \(\{ \pi^{(k)} \}\) that progressively approximate the target distribution. This is achieved by representing each \(\pi^{(k)}\) with a set of particles, each assigned an importance weight that reflects its contribution to the target distribution. Consider a hidden Markov model characterized by hidden states \(X = (X_1, \ldots, X_n)\) and observations \(Y = (Y_1, \ldots, Y_n)\). The posterior distribution of interest is \(p(X_n | Y_n)\), which can be decomposed recursively as \[p(X_n | Y_n) = \frac{p(X_{n-1}, Y_{n-1}) f(X_n | X_{n-1}) g(Y_n | X_n)}{p(Y_n)},\] where \(f(X_n | X_{n-1})\) denotes the state transition density, and \(g(Y_n | X_n)\) represents the likelihood of observing \(Y_n\) given the state \(X_n\). Direct sampling from \(p(X_n | Y_n)\) is typically infeasible due to its complexity, so SMC methods approach this challenge by sequentially sampling from a series of intermediate distributions that bridge the gap between the prior and the posterior.

In practice, SMC employs importance sampling, where particles \(X_n^{(i)}\) are drawn from a proposal distribution \(q(X_n | Y_n)\). Each particle is assigned an importance weight \(w_n^{(i)}\), calculated as

\[w_n^{(i)} = \frac{p(Y_n | X_n^{(i)}) p(X_n^{(i)})}{q(X_n^{(i)} | Y_n)}.\] The posterior distribution is then approximated by a weighted sum of delta functions \(\delta\) centered at the particles: \[\hat{p}_N(dX_n | Y_n) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N W_n^{(i)} \delta_{X_n^{(i)}}(dX_n),\] where \(W_n^{(i)}\) are the normalized weights. However, a common issue in SMC is weight degeneracy, where a few particles may dominate the weight distribution, leading to a loss of diversity among particles. To address this, resampling steps are incorporated to generate a new set of equally weighted particles, thereby maintaining the diversity and representativeness of the particle population.

Importance sampling is also a general variance reduction technique and will be revisited again in Section 2.6

Discrete-Event Simulation

Before defining discrete-event systems or models, we briefly summarize discrete-time systems, where the state of the system changes at fixed time points, usually at evenly spaced deterministic intervals specified in advance by the system modeler. For example, the simulation of a system driven by ordinary differential equations is generally carried out in this manner. In contrast, in a discrete-event system, the state of the system doesn’t change until an event in the system occurs. Typical examples include queueing and inventory systems, where events include arrivals, service completions, departures, demand arrivals, and order placements and arrivals. In a queueing system, the state of the system would increase with arrivals and decrease with departures.

Discrete-event simulation refers to the simulation of a discrete-event system model, which is characterized by the next-event mechanism for advancing the simulation clock (time) and used in most DES software. The simulation keeps a running event list schedule, which drives the dynamics of the system state. In each step of the simulation, the next event on the list advances the simulation clock time and updates the state variables and the event list schedule accordingly.

To illustrate, consider perhaps the simplest discrete-event system: a single-server queue, where customers arrive (one at a time), are served after possibly queueing if the system is already busy, and then leave upon service completion, as depicted in Figure 2.1.

In this case, there would be two main types of events: an arrival and a departure (or service completion), where the latter only possible when the system is busy.

The event list schedule at any time would comprise at most three possible events: arrival, service completion (departure), and end of simulation (which could be a fixed time or a time governed by an arrival or service event, e.g., the completion of a certain number of services). If the system state is the number of customers in the system, then the state increases by one with an arrival event denoted by \(E_i\) and decreases by one with a service completion or departure event denoted by \(D_i\). Figure 2.2 illustrates a possible sample path or realization for the queue, where the state is the number of system, and the system starts empty.

For queueing systems, typical outputs of interest include utilization (fraction of time the server or system is busy), delay in queue, total system time (also known as cycle time and flow time), number in queue, and number in system. Performance metrics include mean, median, variance, probabilities, quantiles, and other statistics derived from the output processes. These can be computed over a discrete set of the customers (e.g., average delay or mean delay) or over continuous time (e.g., average number in system or utilization).

Agent-Based Simulation

An agent is an autonomous “entity” capable of sensing its environment, including other agents, and using this information to make decisions. Agents possess attributes and a set of basic if/then rules that govern their behaviors. They may also learn (improve their understanding of other agents and the environment) and adapt their behaviors (modify their decision rules) over time, requiring some form of memory. Examples of agents include people, animals, vehicles, and organizations. The key characteristic in an ABS is the interactions of the entities (agents) significantly with other entities and with their environment.

Thus, ABS models focus on the behavior and interaction of individual agents, whereas DES models take a system-level view. Complex ABS models are typically implemented using object-oriented software (e.g., Java), where instance variables correspond to attributes and methods correspond to behaviors. While in principle ABS models can be implemented using commercial DES software packages, it may not be the most natural and user-friendly platform. Simulation packages or toolkits specifically designed for performing ABS generally require object-oriented programming, usually in Java, which can be used to model the diversity and complexity of agent behaviors. ABS platforms such as AnyLogic, MASON, NetLogo, and Repast Simphony offer robust tools for modeling complex systems. ABS has been applied to various fields, including defense, economics, epidemiology, games, homeland security, logistics, manufacturing, movies, transportation, social sciences, and supply chains.

Extended Example from Logistics Management

We illustrate DES and ABS using a warehouse management example.

Warehouse operations are analyzed by simulating dynamics on an event-by-event basis and generating comprehensive performance reports. In stochastic warehouse management systems, goods are systematically organized on storage racks while orders arrive randomly, embodying the inherent uncertainties of logistics operations. To address the escalating demand for efficient logistics, automated guided vehicles (AGVs) have been extensively deployed in intelligent warehouses as the primary mode of transportation. However, optimizing the efficiency of AGVs presents significant challenges due to high order volumes and limited spatial constraints that restrict AGV movement. The optimization of AGV operations encompasses two primary issues: scheduling, which involves the allocation of orders to AGVs within the bounds of available resources, and routing, which entails determining the most effective travel paths for AGVs to fulfill transportation requests. Traditionally, these two problems were investigated independently; however, recent studies have increasingly adopted integrated approaches that simultaneously address scheduling and routing to enhance overall system performance. The structural configuration and input parameters of these simulation models play a crucial role in the design and effectiveness of scheduling and routing algorithms for AGVs, thereby influencing the overall efficiency and responsiveness of the warehouse operations.

This DES framework is intrinsically related to ABS when considering the modeling of AGVs as autonomous agents within the simulation environment. In an ABS approach, each AGV is represented as an independent agent endowed with its own decision-making capabilities, behaviors, and interactions with other agents and the environment. This agent-centric perspective allows for more dynamic and adaptive optimization of both scheduling and routing processes. For instance, AGV agents can autonomously negotiate paths to avoid collisions, adapt to real-time changes in order volumes, and collaboratively optimize their tasks based on local information and predefined rules. By modeling AGVs as agents, the simulation can capture emergent behaviors and complex interactions that arise from the autonomous actions of individual AGVs, leading to more realistic and efficient warehouse operation simulations. Furthermore, ABS facilitates the incorporation of learning algorithms, enabling AGV agents to improve their performance over time through experience. This enhances the system’s ability to handle variability and unforeseen disruptions, thereby increasing the overall resilience and efficiency of warehouse operations. Consequently, integrating ABS with DES methodologies provides a more comprehensive and flexible framework for modeling and optimizing AGV operations, significantly enhancing the simulation’s capacity to reflect the nuanced and adaptive nature of real-world logistics environments.

In practice, orders arrive randomly and independently at the warehouse management system, each specifying a particular picking rack and a delivery location for AGVs. The arrival process of these orders must be modeled to capture the stochastic nature of order inflow. The AGV traffic network is frequently represented as a grid comprising traversable square units, each maintaining consistent size and shape. Figure 2.3 illustrates the grid-based representation of an AGV traffic network – the spatial organization and movement pathways of AGVs within the simulated warehouse environment. This grid-based framework facilitates the graphical visualization and simulation of warehouse layouts and AGV movements, ensuring systematic and efficient operations. Within this grid, each square unit accommodates only one AGV at a time, thereby preventing congestion and collisions. Specific squares are designated as berths for AGV parking prior to order assignment, picking racks where AGVs collect goods, and delivery locations where goods are dispatched to fulfill orders. Additionally, certain squares are marked as obstructed or reserved to denote areas inaccessible to AGVs due to obstacles or immovable structures, thereby enhancing the realism and accuracy of the simulation. AGVs navigate the grid by executing horizontal and vertical movements between adjacent square units, adhering to the constraints of single occupancy per square.

The underlying simulation model incorporates inherent randomness through the stochastic arrival of orders and employs distinct modules for scheduling and routing. The scheduling module is responsible for assigning incoming orders to available AGVs, while the routing module determines the optimal travel paths for AGV movement within the grid network. The AGVs are assumed to be homogeneous in function, with each AGV possessing sufficient battery capacity to handle all assigned orders without necessitating recharging or battery replacement during operation, as AGVs can be recharged or swapped at designated berths. This assumption simplifies the model by eliminating complexities associated with battery management and potential AGV breakdowns during transit. Each AGV operates at a constant speed, with fixed loading and unloading durations. Consequently, an AGV can exist in one of four states at any given time: (1) picking state, moving to a picking rack; (2) delivering state, moving to a delivery location; (3) parking state, awaiting new assignments post-unloading; and (4) idle state, stationed at the berth with or without new orders. The key elements of the AGV movement are illustrated in Figure 2.5.

In the simulation model, the AGV scheduling module is triggered upon receiving an order or when an AGV transitions to a parking state. At each scheduling invocation, the set of available orders and the set of active AGVs are identified. The scheduling optimization problem is deterministic, with decision variables indicating the assignment of order to AGV. Following scheduling, the AGV routing module is activated after loading and unloading processes. This module comprises partial route request submodules that segment the full path into fixed-length partial routes, ensuring collision-free navigation. AGVs move only if the requested partial routes are unoccupied, with priority given based on request time. The overall system objective is to design efficient scheduling and routing policies to minimize an adjusted average order cycle time.

A natural extension of this framework is to use a world model to support AGV scheduling and routing decisions directly. In this setting, the world model serves as a structured digital representation of the warehouse environment, encoding not only the geometric layout of the traffic network, but also the operational semantics of the system, such as workstation locations, material buffers, traffic directions, equipment states, and congestion patterns. This representation enables the scheduling module to reason over a simulation-ready environment rather than over a purely abstract graph.

More specifically, the world model provides a unified state description for AGV dispatching. It can aggregate real-time information on order arrivals, AGV locations, queue lengths, machine status, and path occupancy, and map them into a form suitable for downstream optimization or learning algorithms. Based on this state, the system can evaluate alternative dispatching and routing decisions before execution, for example by estimating travel times, blocking risk, idle time, and the impact of local congestion on downstream tasks. In this sense, the world model acts as an interface between the physical warehouse and the decision layer, allowing AGV scheduling to be performed in a closed loop with prediction, simulation, and feedback.

From the perspective of simulation and optimization, this capability is particularly valuable because AGV control is inherently dynamic and spatially coupled. A world-model-based approach makes it possible to combine discrete-event simulation for order flows, agent-based modeling for AGV interactions, and optimization or reinforcement learning for dispatching and routing. As a result, the system can move beyond static shortest-path planning and instead support anticipative scheduling policies that account for future congestion, resource contention, and changing operational priorities.

Advances in MC Methods and Simulation

Recent advances in MC simulation have focused on enhancing its scalability and applicability to increasingly complex systems through the integration of high-performance computing and parallel processing techniques. MCMC and SMC methods are widely used techniques in computational statistics particularly adept at approximating complex probability distributions that are otherwise intractable to sample directly. MCMC methods leverage the construction of a Markov chain whose equilibrium distribution corresponds to the target distribution, facilitating sampling in high-dimensional spaces commonly encountered in Bayesian inference and various other statistical applications.

Additionally, adaptive MC methods that dynamically adjust sampling strategies based on the evolving simulation state have been developed to further improve efficiency and accuracy. ML has also fostered the creation of surrogate models that approximate complex simulation outputs with significantly reduced computational costs, thereby facilitating more efficient optimization and analysis of large-scale systems.

2.2 Discrete Stochastic Optimization

Discrete optimization via stochastic simulation focuses on finding the best settings for variables that take on values from a countable set, either ordered or categorical. Ordered variables can assume integer or discrete values, where the order of these values has a physical meaning. Categorical variables encompass a broader range of discrete decisions, from binary (e.g., on-off or 0-1) variables to more abstract choices, such as selecting a sequence of actions from a finite set. While ordered variables can be logically represented using binary variables, retaining their order generally makes sense when the order has physical meaning, as it can lead to more efficient search procedures when there are a large number of alternatives.

In this section, we focus on finding the best in the finite setting where the number of alternatives is small and fixed; hence, there is no “search” process in the optimization, and the optimum is found by simply ordering the given alternatives. The challenge is that the objective function is noisy, i.e., the performance of each alternative can only be estimated by sampling; thus, the “decision variables” in the resulting “optimization” problem relate to determining how to allocate a sampling budget among the alternatives. In the simulation context, this translates to deciding which alternatives to simulate next, i.e., how to allocate simulation replications. This section only considers finding the best among the alternatives. Other objectives not considered here include selecting a subset that is likely to contain the best, providing a complete ordering of the alternatives, and comparing each alternative to a known standard or control. Note that “best” will be minimum mean (think of costs) for ranking and selection but maximum mean (rewards) in the multi-armed bandit setting.

Statistical Ranking and Selection (R&S)

We consider the classical R&S problem of selecting the best alternative from a finite set of alternatives with unknown means \((\mu _1, \ldots ,{\mu _k})\), where best here will be the alternative with the smallest mean, to be denoted throughout by \(*\), i.e., \(\mu_* \leq \mu\). In the simulation context, each alternative typically represents a complex stochastic model (e.g., a queueing network), so the mean of the random output (e.g., average system time) cannot be determined analytically but can be estimated via simulation.

A R&S procedure generally consists of two phases: sampling and selection. In the sampling phase, the alternatives are sampled (simulation replications conducted to estimate output performance) until some stopping condition is satisfied. Once the sampling has concluded (all the simulation replications finished), then a single system is selected to be declared the best. The probability of correct selection (PCS) is the probability that the selected alternative corresponds to *. For example, if the sample means denoted by \(\bar{X}_i\) are used to select the best, then PCS is given by the joint probability \[{\rm{PCS}} = \mathbb{P}(\bar{X}_* \leq \bar{X}_i ~\forall i).\]

The unknown means of the alternatives are assumed to be estimated using independent random samples (simulation replications) following a joint distribution, \(({X_{1,\ell }}, \ldots ,{X_{k,\ell }}) \sim Q( \cdot ;\theta )\), where \(\ell \in \mathbb{Z}^+\) and \(\theta\) includes all unknown parameters of the sampling distribution, including \({\mu _1}, \ldots ,{\mu _k}\) and the standard deviations \({\sigma _1}, \ldots, {\sigma _k}\).

A common assumption in the R&S literature is that samples from different alternatives (i.e., \({X_{i,\ell }},{X_{j,\ell }}\) for \(i \ne j\)) follow independent normal distributions. In practice, the output of a stochastic model is often the average of many random variables (e.g., average system time of customers served), justifying the normality assumption by a CLT. If the samples are not normally distributed, batching can be used to achieve approximate normality. By the LLN, sample means converge to the true means almost surely (a.s.), i.e., \(\bar{X}_i(n) := \frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{\ell = 1}^n {{X_{i,\ell }}} \to {\mu _i}\) a.s. \(n \to \infty\), for \(i = 1, \ldots ,k\). Thus, with an infinite simulation budget, the best alternative could eventually be selected. However, simulation replications are typically costly, often resulting in a very limited simulation budget in practice.

Traditional R&S approaches provide a statistical guarantee on PCS or other similar measures, often following the indifference-zone formulation, which specifies an indifference zone parameters \(\Delta\) and stipulates that for any suboptimal alternative \(k \ne {*}\), \[\begin{equation*} {\mu _{{*}}} - {\mu _k} \ge \Delta. \end{equation*}\] Procedures derived under the indifference-zone formulation guarantee PCS under the worst-case scenario, often referred to as the slippage configuration, which typically leads to conservative allocations resulting in more simulation replications than needed for typical problems. In particular, these procedures lead to an allocation of sampling (simulation replications) that is basically proportional to the (estimated) variance of each alternative. In other words, the optimal solution to the following pseudo-optimization problem:

\[\begin{equation*} \min _{\{ {n_k, k = 1,\ldots, K }\}} L(n_1,\ldots,n_K) {\text{~subject to~}} {\rm{PCS}} \geq 1 - \alpha, \end{equation*}\]

where \(n_i\) denotes the number of replications allocated to alternative \(i\), \(L\) denotes some linear “loss” function on the allocations, e.g., the sum, and \(\alpha \in [0,1]\), e.g., 0.05 for a 95% “confidence” level, is given by \[n_i \propto \sigma^2_i.\]

Algorithmically, R&S procedures are implemented in stages that range from two stages to fully sequential. In a two-stage procedure, the first stage is used as a “pilot” run for estimating parameters used to compute the final allocation that is realized in the second stage. A generic version of such a procedure would generally take \(n_0\) samples for each of the \(K\) alternatives in the first stage to calculate (e.g., using statistics such as the sample means and variances) a theoretically optimal \(n_i\) for the total allocation for each alternative \(i\), and then sample \((n_i-n_0)^+\) samples in the second stage, where \(x^+ \equiv \max(x,0)\) denotes the nonnegative part of the argument. On the other hand, a fully sequential procedure would allocation one simulation replication at a time, which could be based on “global” or “local” information, where the latter usually leads to some type of “greedy” algorithm; however, even these methods often begin with an initialization phase, e.g., with \(n_0\) samples for each of the \(K\) alternatives.

Figure 2.6 presents an example with five alternatives, where shown are the corresponding stylized box plots for the means based on \(n_0\) initial replications for each alternative. Further sampling based on estimated uncertainty would allocate more replications to alternatives \(1\), \(4\), and \(5\), to ensure the level of PCS is achieved in the worst-case scenario described earlier.

For this example, however, it appears unlikely that alternatives \(1\), \(4\), and \(5\) are better than \(2\) and \(3\), so that allocating a significant number of additional simulation replications to them would be an inefficient use of computational resources.

Rather, intuition might lead one to conclude that for the objective of determining the best (minimum mean) alternative, simulation replications should be allocated to those alternatives with either a larger variance or smaller mean. Balancing allocations based on these two factors is what is known as the “mean-variance” or “exploitation-exploration” tradeoff. Formalizing this trade-off necessitates specifying a metric to measure sampling efficiency, e.g., PCS.

Thus, an alternative approach to guaranteeing a PCS level under all possible scenarios is to consider maximizing the PCS under a computing budget constraint, which is taken up next.

Optimal Computing Budget Allocation (OCBA)

Optimal Computing Budget Allocation (OCBA) formulates the following optimization problem, with an objective of maximizing PCS by allocating a fixed total number of simulation replications \(N\) across \(K\) alternatives:

\[\begin{equation*} \max _{\{ {n_k, k = 1,\ldots, K }\}} \quad {\rm{PCS}}\quad {\text{subject to}}\quad \sum\limits_{k = 1}^K {{n_k}} = N, \end{equation*}\] where \({n_k}\) is the number of replications allocated to alternative \(k\). Under the asymptotic limit on the total budget, i.e., as \(N \rightarrow \infty\), the following OCBA sampling ratios \({r_k}:=\lim_{N \rightarrow \infty} n_k/N\) are derived:

\[\begin{equation} \frac{r_i}{r_j} = \frac{\sigma^2_i/\delta^2_i}{\sigma^2_j/\delta^2_j},~\forall i, j \neq *,~~ ~\left( \frac{r_*}{\sigma_*} \right)^2 = \sum_{i \neq *} \left( \frac{r_i}{\sigma_i} \right)^2, ~ \delta_i := \mu_i - \mu_*, \end{equation}\]

an allocation that has the following intuitive properties:

  • proportional to variance, except for the best;

  • inversely proportion to the squared difference from the best, again except for the best;

  • more to the best unless its variance is very small.

The allocation prescribed by (2.3) can be used to propose sequential simulation sampling procedures that can be implemented in two or more phases or fully sequentially following an initial estimation phase to estimate the means and variances.

Returning to the example shown in Figure 2.6, the allocations would be higher for alternatives 2 and 3, matching intuition if the goal is to efficiently find the best.

An essential element of OCBA is the way it captures the exploration-exploitation trade-off, which is not reflected in traditional R&S procedures, but is also explicitly considered in procedures based on multi-armed bandit models to be described shortly. However, incorporating OCBA into an actual algorithmic procedure is heuristic in nature, due to the asymptotic nature of the main theoretical basis, as the allocations are based on unknown parameters, including the means themselves.

To quantify the uncertainty of the parameter, which we will denote now by \(\theta\), suppose \(\theta\) follows a prior distribution \(F( \cdot ;{\zeta _0})\) that reflects our prior knowledge of the unknown parameter, where \({\zeta _0}\) is called a hyper-parameter. Let \({X_i}(N) = ({X_{i,1}}, \ldots ,{X_{i,{n_i}}})\) and \({{\cal E}_N} = \{ {\zeta _0},{X_1}(N), \ldots ,{X_K}(N)\}\). The posterior and predictive distributions can be calculated using Bayes rule. If the prior distribution is a conjugate prior of the sampling distribution, the posterior distribution remains in the same parametric family as the prior, denoted as \(F(\cdot ;{\zeta_N})\), where \({\zeta_N}\) is the posterior hyper-parameter. Under a conjugate prior, the information set \({\cal E}_N\) is fully determined by the posterior hyper-parameters, i.e., \({{\cal E}_N} = {\zeta _N}\).

PCS can also be expressed more generally as follows:

\[\begin{equation*} {\rm{PCS}}(\theta ) =\mathbb{P}({\mu _S} < {\mu _i},{\mkern 1mu} \forall i \ne S\mid \theta ) = \mathbb{E}[{L_{0 - 1}}({{\cal E}_N})\mid \theta ], \end{equation*}\] where \(S\) is the selection decision after allocating a total of \(N\) simulation replications, and \({L_{0 - 1}}({{\cal E}_N}) = {\bf{1}}\{ S = *\}\) is the zero-one loss after \(N\) replications. As mentioned previously, for simulation sample allocation, the loss occurs only at the end, after allocating all \(N\) simulation replications.

For a normal distribution \(N({\mu _i},\sigma _i^2)\) with unknown mean and known variance, the conjugate prior is a normal distribution \(N({\mu _{i(0)}},\sigma _{i(0)}^2)\). The posterior distribution of \(\mu_i\) is the normal distribution \(N(\mu_{i(N)}, \sigma_{i(N)}^2)\), where \[\begin{equation*} {\mu _{i(N)}} = \sigma _{i(N)}^2\left( {\frac{{{\mu _{i(0)}}}}{{\sigma _{i(0)}^2}} + \frac{{{n_i}{{\bar X}_{i(N)}}}}{{\sigma _i^2}}} \right),\quad \sigma _{i(N)}^2 = {\left( {\frac{1}{{\sigma _{i(0)}^2}} + \frac{{{n_i}}}{{\sigma _i^2}}} \right)^{ - 1}}, \end{equation*}\]

and the predictive distribution of \({X_{i,{n_i} + 1}}\) is \(N({\mu _{i(N)}},\sigma _i^2 + \sigma _{i(N)}^2)\). If \({\sigma _{i(0)}} \to \infty\), \({\mu _{i(N)}} \approx {\bar X_{i(N)}}\), corresponding to an uninformative prior.

The dynamic decisions in the simulation sample allocation problem can be formulated as a dynamic allocation and selection (A\(\&\)S) policy. The allocation policy is a sequence of mappings \({{\cal A}}( \cdot ) = ({{\cal A}_1}( \cdot ), \ldots ,{{\cal A}_t}( \cdot ))\) that sequentially allocates each sample to an alternative based on collected information, and the selection policy \(S( \cdot )\) makes a final decision to select the best alternative after all simulation replications are exhausted. The optimal A\(\&\)S policy \(({\cal A}^*,{S^*})\) satisfies a Bellman equation. The commonly assumed selection policy chooses the alternative with the largest posterior mean, although it may not be the optimal selection policy.

A dynamic sampling procedure derived from an approximate dynamic programming paradigm aims to find a suitable value function approximation (VFA) for the Bellman equation. A simple approach fixes the selection policy to choose the alternative with the largest posterior mean and one-step-look-ahead for allocating the next replication. Consequently, the VFA becomes an integral of the standard normal density over a region defined by \(k-1\) hyperplanes. Denoting the index of the posterior-best alternative at time \(t\) by \({\langle 1\rangle _t} = \arg {\max _{i}\mu _{i(t)}}\), the VFA can be further simplified to an analytical form, leading to the following asymptotically optimal allocation policy (AOAP), whose form reflects a similar (posterior) mean-variance tradeoff as the OCBA formula:

\[\begin{equation*} {{\cal A}_{t + 1}}({{\cal E}_t}) = \arg {\max _{i = 1, \ldots ,K}}{\hat V_t}({{\cal E}_t};i), \end{equation*}\] where for all \(j \ne {\langle 1\rangle _t}\), \[\begin{equation*} {\hat V_t}\left( {{{\cal E}_t};j} \right) \buildrel\textstyle.\over= \min \left\{ {\frac{{{{\left( {\mu _{{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t)}} - \mu _{j(t)}} \right)}^2}}}{{ {\sigma _{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t)}^2} + {{{\sigma _{j(t + 1)}^2} }}}},{{\min }_{\ell \ne {{\langle 1\rangle }_t},j}}\frac{{{{\left( {\mu _{{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t)}} - \mu _{\ell(t)} } \right)}^2}}}{{{{ {\sigma _{{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t)}}^2} }} + {{ {\sigma _{\ell(t)} }^2 }}}}} \right\}, \end{equation*}\] and \[\begin{equation*} {\hat V_t}\left( {{{\cal E}_t};{{\langle 1\rangle }_t}} \right) \buildrel\textstyle.\over= {\min _{j \ne {{\langle 1\rangle }_t}}}\frac{{{{\left( {\mu _{{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t)}} - \mu _{j{(t)}}} \right)}^2}}}{ \sigma _{{\langle 1\rangle }_t(t + 1)}^2 + \sigma _{j(t)}^2 }. \end{equation*}\]

Consistency, in the context of AOAP, refers to the algorithm’s ability to correctly identify the best alternative as the number of allocated samples tends to infinity, expressed as \[\begin{equation*} {\lim _{t \to \infty }}\hat S({{\cal E}_t}) = *. \end{equation*}\] This property ensures that, given sufficient samples, AOAP reliably converges to the optimal solution, thereby mitigating the risk of misidentification inherent in finite-sample scenarios. Moreover, the sampling ratio for each alternative, \(r_k^{(t)}:= n_k/t\), asymptotically achieves the optimal large deviations decay rates for the probability of false selection:

\[\begin{equation*} \mathop {\lim }\limits_{t \to \infty } r_i^{(t)} = r_i^{\rm LD} ~\mbox{a.s.} ~\forall i, \end{equation*}\]

\[\begin{equation*} \frac{{{{\left( {{\mu_i} - {\mu_*}} \right)}^2}}}{{{{\left( {{\sigma_i}} \right)}^2}/r_i^{\rm LD} + {{\left( {{\sigma_*}} \right)}^2}/r_*^{\rm LD}}} = \frac{{{{\left( {{\mu _{\langle j\rangle }} - {\mu _*}} \right)}^2}}}{{{{\left( {{\sigma _{\langle j\rangle }}} \right)}^2}/r_{\langle j\rangle }^{\rm LD} + {{\left( {{\sigma _*}} \right)}^2}/r_*^{\rm LD}}}, ~i,j \ne *, \end{equation*}\] \[\begin{equation*} \left( \frac{r_*^{\rm LD}}{\sigma _i} \right)^2 = \sum\limits_{i \ne *} {\left( \frac{r_i^{\rm LD}}{{\sigma _i}} \right)}^2. \end{equation*}\]

Bandit Models for Ranking and Selection

The multi-armed bandit (MAB) problem has a similar overarching goal as statistical R&S, in that both aim to identify the best option (referred to as “arms” as in the old-fashioned mechanical slot machines) from a set of alternatives with unknown expected means. However, the objective of the standard MAB problem is to maximize cumulative expected rewards, whereas the objective of R&S is to maximize PCS or ensure that it exceeds a desired level. The latter can be viewed as a “special case” of the former by taking just a single binary reward at termination with no other rewards along the way, and this is known as the best-arm identification (BAI) problem in the MAB community.

Although the MAB problem is described in terms of rewards, with the goal is to choose a sequence of arms \(A_1, A_2, \ldots, A_N\) to maximize \[\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^N \mu_{A_t} \right],\] the standard solution approach is to analyze the expected cumulative regret after \(N\) “pulls” of the arms, as defined by

\[\begin{equation*} R(T) =\mathbb{E} \left[\mathop \sum \limits_{t = 1}^N ({\mu_*} - {\mu _{{A_t}}})\right]. \end{equation*}\]

As alluded to earlier, an optimal policy balances exploitation (sampling arms with currently high estimated means) and exploration (sampling arms with high uncertainty). A pure exploration policy would simply select the arm with the current highest estimated mean, whether using the sample mean in the frequentist setting or the mean of the posterior distribution in the Bayesian setting.

A widely used algorithm, due to its computational simplicity and hence ease of implementation, is the upper confidence bound (UCB) algorithm, which simply pulls (chooses) the arm with the highest UCB, i.e., \[A_t = \arg {\max _k} ~ {\rm UCB}_k^{(t)},\] where \({\rm UCB}_k^{(t)}\) is a running index for each arm \(k\) after \(t\) total pulls, defined as the sample mean \(\bar{X}_k(n_k^{(t)})\), where \(n_k^{(t)}\) is the number of pulls of arm \(k\) after \(t\) total pulls, plus some “fudge” factor that accounts for uncertainty: generally some multiple of either the standard deviation or the range of the arm’s (unknown) reward distribution, where the multiplicative factor reflects the weighting to balance exploitation and exploration, with a weight of zero reflecting pure exploitation. In the AI/ML community, it is commonly assumed that the range is known and often scaled to the interval [0,1], whereas in the statistics and SO community, the variance is usually assumed to be unknown and hence estimated by the sample variance. MCTS was introduced using UCB with known range, because its main motivating application was games, where if the reward was the probability of winning, then its support would be [0,1], and the UCB index for each arm could take the form \[{\rm UCB}_k^{(t)} := \bar{X}_k(n_k^{(t)}) + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln t}{n_k^{(t)}}}.\]

Although UCB is straightforward and easy to implement, better performance can often be obtained using Thompson sampling (TS), which is a Bayesian algorithm that maintains a posterior distribution over each arm’s mean reward and selects arms based on the probability that they are optimal. Specifically, TS samples \(\theta_k\) from the posterior distribution for \(\mu _k\) for each arm \(k\), where \(\theta_k\) refers to a component of the global parameter \(\theta\) defined earlier, and then the arm with the highest sampled \(\theta_k\) is chosen (pulled) for the next reward:

\[\begin{equation*} {A_t} = \arg {\max _k} {\, \theta _k}. \end{equation*}\]

While TS has demonstrated strong performance in minimizing cumulative regret in traditional MAB settings, it often underperforms in BAI or R&S tasks due to insufficient exploration of near-optimal arms. The Top-Two Thompson Sampling (TTTS) procedure enhances exploration by sampling **two candidate arms** from the posterior distribution and probabilistically selecting between them. Specifically, TTTS first draws two independent samples of the parameter vector and identifies the corresponding best arms under each. It then selects the first sampled arm with probability \(p\), and the second (distinct) one with probability \(1-p\). This approach increases the likelihood of allocating samples not only to the posterior-best arm, but also to arms with non-negligible posterior optimality, thereby improving PCS in simulation allocation tasks.

Unlike classical TS, which samples each arm in proportion to its posterior probability of being optimal, TTTS explicitly introduces randomized competition between the top candidates—whose identities are themselves random under the Bayesian model.

2.3 Continuous Stochastic Optimization

In this section, we consider the stochastic optimization problem:

\[\begin{equation} {\min _{\theta \in \Theta }}f(\theta), \end{equation}\] where \(f(\theta) =\mathbb{E}[Y(\theta;\omega )]\) for \(Y(\theta;\omega )\) a sample performance, \(\omega\) denotes the stochastic effects, and \(\Theta \subseteq \mathbb R{^d}\) is a continuous parameter space. The objective is to find an optimal solution

\[\begin{equation*} {\theta^*} \in \arg {\min _{\theta \in \Theta }}f(\theta). \end{equation*}\]

We focus on two iterative approaches: stochastic approximation, which uses gradient-based search (stochastic gradient descent in AI/ML), and response surface methods. Section 2.5 provides a review of the main stochastic gradient estimation techniques.

Stochastic Approximation (SA)

Stochastic Approximation is an iterative algorithm for solving (2.4) that parallels steepest descent in deterministic optimization:

\[\begin{equation} {\theta_{n + 1}} = {\Pi _\Theta }\left( {{\theta_n} - {a_n} \widehat{ \nabla f}({\theta_n})} \right), \end{equation}\]

where \({\Pi _\Theta }(\theta)\) projects \(\theta\) back into the feasible region \(\Theta\) if \(\theta \notin \Theta\), \({a_n}{\rm{ }} > {\rm{ }}0\) is the step size multiplier, \(\widehat{ \nabla f}({\theta_n})\) is an estimate of the gradient \(\nabla f({\theta_n})\). The projection operator is only required in the constrained setting, and a maximization problem would reverse the sign in (2.5). The Robbins-Monro (RM) algorithm uses an unbiased estimator for \(\nabla f(\theta)\), whereas the Kiefer-Wolfowitz (KW) algorithm employs finite-difference estimates. Under certain conditions, RM and KW have asymptotic convergence rates of \(O({n^{ - 1/2}})\) and \(O({n^{ - 1/3}})\), respectively.

The SA algorithm’s finite-time performance is discussed in the next chapter in AI/ML settings, but the choices of step-size sequence \(\{a_n\}\), gradient estimator \(\widehat{ \nabla f}({\theta_n})\), and projection operator \({\Pi _\Theta }\) significantly impact the algorithm’s performance, as does the perturbation size sequence for the finite-difference estimates in the KW algorithm. (Stochastic) gradient estimation techniques will be described in Section 2.5. We briefly discuss the other factors that influence SA performance here.

It is well known that the sequence \(\{a_n\}\) often requires tuning in practical implementation, because if \({a_n}\) is too large, the algorithm may oscillate without approaching the optimum \({\theta^*}\), whereas if \({a_n}\) is too small, convergence is slow, and the iterates may barely move.

Adaptive step-size rules can be used to mitigate sensitivity to the step-size sequence. For example, in the scalar case \(d\)=1, Kesten’s rule decreases the step size only when there is a directional change in the iterates, i.e., \(\left( {{\theta_{n + 1}} - {\rm{ }}{\theta_n}} \right)\left( {{\theta_n}{\rm{ }} - {\rm{ }}{\theta_{n - 1}}} \right){\rm{ }} < {\rm{ }}0\). In the multi-dimensional case \(d>1\), directional change can be assessed via the inner product \(\langle \theta_{n+1} - \theta_n,\, \theta_n - \theta_{n-1} \rangle < 0\). This adaptive step size maintains momentum when iterates move in the same direction, indicating they may be far from the optimum. An extension of this idea is to increase the step size to speed up convergence when consecutive errors in estimates have the same sign. Another alternative is to mimic the deterministic Newton-Raphson method by estimating the inverse Hessian.

Iterate averaging is a commonly employed technique for enhancing the robustness in estimating the optimal solution. This approach combines larger step-sizes with averaging a subset of the most recent iterates that is returned as estimated optimum, thus decreasing reliance on the last iterate. Easy to implement, its robustness includes reduced sensitivity to the initial step size choice. Under appropriate conditions, iterative averaging exhibits \(O({n^{ - 1/2}})\) asymptotic convergence rate.

A generalization of iterate averaging is the robust stochastic approximation (RSA) algorithm, which uses the step-size sequence \(a_n\) for weighting the iterates. Another generalization introduces a function into the objective function, acting as a regularization term to prevent the next iterate \(\theta_{n+1}\) from being too far from \(\theta_{n}\).

Early asymptotic theory for SA assumed the the objective function satisfy specific global conditions such as monotonicity in the original root-finding problem and convexity for optimization. Relaxations include only requiring such conditions to hold within a compact set containing the optimum, allowing the projection operator to be used adaptively in the SA algorithm, even for an unconstrained optimization problem. In practice, the projection set \(\Theta\) must be chosen to include the (unknown) optimal solution \(\theta^*\); however, making \(\Theta\) much larger than necessary can slow convergence by enlarging the search space. Adaptively increasing the search space can lead to provable convergence with an appropriate projection operator, such as adaptively projecting the iterates onto an increasing compact set.

Finite difference gradient estimates used in KW-type SA algorithms become computationally expensive in high dimensions, since the computational cost grows linearly with the parameter dimension. Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (SPSA) requires only two estimates of the objective function to approximate the gradient, making the computational cost independent of the parameter space dimension.

Response Surface Methods

Response surface methods are typically used in continuous optimization problems to learn input–output relationships and approximate the underlying simulation with a surface, known as a meta model or surrogate model. This functional form allows the use of powerful derivative-based optimization techniques. Response surface methods are referred to in the literature as multi-disciplinary design optimization, metamodel-based optimization, and sequential parameter optimization, and were initially developed for experimental design in physical processes.

Different response surface algorithms vary in several ways: the choice between regression and interpolation; the nature of the functional form used for approximation (polynomials, splines, kriging, radial basis functions, neural networks); the method for determining the number and location of new samples; the approach to updating the response surface. RSM approaches can build effective surrogate models in local regions and use these models sequentially to guide the search; construct surrogate models for the entire parameter space using space-filling designs, and then sample in areas where finding better solutions is likely.

To address SO problems, a two-phase sequential response surface methodology (RSM) was proposed:

  • Phase I (iterative): Build a first-order model using samples from a design of experiments. Based on a gradient estimate derived from the first-order fitted surface, move in an improving direction. Repeat until the estimated gradient was close to zero.

  • Phase II: Construct a more detailed quadratic model to derive a final estimated optimum.

Trust region methods can be used to implement sequential RSM by controlling the region of approximation and providing update criteria for surrogate models, aiding in analyzing convergence properties. Once a metamodel or response surface \(f\) is built around a trust region center \({\theta_i}\), trust region algorithms involve solving the trust-region subproblem \(\min f({\theta_i} + s)\) subject to \(s \in B({\theta_i},\Delta )\), where \(B\) is a ball defined by the center-radius pair \(({\theta_i},\Delta )\) and criteria for updating the trust region center and radius are well-defined.

Because sequential RSM is a gradient-based search method, it is prone to getting caught in local optima, so an alternative approach is to build a global response surface sequentially, for which Bayesian methods are well suited, most commonly implemented using techniques such as kriging/Gaussian process regression. Subsequent samples are chosen based on an improvement metric to balance exploitation and exploration. Specific metrics include expected improvement, expected value of information, knowledge gradient, most probable improvement, and maximum information gain.

2.4 Markov Decision Processes

Markov decision processes provide a formal mathematical framework for modeling sequential decision-making under uncertainty. A decision maker chooses an action, receives a reward (or incurs a cost), and then the system state transitions stochastically, with its dynamics depending only on the current system state and the chosen action. This is repeated until the end of the problem horizon.
