Formally, an MDP is defined by a tuple \((S, A, P, R)\), where \(S\) is a set of states, \(A\) is a set of actions, \(P(s' \mid s, a)\) denotes the transition probability of moving from state \(s\) to state \(s'\) after taking action \(a\), and \(R(s, a)\) is the reward function that specifies the immediate (one-period) reward received for performing action \(a\) in state \(s\). A more general MDP model would take the reward as random and depending on the next state. For simplicity of exposition, we assume both state and action spaces are finite, and time-homogeneity for all of the underlying model parameters such as the feasible action space and one-period reward.

A policy \(\pi: S \rightarrow A\) is a mapping from states to actions, specifying what action to take in a given state. This definition provides a deterministic policy, whereas a randomized policy would map to a probability distribution over the set of actions, for which we would write \(\pi(a|s)\) as the probability of taking action \(a\) in state \(s\). Solving an MDP model means finding a policy that optimizes some function of the cumulative rewards.

We consider the setting of finding an optimal policy \(\pi^*\) that maximizes the expected value of the cumulative discounted reward over time starting from initial state \(s_0\), defined by \[\sum_{i=0}^T \gamma^i R(s_{i},\pi(s_{i})),\] where \(s_i\) is the state in period \(i\), \(\gamma \in [0,1]\) is the discount factor, and \(T\) is the time horizon, which could be finite or infinite, fixed or random (e.g., a stopping time). Another extension is the initial state specified by a distribution.

If all the components of the MDP model are known – most importantly the transition dynamics and reward function, then dynamic programming methods can be used to solve for the optimal solution in finite-horizon problems.

Henceforth, we consider infinite-horizon problems, where methods such as value iteration and policy iteration are used, which rely on recursions based on the Bellman equation for the value function, which is defined as follows for a fixed policy \(\pi\): \[V^\pi(s) = E \left[ \sum_{i=0}^{\infty} \gamma^i R(s_{i},\pi(s_{i})) \right],\] for initial state \(s_0 = s\). The Bellman equation provides the following recursive relationship for \(V^{\pi}\): \[\begin{equation} V^\pi(s) = R(s,\pi(s)) + \gamma \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,\pi(s)) V^\pi(s^\prime), ~\forall s \in S, \end{equation}\] where \(V^\pi(s)\) represents the expected cumulative reward starting from state \(s\) and following policy \(\pi\). For a randomized policy, the Bellman equation would be the following: \[\begin{equation} V^\pi(s) = \sum_{a \in A} \pi(a \mid s) \left[ R(s,a) + \gamma \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) V^\pi(s^\prime) \right], ~\forall s \in S. \end{equation}\]

The optimal value function is given by \[\begin{equation} V^*(s) = \max_{\pi \in \Pi} V^{\pi}(s), ~\forall s \in S, \end{equation}\] where \(\Pi\) is the set of feasible policies, and an optimal policy must then satisfy \[\pi^*(s) \in \arg\max_{\pi \in \Pi} V^{\pi}(s), ~\forall s \in S.\]

An important concept in RL is the Q-function, which represents the expected return of taking action \(a\) in state \(s\), and then following the optimal policy thereafter, defined by \[Q(s,a) = R(s,a) + \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) \gamma V^*(s^\prime), ~\forall s \in S.\] Using this definition, the optimal value function can be expressed as \[V^*(s) = \max_{a \in A} Q(s,a), ~\forall s \in S,\] and the optimal policy \(\pi^*(s)\) can be expressed as selecting the action that maximizes the Q-value: \[\pi^*(s) \in \arg\max_{a \in A} Q(s, a), ~\forall s \in S.\]

To solve for the optimal policy, two widely-used methods are value iteration and policy iteration. Value iteration is an iterative approach that finds the optimal value function \(V^*(s)\) by repeatedly updating the value function based on the finite-horizon Bellman optimality equation, essentially carrying out dynamic programming. At each iteration \(k\), the value of a state is updated according to (cf. (2.6) & (2.8)) \[V_{k+1}(s) = \max_{a \in A} \left[ R(s,a) + \gamma \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) V_k(s^\prime) \right], ~\forall s \in S,\] continuing the iteration until the values converge to within a small threshold, at which point the optimal value function \(V^*(s)\) is found. Once the value function converges, the optimal policy \(\pi^*\) can be derived by selecting the action that maximizes the expected value at each state: \[\pi^*(s) \in \arg\max_{a \in A} \left[ R(s,a) + \gamma \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) V^*(s^\prime) \right], \forall s \in S.\]

Policy iteration takes a different approach by alternating between two steps, policy evaluation and policy improvement, as follows:

  • Policy evaluation: The value function \(V^\pi(s)\) for a given policy \(\pi\) is computed by solving the Bellman equation – either (2.6) or (2.7), depending on the type of policy – for each state \(s \in S\).

  • Policy improvement: The policy is updated by choosing the action that maximizes the expected return for each state: \[\pi^\prime(s) \in \arg\max_{a \in A} \left[ R(s,a) + \gamma \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) V^\pi(s^\prime) \right],~\forall s \in S.\]

The policy improvement step ensures that the new policy \(\pi^\prime\) is at least as good as the old policy \(\pi\), and if no further improvement is possible, the algorithm terminates. Since each iteration improves the policy and there are a finite number of stationary policies, the process is guaranteed to converge to the optimal policy \(\pi^*\) in a finite number of iterations.

2.5 Stochastic Gradient Estimation

The gradient estimator is key to the success of an SA algorithm used for optimization. The most readily available gradient estimates employ finite differences, requiring only performance estimates and no knowledge of the underlying system, as in black-box simulation. However, finite-difference-based gradient estimates can be noisy if the measurements are highly volatile and become computationally expensive in high dimensions, as the number of samples or simulation replications needed to obtain a single estimate grows linearly with the parameter dimension. Moreover, finite-difference estimators are generally biased. In this section, we discuss the main “direct” stochastic gradient estimation techniques that provide unbiased estimators, specifically approaches based on perturbation analysis, the likelihood ratio method, and the method of weak derivatives. Many of these require just a single simulation replication to provide both estimates of the output performance and its gradient.

Stochastic gradient estimation methods aim to estimate the gradient of an output performance from a stochastic model. We focus on outputs that are expected values. Specifically, for the SO problem (2.4), we wish to estimate

\[\begin{equation*} {\nabla _\theta } \mathbb{E}[Y(\theta ;\omega )] = \left( {{{\partial \mathbb{E}[Y(\theta ;\omega )]} \over {\partial {\theta _1}}}, \ldots ,{{\partial \mathbb{E}[Y(\theta ;\omega )]} \over {\partial {\theta _d}}}} \right). \end{equation*}\]

The infinitesimal perturbation analysis (IPA) or pathwise gradient is given by \({\nabla _\theta }Y(\theta ;\omega )\). Under appropriate conditions justifying the interchange of differentiation and integration, the IPA estimator is unbiased, i.e.,

\[\begin{equation*} \mathbb{E}[{\nabla _\theta }Y(\theta ;\omega )] = {\nabla _\theta } \mathbb{E}[Y(\theta ;\omega )]. \end{equation*}\]

Sufficient conditions required for unbiasedness stem from the application of the dominated convergence theorem, which for the IPA estimator generally means that the performance function \(Y( \cdot ;\omega )\) must be Lipschitz continuous almost surely. When \(Y( \cdot ;\omega )\) is discontinuous, smoothed perturbation analysis (SPA) can be applied to derive an unbiased gradient estimator by conditioning on certain quantities to smooth out the discontinuities. Viewing the randomness or sample path \(\omega\) as a sequence of \(m\) input random variables \(X = ({X_1}, \ldots ,{X_m})\), we rewrite the output \(Y(X(\theta);\theta)\) as a function of input variables \(X\) in place of the dependence on \(\omega\) (and reverse the order of the arguments), and the IPA estimator is given by the chain rule:

\[\begin{equation} \nabla_{\theta}Y(X;\theta) = \left. \sum\limits_{j = 1}^m {{{\partial Y(x;\theta )} \over {\partial {x_j}}}} \right|_{x = X} {{\partial {X_j}} \over {\partial \theta }} + {{\partial Y(X;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }}, \end{equation}\]

where the random variable derivatives \({{\partial {X_j}} \over {\partial \theta }}\) are defined by

\[\begin{equation*} {{\partial {X_j}} \over {\partial \theta }} = - {{{{\partial F_j({X_j};\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }}} \over {f_j({X_j};\theta )}},\quad j = 1, \ldots ,m, \end{equation*}\]

with \({F_j}( \cdot ;\theta )\) and \({f_j}( \cdot ;\theta )\) denoting the c.d.f. and p.d.f. of \(X_j\).

As mentioned earlier, for the IPA estimator to be unbiased, the performance function generally needs to be Lipschitz continuous. To demonstrate where this fails and the IPA estimator is biased, consider the output performance function \(Y=1\{ {X_1} + {X_2} \le 0\}\). The indicator function introduces a discontinuity, making the IPA estimator biased. By conditioning on \(X_2\), SPA can be applied to address the discontinuity as follows:

\[\begin{align*} \nabla_{\theta} \mathbb{E}[Y(X_1,X_2;\theta)] & = {{\partial \mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[1\{ {X_1} + {X_2} \le 0\} |{X_2}]]} \over {\partial \theta }} = {{\partial \mathbb{E}[{F_1}( - {X_2};\theta )]} \over {\partial \theta }} \\ & = \mathbb{E} \left[ {{{\partial {F_1}( - {X_2};\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }} - {f_1}( - {X_2};\theta ){{\partial {X_2}} \over {\partial \theta }}} \right], \end{align*}\] where the second equality assumes \(X_1\) is independent of \(X_2\), so that an unbiased SPA estimator is given by \[\begin{equation*} {{\partial {F_1}( - {X_2};\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }} - {f_1}( - {X_2};\theta ){{\partial {X_2}} \over {\partial \theta }}. \end{equation*}\]

More generally, SPA is an application of conditional Monte Carlo, where the IPA estimator \(dY/d\theta\) is biased, usually due to a discontinuity caused either by the form of the output function or from the underlying dynamics of the system. By selecting an appropriate \(X\), which could denote a vector of random variables in the system, the SPA estimator differentiates the conditional expectation \(\mathbb{E}[Y|X]\). The success of SPA depends on finding an appropriate \(X\) such that the resulting conditional expectation derivative \(d\mathbb{E}[Y|X]/d\theta\) is sufficiently smooth to be unbiased and can be efficiently estimated.

Next we consider the likelihood ratio (LR) method, also known as the score function method. Suppose the (joint) p.d.f. of \(X\) depends on the parameter \(\theta\), i.e., \(f(\cdot; \theta)\), but the support of \(f\) denoted by \(\Omega\) does not depend on \(\theta\).

Then the gradient of \(\mathbb{E}[Y]\) is given by (under appropriate conditions justifying the interchange of differentiation and integration)

\[\begin{align*} {\nabla _\theta }\mathbb{E}[Y(X)] & = {\nabla _\theta }\int_\Omega Y (x)f(x;\theta ){\mkern 1mu} dx = \int_\Omega Y (x){\nabla _\theta }f(x;\theta ){\mkern 1mu} dx \\ &= \int_\Omega Y (x){\nabla _\theta }\ln f(x;\theta )f(x;\theta ){\mkern 1mu} dx =\mathbb{E}[Y(X){\nabla _\theta }\ln f(X;\theta )], \end{align*}\]

so the LR gradient estimator is given by

\[\begin{equation} Y(X){\nabla _\theta }\ln f(X;\theta ). \end{equation}\]

Because the parameter \(\theta\) does not directly appear in \(Y\), the LR estimator (2.10) will be unbiased even if \(Y\) is not well behaved, e.g., if \(Y\) is discontinuous. When the parameter \(\theta\) appears directly in \(Y\), a method called push-out LR can be applied if a suitable change of variables can be found. We illustrate with the simplest example: Consider the output \(Y=1\{ {X_1} + \theta \le 0\}\), for which LR cannot be directly applied (nor IPA, since the function is discontinuous). By a change of variable \({X_2} = {X_1} + \theta\), the density of the new random variable \({X_2}\) is \({f_2}(x - \theta ;\theta )\). Then the LR method can be applied to the new output function \(Y = 1\{ {X_2} \le 0\}\), as the parameter \(\theta\) has been pushed out of the original output function \(Y\) and into the input density.

If \(\theta\) appears in both the input distribution and the output performance, where both are sufficiently well behaved, then a combined IPA/LR estimator can be used, which would simply add the two estimators given by (2.9) and (2.10). However, in the case where this is not the case, e.g., either the output function or input density is discontinuous with respect to the parameter (or the support depends on the parameters), then other methods must be used, such as SPA discussed earlier or the generalized likelihood ratio (GLR) method, which is an extension of the combined IPA/LR estimator and can also be viewed as a generalized version of the push-out LR method. The GLR method assumes an output function of the following form: \[Y = \varphi (g(X;\theta )),\] where \(g\) is an \(m\)-dimensional vector of twice-differentiable functions and \(\varphi\) is a measurable function that is not necessarily continuous.

Then the GLR estimator is the following extension of the LR estimator:

\[\begin{equation} \varphi (g(X;\theta )) \left( {\nabla _\theta }\ln f(X;\theta ) + d(X;\theta) \right), \end{equation}\]

\[\begin{eqnarray*} d(x;\theta) &=& \sum\limits_{i = 1}^m {e_i^T} {J_g^{-1}}(x;\theta) \left( \frac{\partial {J_g}(x;\theta )}{\partial {x_i}} \right) J_g^{-1}(x;\theta) \frac{\partial g(x;\theta )}{\partial \theta } \\ & & \hspace{-40pt} - {\rm trace} \left( J_g^{-1}(x;\theta )\frac{\partial J_g(x;\theta )}{\partial {\theta}} \right) -{\left[ {{J_g^{-1}}{{(x;\theta )}}{{\partial g(x;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }}} \right]^T} {\nabla_x }\ln f(x;\theta), \\ & & {{\partial g(x;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }} = {\left( {{{\partial {g_1}(x;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }},{{\partial {g_2}(x;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }},...,{{\partial {g_m}(x;\theta )} \over {\partial \theta }}} \right)^T}, \end{eqnarray*}\]

where \({J_g}(x;\theta )\) denotes the \(m \times m\) Jacobian matrix of vector \(g\) with respect to the vector \(x\), \(e_i\) represents the unit vector in the \(i\)-th direction, and \({{\partial {J_g}(x;\theta )} \over {\partial z}}\) denotes the matrix obtained by differentiating each element of \({J_g}(x;\theta )\) with respect to \(z\) individually.

The final stochastic gradient estimation approach we consider is the method of weak derivatives (WD), also known as measure-valued differentiation, where the setting is the same as that of the LR method, i.e., with (joint) input p.d.f. \(f(\cdot; \theta)\) and the support of \(f\) not depending on \(\theta\). Again following the first steps used in deriving the LR estimator by passing the gradient operator inside the expectation (under appropriate conditions justifying the interchange of differentiation and integration):

\[\begin{align*} {\nabla _\theta }\mathbb{E}[Y(X)] & = {\nabla _\theta }\int_\Omega Y (x)f(x;\theta ){\mkern 1mu} dx = \int_\Omega Y (x){\nabla _\theta }f(x;\theta ){\mkern 1mu} dx \\ &= \int_\Omega Y (x){c(\theta)[f^{(+)}(x;\theta) - f^{(-)}(x;\theta)] } dx \\ & = c(\theta) \mathbb{E}[Y(X^{(+)})-Y(X^{(-)})], ~~X^{(\pm)} \sim f^{(\pm)}(\cdot; \theta), \end{align*}\]

where \(f^{(\pm)}\) are (joint) p.d.f.(s) and the triplet \((c(\theta), f^{(+)}(\cdot; \theta), f^{(-)}(\cdot; \theta))\) is called the weak derivative, which always exists (e.g., Hahn-Jordan decomposition) but is not unique; in fact, there are an infinite number of weak derivatives. Then the WD gradient estimator is given by

\[\begin{equation} c(\theta) [Y(X^{(+)})-Y(X^{(-)})]. \end{equation}\]

Again, because the parameter \(\theta\) does not directly appear in \(Y\), the WD estimator (2.12) will be unbiased even if \(Y\) is not well behaved, e.g., if \(Y\) is discontinuous.

We conclude this section with a simple queueing example to illustrate the IPA, LR, and WD estimators.

Single-Server Queue Example

Consider a single-server queue, where \({A_i}\) denotes the interarrival time between the \((i\)-\(1)\)th and \(i\)th customers and \(X_i\) is the service time of the \(i\)th customer. The output performance of interest is \(Y_i\), the system time of the \(i\)th customer, comprising both queueing and service time.

Assume the system starts empty and operates under the first-come, first-served (FCFS) queue discipline, the completion times of the first \(N\) customers are determined solely by their arrival times and service durations, and the system time of a customer satisfies the recursive Lindley equation:

\[\begin{equation*} {Y_{i + 1}} = {X_{i + 1}} + ({Y_i} - {A_{i + 1}})^+. \end{equation*}\]

Furthermore, assume the arrival process is independent of the service times, which themselves are mutually independent but not necessarily identically distributed, with p.d.f. and c.d.f. for \(X_i\) given by \(f_i\) and \(F_i\), respectively. If \(\theta\) is a parameter within the service time distribution(s), then the IPA estimator can be derived by differentiating the above Lindley equation with respect to \(\theta\):

\[\begin{equation} {{d{Y_{i + 1}}} \over {d\theta }} = {{d{X_{i + 1}}} \over {d\theta }} + {{d{Y_i}} \over {d\theta }} \cdot {\bf{1}}\{ {Y_i} \ge {A_{i + 1}}\}. \end{equation}\]

For the average system time over the first \(N\) customers denoted by \(\bar{Y}_N = {1 \over N}\sum\limits_{i = 1}^N {{Y_i}},\) the IPA estimator for the derivative of the average system time is given by

\[\begin{equation} \nabla_{\theta} \bar{Y}_N = {1 \over N}\sum\limits_{i = 1}^N {{{d{Y_i}} \over {d\theta }}} = {1 \over N}\sum\limits_{m = 1}^M {\sum\limits_{i = {n_{m - 1}} + 1}^{{n_m}} {\sum\limits_{j = {n_{m - 1}} + 1}^i {{{d{X_j}} \over {d\theta }}} } } , \end{equation}\] where \(M\) denotes the number of observed busy periods, and \(n_m\) represents the index of the last customer served in the \(m\)-th busy period (with \({n_0} = {\rm{ }}0\) and \({n_M}{\rm{ }} = {\rm{ }}N\) for \(M\) complete busy periods). The implementation of this IPA estimator requires maintaining two ongoing quantities: one corresponding to Equation (2.13) and another for the summation in Equation (2.14). Therefore, the additional computational overhead is minimal, and no modifications to the underlying simulation are necessary.

Again, assuming the interarrival times and service times are independently generated, the joint p.d.f. of the input random variables is the product of the p.d.f of the arrival time distribution and the individual service time distributions, given by

\[\begin{equation*} g({A_1}, \ldots ,{A_N})\prod\limits_{i = 1}^N {{f_i}} ({X_i};\theta ), \end{equation*}\] where \(g\) represents the joint p.d.f. of the arrival times. Since \(g\) doesn’t depend on \(\theta\), the LR gradient estimator is given by

\[\begin{equation*} \left. \nabla_{\theta} \bar{Y}_N \right|_{{\rm{LR}}} = \bar{Y}_N \sum\limits_{i = 1}^N {{{\partial \ln {f_i({X_i};\theta )}} \over {\partial \theta }}} . \end{equation*}\]

The WD gradient estimator is similarly derived by applying the product rule of differentiation:

\[\begin{eqnarray*} \left. \nabla_{\theta} \bar{Y}_N \right|_{{\rm{WD}}} &=& \sum\limits_{i = 1}^N {{c_i}} (\theta )\left[ {\bar{Y}_N}({A_1}, \ldots ,{A_N}, X_1, \ldots , X_i^{( + )}, \ldots , X_N) \right. \\ & & \left. ~~~~~~~~~~- {\bar{Y}_N}({A_1}, \ldots ,{A_N}, X_1, \ldots ,X_i^{( - )}, \ldots , X_N) \right], \end{eqnarray*}\] where \(X_i^{(\pm)}\sim f_i^{(\pm)}\) for \(i = 1, \ldots ,N\), and \(({c_i}(\theta),f_i^{(+)},f_i^{(-)})\) is a weak derivative for \({f_i}\). It is important to note that in general the implementation of the estimator requires \({2^N}\) distinct sample paths and corresponding sample performance estimates whenever the parameter \(\theta\) appears in \(N\) input random variables. However, randomization can be used to reduce the number of required sample paths.

2.6 Variance Reduction Techniques

Variance reduction techniques enhance the computational efficiency of estimation from a stochastic simulation model, in the best cases leading to orders of magnitude improvements. However, care must be taken in their application, because degradation of performance is also possible, except for the use of conditional Monte Carlo, which guarantees that one can do no worse. We end this chapter by briefly summarizing some of the most widely used approaches: antithetic variates, control variates, indirect estimation, conditional Monte Carlo, importance sampling, splitting, stratified sampling, the quasi-Monte Carlo method, and common random numbers. Common random numbers differs from the others in that it addresses comparison between outputs of two different simulation models, whereas the others aim at increasing precision of output estimates from a single simulation model. Common random numbers can generally be used in conjunction with any of the others, which themselves may sometimes also be combined.

For each of the variance reduction techniques, we provide the main idea and an example (or two) based on the FCFS single-server queue used in the previous section, where \(\bar{Y}_N\) will denote the output random variable of interest (average system time over \(N\) customers), generated by interarrival time and service time input random variables \(\{A_i, X_i\}\). Otherwise, \(Y\) denotes the “generic” output random variable, and the superscript “(\(i\))” denotes the \(i\)th simulation replication for any random variable.

  • Antithetic Variates
    (i) Main idea: Exploit induced negative correlation by using pairs of simulation replications, generally via the random numbers, based on the following basic relationship: \[{\rm{Var}}\left( Y + Y' \right) = {\rm{Var}} Y + {\rm{Var}} Y' + 2 {\rm{Cov}} (Y,Y'),\] where the righthand side is less than the sum of the individual variances if \(Y\) and \(Y'\) are negatively correlated.
    (ii) Example: Use random numbers \(U\) and \(1-U\) for the inputs in pairs, where \(U \sim U(0,1)\), rather than i.i.d. \(U\) and \(U'\). If \(\bar{Y}_N\) is a monotone function of the input random variables – as might be expected for average system time as a function of interarrival times and service times, then the paired average \((\bar{Y}_N^{(1)} + \bar{Y}_N^{(2)})/2\) using these antithetic variates is expected to have lower variance than if two independent replications were used, since \[{\rm{Var}}\left( \bar{Y}_N^{(1)} + \bar{Y}_N^{(2)} \right) < {\rm{Var}} \bar{Y}_N^{(1)} + {\rm{Var}} \bar{Y}_N^{(2)} \mbox{~if~} {\rm{Cov}}\left( \bar{Y}_N^{(1)}, \bar{Y}_N^{(2)} \right) <0.\]
    (iii) Practical utility: Minimal compared to most other variance reduction techniques but easy to implement.

  • Control Variates
    (i) Main idea: Exploit strong correlation between the output random variable of interest \(Y\) (which has unknown mean) and another system random variable \(Z\) with known mean \(\mathbb{E}[Z]\), with the new control variate estimator given by \[Y - \lambda (Z -\mathbb{E}[Z]),\] which can be seen to be unbiased with variance \[{\rm{Var}}~Y + \lambda^2 {\rm{Var}}~Z -2\lambda {\rm{Cov}}(Y,Z).\] An appropriate choice of \(\lambda\) results in variance reduction, and the theoretically optimal choice is \(\lambda ^* = {{\rm{Cov}}(Y,Z)}/{\rm{Var}}~Z\) with the corresponding minimal variance \({\rm{Var}}~Y - {{{[{\rm{Cov}}(Y,Z)}]^2}} / {{\rm{Var}}~Z}\). For multiple random variables correlated with \(Y\), linear combinations can be used as control variates.
    (ii) Examples: System times and service times should be highly positively correlated in a queueing system and especially for the FCFS single-server queue, so that \(\lambda\) would be positive. Under the scenario where the service times have a common mean \(\mathbb{E}[X]\), the control variate estimator using average service time as a control variate would be \[\bar{Y}_N - \lambda (\bar{X}_N -\mathbb{E}[X]).\] Other obvious possible control variates include the arrival rate and the system/server utilization. A less obvious control variate would be using the steady-state mean system time, which would be known if the arrival process were Poisson, but then finding an unbiased estimator for this known quantity would require care.
    An example from finance: \(Y\) could be the payoff of an American call option with no analytical expectation, while \(Z\) could be the payoff of a European call option with a known analytical expectation. Since \(Y\) and \(Z\) are positively correlated (both depend on the underlying asset price), \(Z\) serves as an effective control variate.
    (iii) Practical utility: Can be highly effective but very problem dependent, as it relies on finding a good control variate such that the correlation is both strong and well understood, as indicated by the theoretically optimal value of \(\lambda^*\) depending on \({\rm{Cov}}(Y,Z)\), which is generally unknown, and \({\rm{Var}}~Z\), which may also be unknown. In particular, if the control variate chosen is independent, then the control variate estimator would have higher variance for any nonzero \(\lambda\).

  • Indirect Estimation
    (i) Main idea: Exploit known relationship between the output random variable of interest and another system random variable. (ii) Example: Using Little’s Law for a queueing system, which relates the time average of the number-in-system process to the customer average system time. Going in the appropriate direction will decrease variance, but not vice versa, and it also depends on whether or not the arrival rate is known or must also be estimated.
    (iii) Practical utility: Generally even more problem or domain dependent than most of the other techniques.

  • Conditional Monte Carlo
    (i) Main idea: Condition on one or more other random variables in the system model. Guaranteed to do no worse than the original estimator by the variance decomposition relationship: \[\begin{equation*} {\rm{Var}}~Y = \mathbb{E}[{\rm{Var}}[Y|X]] + {\rm{Var}}[\mathbb{E}[Y|X]] \ge {\rm{Var}}[\mathbb{E}[Y|X]]. \end{equation*}\] (ii) Example: To estimate the expected system time in the FCFS single-server queue, condition on the number of customers in queue (if any), say \(N_q\), and the remaining service time of the customer in service (if any), say \(S_r\), for which the conditional MC estimator would be given by \[S_r + N_q \mathbb{E}[X].\] If the service times were exponential, an even lower variance estimator would simply take the number in system that each arriving customer sees and multiply that by the mean service time \(\mathbb{E}[X]\). (iii) Practical utility: Can be highly effective but very problem dependent, as it relies on finding appropriate random variables on which to condition that leads to a conditional expectation quantity that can be easily estimated from the simulation.

  • Importance Sampling
    (i) Main idea: Change the underlying input distribution(s) and then reweight the output estimator accordingly by the likelihood ratio related to the input distributions.
    (ii) Example: If the output of interest is the probability of the system reaching a huge number of customers in the system, then the obvious way to increase the frequency of this occurring is to increase the arrival rate or the service time, which is how importance sampling could be implemented in a queueing system.
    (iii) Practical utility: Most useful in rare event simulation when an event is unlikely to occur in a simulation, so that the output may return 0 almost all of the time, giving deceptive confidence intervals, since the output is obviously not normal. This technique can also be used to generate multiple sample paths (simulation replications) at different input settings from the original simulation settings, again by using the likelihood ratio, and this application is sometimes known as “green simulation” for its reuse of the single sample path.

  • Splitting
    (i) Main idea: Selectively prune or split sample paths (simulation replications) according to some criteria, with the idea that pruned paths are not likely to contribute to the desired output, whereas the paths to be split are more promising. When and how to carry out the splitting is the key, and many procedures have been proposed.
    (ii) Example: Using the same example as for importance sampling, where the output of interest is the probability of the system reaching a huge number of customers in the system, then intuitively splitting should occur for highly congested sample paths that are more likely to reach the large number in system, whereas paths in which the system is empty might be pruned.
    (iii) Practical utility: Like importance sampling, this technique can be useful in rare event simulation, and is sometimes viewed as a type of dual or complementary approach to importance sampling.

  • Stratified Sampling
    (i) Main idea: Partition sample paths (simulation replications) into groups such that the variation within each group is relatively small, thus reducing the overall variance.
    (ii) Example: Consider the FCFS single-server queue with a distribution on the initial number in system (state), e.g., 0 or 1 with equal probability. Rather than randomly sampling from this initial distribution and averaging over all replications, one could divide the total number of replications into half for each of the two initial states, and average within each set of replications (initial state 0 or state 1) and then give equal weight to compute the final overall average. In some ways, this particular example could be viewed as combining conditional Monte Carlo and quasi-Monte Carlo (to be described next), as it uses conditioning on the initial state, which is deterministically distributed rather than randomly sampled.
    (iii) Practical utility: When a good stratification is available – again very problem dependent, this approach can be effective.

  • Quasi-Monte Carlo
    (i) Main idea: Rather than using i.i.d. \(U(0,1)\) random numbers, the goal is to generate “low-discrepancy” sequences deterministically, i.e., to space the points appropriately.
    (ii) Example: Replace the random number generators producing the two interarrival and service time sequences with a separate quasi-MC stream for each of the two processes.
    (iii) Practical utility: Effective for low-dimensional problems, but does not provide confidence intervals for the output without using the randomized form to generate independent replications, each based on a quasi-MC simulation. May also have problems with discontinuous output performance measures.

  • Common Random Numbers
    (i) Main idea: Try to use the same randomness for the same processes in different systems being compared, to induce positive correlation between the corresponding output random variables, specifically reducing the variance of the difference estimator between the two means, again based on the basic relationship used earlier for antithetic variates, expressed now as a difference rather than a sum: \[\begin{equation*} {\rm{Var}}(Y_A - Y_B) = {\rm{Var}}~Y_A + {\rm{Var}}~Y_B - 2{\rm{Cov}}(Y_A,Y_B), \end{equation*}\] where \(Y_A\) and \(Y_B\) denote the output for two different systems A and B, respectively. A confidence interval for the difference would be interpreted as there being a statistically significant difference in performance between the two systems if it did not contain 0.
    (ii) Example: For the FCFS single-server queue, if we wish to compare two systems with different service time distributions but the same mean and arrival process, then common random numbers should work well by using a separate stream of random numbers for the service times and the exact same interarrival times for the arrival process, allowing perfect coupling for that process.
    (iii) Practical utility: If implemented correctly, this is a particularly effective technique, but again its success can be very problem dependent. The concept of synchronization is the key to achieving positive correlation between systems being compared. Generally, this means assigning different random number generators to different processes in a system, so that when comparing multiple systems, the corresponding processes could use the same stream of random numbers to increase the likelihood of inducing positive correlation.

2.8 Industrial AI Agents for Simulation and Optimization

The methodologies reviewed in the previous sections—stochastic simulation, stochastic optimization, Markov decision processes, and gradient-based learning—provide the algorithmic foundations for a new class of intelligent industrial systems. In such systems, simulation is not merely a tool for offline analysis, and optimization is not limited to one-shot decision support. Rather, they are embedded into an operational loop in which AI agents continuously perceive the factory state, reason over structured industrial data, call specialized optimization and prediction modules, and generate or recommend actions under safety constraints.

A central insight emerging from recent industrial practice is that strong foundation models alone are insufficient for reliable deployment in manufacturing environments. What is equally important is an engineering layer that connects models to plant data, operational tools, expert knowledge, execution logic, and safety constraints. This layer can be viewed as an agent harness: a system-level infrastructure that enables AI agents to interact with industrial environments in a controllable, auditable, and reusable way. In this perspective, the agent is not a standalone large language model, but a composite decision-making system built on top of simulation, optimization, and structured operational knowledge.

This section presents three complementary examples of such industrial AI systems. The first is an industrial agent harness for digital factories, which integrates data modeling, digital twins, hybrid agents, skills, and safety mechanisms into a unified framework. The second is a PMC (production planning and control) agent, which illustrates how AI agents can support planning tasks in high-mix, low-volume, and highly customized manufacturing settings. The third is a world-model-based layout generation framework, which shows how simulation-ready digital factory environments can be constructed directly from heterogeneous engineering inputs. Together, these examples show how the concepts developed throughout this chapter can be assembled into deployable industrial intelligence systems.

2.8.1 Industrial Agent Harness for Digital Factories

A major obstacle to deploying AI agents in industrial settings is not merely model capability, but the lack of a reusable infrastructure that connects models to factory environments. In many plants, data remain fragmented across operational technology (OT) and information technology (IT) systems, interfaces differ from one site to another, expert knowledge is tacit rather than formalized, and the cost of initializing digital models is substantial. As a result, industrial AI solutions often remain project-specific and difficult to scale across factories. The industrial agent harness addresses this problem by providing a common layer that standardizes data access, context construction, tool invocation, knowledge encapsulation, and operational safeguards.

The proposed harness is built on several principles. First, the agent is viewed as the combination of a model and a harness, where the model may be replaced over time, but the harness provides the persistent engineering interface to the factory. Second, the overall architecture is hybrid: routine and well-structured tasks are handled by deterministic modules such as rule engines, operations research solvers, reinforcement learning policies, or specialized machine learning models, while large language models are reserved for intent understanding, cross-module coordination, exception handling, and explanation. Third, the system relies on structured workflow orchestration, in which predefined execution graphs support routine operation and dynamic replanning is triggered when disturbances or abnormal events arise. Fourth, expert knowledge is encapsulated in reusable skills, which are loaded on demand rather than injected into the model context all at once.

From the agent’s perspective, effective industrial decision-making requires at least three layers of context. The first is a static model layer, which describes the relatively persistent structure of the plant, including equipment, process flow, and topology. The second is a real-time state layer, which captures the current operating condition of the system, such as machine status, inventory levels, production progress, or sensor measurements. The third is a historical experience layer, which stores past decisions, calibrated parameters, and successful recovery paths. Together, these three layers provide the agent with both perception and memory: the static layer tells the agent what the factory is, the dynamic layer tells it what is happening now, and the historical layer tells it what has worked before.

Constructing this context requires a gradual modeling pipeline. The first stage converts engineering drawings such as CAD files into structured representations of factory assets and spatial layouts. The second stage connects physical devices through protocol adaptation and data channel binding. The third stage introduces production data, including bills of materials, process parameters, logistics information, and operational targets. The fourth stage performs simulation-based calibration and validation so that the resulting digital twin can serve as a reliable baseline for downstream decision support and agent execution. This four-stage process is important because it reduces the deployment barrier: rather than demanding a fully detailed digital factory from the outset, it allows the agent environment to be built incrementally.

Once the plant representation is available, different classes of agents can be organized into a hybrid decision architecture. Rule-based agents handle threshold-triggered monitoring, alarms, refilling logic, and compliance checks. Operations research modules solve structured planning problems such as production scheduling and routing. Reinforcement learning agents adapt control policies in dynamic settings, for example by adjusting local dispatching or pacing decisions after disturbances. Specialized machine learning models support tasks such as predictive maintenance and visual inspection. The large language model plays a different role: it interprets human intent, synthesizes information across plant status, order demand, inventory, and capacity, coordinates the invocation of specialized modules, and explains the resulting decisions in a human-readable form. In other words, the LLM does not replace optimization or simulation; rather, it orchestrates them.

A key mechanism in this architecture is the skill system. System skills encode how the factory is represented and how the agent ecosystem operates, including data model structure, topology semantics, protocol configuration, and coordination logic. Business skills encode domain-specific procedures, such as scheduling rules, fault diagnosis workflows, traceability requirements, operating constraints, and calibration parameters. To manage context efficiently, skills are loaded progressively. At startup, only compact skill descriptions are retained. Full instructions and associated references are injected into the model context only when a task activates the corresponding skill, and they may be released afterward. This design reduces context overload while preserving access to detailed industrial knowledge when needed.

The harness also requires workflow management and memory control. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) can serve as the backbone for normal process execution, linking constraint checking, scheduling, simulation-based validation, and reporting into a persistent pipeline. When exceptions occur, the large language model can intervene, diagnose the disruption, reorganize the workflow, and resume execution from checkpoints. Because industrial agents often run continuously, context management becomes essential: recent goals and critical interaction history should remain directly available; older decisions can be compressed into structured summaries; raw logs and sensor streams should be offloaded to external storage while preserving retrieval paths. This layered memory strategy is necessary to avoid context saturation during long-horizon operation.

Finally, no industrial agent architecture is viable without a strong safety harness. In manufacturing environments, incorrect actions may damage equipment, waste production, or create hazards for human operators. For this reason, the architecture adopts multiple safety layers. One layer is progressive trust, where the agent may begin with monitoring and recommendation functions before being allowed to participate in collaborative or autonomous control. A second layer is operation verification, where different classes of actions require different confirmation mechanisms. A third layer is simulation-based validation: production actions should first be tested in the digital twin before execution in the physical plant. In particular, a hard boundary is maintained between the AI layer and the lowest-level control systems, so that the agent does not directly issue unsafe commands to industrial controllers. This illustrates an important principle of industrial AI: safety is not an afterthought but part of the system architecture itself.

Overall, the industrial agent harness provides a concrete example of how simulation, optimization, digital twins, and AI can be integrated into a practical industrial operating system. The main contribution of such a framework lies not in any single model, but in the engineering of reusable interfaces between models, plant data, industrial tools, and safety constraints.

2.8.2 PMC Agents for Production Planning and Control

Production planning and control (PMC) is a natural application domain for industrial AI agents because it combines demand uncertainty, supply constraints, capacity limitations, and frequent replanning. These challenges are especially pronounced in high-mix, low-volume, and highly customized manufacturing environments, where planning decisions must be revised frequently and manual forecasting or coordination becomes costly and error-prone. In such settings, the fundamental tension is between uncertain and changing demand on the one hand, and rigid procurement and production constraints on the other.

The PMC agent addresses this challenge by treating planning support as an interactive computational service rather than as a monolithic enterprise system. Instead of building a large and rigid software stack, the design philosophy is to create an AI-based “PMC calculator” that planners can query on demand. Through conversational interaction, the planner can request demand-plan generation, evaluate supply feasibility, inspect bottlenecks, and revise plans quickly. This interaction model is important because in many production settings the value of AI does not come only from full automation, but from reducing the cognitive and coordination burden on human planners while preserving transparency and controllability.

Conceptually, the PMC agent can be interpreted using the tools introduced earlier in this chapter. The planning environment can be modeled as a stochastic decision system whose state includes demand information, inventory positions, work-in-process, available capacity, supplier commitments, and production priorities. Simulation can be used to evaluate the downstream consequences of a candidate production plan under uncertainty, for example by estimating lateness risk, capacity utilization, or service-level performance. Optimization modules can then search for schedules or allocations that balance multiple constraints. When the environment evolves over time and decisions are repeatedly updated, the problem also admits an MDP interpretation, with the agent using current state information to propose or refine actions over a rolling horizon. In this sense, the PMC agent is not a separate topic from simulation optimization; it is one of its most relevant industrial embodiments.

In practical deployment, the role of the large language model is not to solve the entire planning problem directly. Rather, it acts as an interface and coordinator. It translates planner intent into structured tasks, retrieves the relevant business rules and planning skills, invokes optimization or simulation modules when needed, and presents the outputs in a way that is easier for planners to interpret and act upon. This division of labor is especially appropriate in PMC settings, where many subproblems are structured enough to benefit from deterministic optimization, while the overall planning loop still requires flexible reasoning, explanation, and exception handling. Thus, the PMC agent can be understood as a domain-specific instance of the broader hybrid-agent architecture discussed above.

From a systems perspective, the significance of the PMC agent lies in demonstrating how conversational AI, stochastic planning, and optimization can be combined into a lightweight but useful industrial decision tool. Rather than replacing existing planning logic entirely, it augments the planner’s ability to process uncertainty, evaluate alternatives, and respond to frequent changes. This makes PMC a compelling application scenario for industrial AI agents and a natural bridge between the theory of simulation optimization and the practical realities of manufacturing operations.

2.8.3 World Models for Direct Engineering Layout Generation

An important extension of the industrial AI framework is the use of world models to directly generate engineering layouts for digital factories. Traditional factory modeling often depends on manual reconstruction from CAD drawings, engineering documents, and equipment lists, which is labor-intensive and difficult to scale across plants. By contrast, a world model aims to infer a structured and simulation-ready representation of the factory from heterogeneous inputs, including layout drawings, device annotations, spatial constraints, and process semantics.

The resulting representation is more than a geometric scene. It acts as a latent operational model of the plant, encoding not only where equipment is located, but also how production zones, logistics paths, and equipment relations should be organized. In this way, the world model bridges the gap between raw engineering inputs and executable digital twins. Once generated, the layout can be rendered as a 2D–3D factory scene, inspected by users, and further refined through human feedback or downstream calibration.

From the perspective of simulation and optimization, this capability is particularly important because it reduces one of the major bottlenecks in industrial deployment: model initialization. A directly generated engineering layout provides the structural prior needed by discrete-event simulation, routing algorithms, scheduling modules, and agent-based coordination. It therefore accelerates the construction of digital twins and enables the agent system to move more quickly from offline modeling to online decision support.

Figure 2.9 illustrates a factory scene generated and rendered through the world-model-based pipeline. Such a scene provides a practical basis for subsequent simulation, verification, and optimization, while also giving human planners an interpretable visual interface for validating the generated plant representation.

Notes and References

MC methods were introduced in the 1940s by Stanislaw Ulam and John von Neumann (Metropolis and Ulam 1949). The genesis of MC simulation is often attributed to an anecdote involving Ulam, who, while engaged in a solitary card game, pondered the probability of drawing a straight flush from a standard deck of 52 cards. This contemplation led Ulam to recognize the potential of using random sampling to solve complex probabilistic problems. John von Neumann played a pivotal role by implementing these random simulation processes on the ENIAC computer, one of the earliest electronic general-purpose computers. MC simulation was pioneered by scientists who employed stochastic methods to address intricate neutron diffusion problems in nuclear reactors (Metropolis et al. 1953). MC simulation is widely used in disciplines from engineering to biology to finance to management, due to the flexibility in modeling complex stochastic systems that would otherwise be intractable (Hammersley 2013).

The historical evolution of DES dates back to the 1960s, a period marked by significant advances in computational capabilities and the increasing complexity of industrial systems (Taylor et al. 2009). Early DES software was developed to address specific industrial problems, primarily in manufacturing and logistics, where the need to optimize production lines and supply chains was paramount (Nance and Sargent 2002). Over the decades, both industry and academia have contributed to the progressive development of DES software, enhancing its flexibility, scalability, and applicability across a myriad of sectors. The integration of DES with other advanced methodologies such as ML has further expanded its utility, enabling the simulation of increasingly complex and high-dimensional systems (Collins et al. 2014; Dagkakis and Heavey 2016; Nelson 2016).

More information on ABS can be found in Brown et al. (2004; Epstein 2009, 1996; Seibel and Kellam 2003; North et al. 2013; Gilbert and Troitzsch 2005; Macal and North 2005), with information on specific ABS platforms found in Grigoryev (2012; Tisue and Wilensky 2004).

ABS can be viewed as a variation of DES, as state changes in virtually all existing ABS occur at a countable number of points in time; for more on the relationship between ABS and DES, see Beeker and Page (2006; Macal et al. 2013; Pegden 2010).

The warehouse management example is based on Zhang, Liu, et al. (2023; Zhang, Li, et al. 2025).

Comprehensive discussions on R\(\&\)S as well as multiple comparison procedures can be found in Bechhofer (1995; Goldsman et al. 1998). A survey tracing the development of techniques in SO over finite sets can be found in Tekin and Sabuncuoglu (2004). Other work in R&S with applicability to the simulation setting include the following: subset selection (Chen et al. 2008; Zhang, Chen, et al. 2023; Zhang, Peng, et al. 2023), feasibility determination (Gao and Chen 2016; Shi et al. 2022), quantile-based selection (Peng et al. 2021; Shin et al. 2022), general sampling distribution selection (Zhang et al. 2020; Chen and Ryzhov 2023), context-dependent selection (Shen et al. 2021; Du et al. 2024; Li et al. 2024; Zhang, Chen, et al. 2025), and large-scale parallel selection (Luo et al. 2015; Zhong et al. 2022; Zhong and Hong 2022; Hong et al. 2022).

The OCBA method was introduced by Chen (1996), and a book providing a comprehensive treatment is Chen and Lee (2011) see also Chen et al. (2000; Branke et al. 2007; Glynn and Juneja 2004; Pasupathy et al. 2014). Extensions and applications include Peng, Xu, et al. (2018) for multi-fidelity models; Du et al. (2024) for context-dependent problems; Wang et al. (2023) for two-stage SO; and Y. Li et al. (2021) for MCTS.

The formulation of the dynamic allocation and selection (A\(\&\)S) policy was introduced in Peng et al. (2016), with the approximate dynamic programming paradigm based on value function approximation for the Bellman equation proposed in Peng, Chong, et al. (2018).

An optimal A\(\&\)S policy for an integrated PCS over the parameter space can be found via a Bellman equation, described in Peng et al. (2016; Peng, Chong, et al. 2018).

Alternatives to PCS such as the probability of good selection are discussed in Eckman and Henderson (2021).

Thompson (1933) introduced Thompson sampling, and Russo (2020) proposed the Top-Two Thompson extension. Alternative sequential sampling procedures include the expected value of improvement (EVI) (Chick and Inoue 2001), knowledge gradient (KG) (Frazier et al. 2008), and expected improvement (EI) (Ryzhov 2016). KG and EI sequentially allocate samples to optimize myopic surrogate criteria.

Search methods for discrete stochastic optimization were not treated here but include techniques such as simulated annealing (Alrefaei and Andradóttir 1999), genetic algorithms, stochastic ruler methods (Yan and Mukai 1992), stochastic comparison (Gong et al. 2000), nested partitions (Shi and Olafsson 2007), ant colony optimization (Dorigo et al. 2006; Dorigo and Blum 2005), Tabu search (Glover and Hanafi 2002), COMPASS (Hong and Nelson 2006), the adaptive hyperbox algorithm for high-dimensional problems (Xu et al. 2013), and the R-SPLINE algorithm (Wang et al. 2013), some of which have proofs of global or local convergence (Hong and Nelson 2009).

Stochastic Approximation (SA) was introduced by Robbins and Monro (1951) to address noisy root-finding problems, and later applied to stochastic optimization by solving for the zero of the gradient when a “direct” gradient estimator is available. Kiefer and Wolfowitz (1952) extended SA to the setting without such an estimator by approximating the gradient using finite-difference methods, and Blum (1954) extended SA to the multi-dimensional setting.

Papers focused on the asymptotic convergence properties of RM, KW, and their variations include Derman (1956; Fabian 1967; Polyak and Juditsky 1992; Polyak and Tsybakov 1990). Asymptotic normality of the SA iterate was established in Fabian (1967). Finite-time theory and error bounds have been derived in Nemirovski et al. (2009a; Ghadimi and Lan 2012a).

Iterate averaging was introduced in Polyak and Juditsky (1992; Ruppert 1988); the robust SA algorithm in Nemirovski et al. (2009b), the accelerated SA algorithm in Ghadimi and Lan (2012b); and the adaptive projection SA algorithm (Andradóttir 1995).

Recommended books on SA include Kushner and Yin (2003; Borkar 2009).

The gradient estimator is key to the success of an SA algorithm used for optimization. The most readily available gradient estimate employs finite differences, requiring only performance estimates and no knowledge of the underlying system, such as in the context of black-box simulation. However, finite difference gradient estimates can be noisy if the measurements are highly volatile and become computationally expensive in high dimensions, as the cost grows linearly with the parameter dimension. Simultaneous Perturbation Stochastic Approximation (SPSA) (Spall 1992) requires only two estimates of the objective function to approximate the gradient, making the computational cost independent of the parameter space dimension. A proposed approach called Secant-Tangents AveRaged stochastic approximation (STAR-SA) employs a hybrid gradient estimator that combines direct gradient estimates with a symmetric finite difference gradient estimate (Chau et al. 2014).

Response surface methodology (RSM) was initially developed for experimental design in physical processes (Box and Wilson 1992). A comprehensive book is Myers et al. (2016). A review of its use for SO problems can be found in Barton and Meckesheimer (2006; Kleijnen 2018), including automating RSM and handling stochastic constraints in Neddermeijer et al. (2000; Nicolai and Dekker 2009).

Bayesian global optimization methods are described in Sacks et al. (1989; Williams and Rasmussen 2006).

The EGO algorithm was introduced by Jones et al. (1998); analogous methods for SO are detailed in Huang et al. (2006; Kleijnen 2009). Kriging for simulation metamodeling includes Van Beers and Kleijnen (2004; Kleijnen and Van Beers 2005; Kleijnen 2009).

A comprehensive book on trust region methods is Conn et al. (2000); its use in SO is described in Chang et al. (2013; Deng and Ferris 2006).

The main stochastic gradient estimation techniques are detailed in Chapter 5 of the simulation optimization handbook (Fu 2015), which also contains chapters on many other SO topics. Stochastic gradient estimation from an AI/ML perspective is reviewed in Mohamed et al. (2020). The GLR method was introduced in Peng, Fu, et al. (2018); see also Peng et al. (2025). Other recent developments in stochastic gradient estimation can be found in Ren and Fu (2024; X. Ren et al. 2025; Fu, Hu, Ryzhov, et al. 2025; Fu, Hu, and Scheinberg 2025).

Research on stochastic gradient estimation for quantiles, conditional value-at-risk, and distortion risk measures is relatively recent (Glynn et al. 2021; Heidergott and Volk-Makarewicz 2016; Hong 2009; Hong and Liu 2009; Jiang and Fu 2015; Hu et al. 2022) and often focuses on estimating the gradient of the distribution function (Peng, Fu, et al. 2018; Liu and Hong 2011; Li and Peng 2024, 2025). Stochastic gradient estimators for risk measures based on a single simulation run are generally biased, with much research attention on their asymptotic properties.

A monograph on MC and QMC is Lemieux (2009), and L’Ecuyer (2018) provides an introduction to RQMC; see also Niederreiter (1992). Application of conditional MC and RQMC to reduce the variance of stochastic gradient estimators is found in L’Ecuyer et al. (2022).

Variance reduction techniques are covered in most textbooks on stochastic simulation, including Law and Kelton (2000; Glasserman 2004; Asmussen and Glynn 2007; Rubinstein and Kroese 2016).

Methods focused on reducing variance specifically in SGD, leading to more stable convergence, include stochastic average gradient (SAG) and stochastic variance reduced gradient (SVRG) (Roux et al. 2012; J Reddi et al. 2015; Johnson and Zhang 2013). While SAG is limited to smooth and convex functions, SVRG enhances applicability by updating gradients every \(m\) iterations, making it effective for both convex and non-convex problems. Additional advances include SDCA, MISO, S2GD, and the SARAH algorithm, with SARAH+ and SARAH++ further improving these approaches by removing the need for careful parameter selection (Shalev-Shwartz and Zhang 2013; Konečný and Richtárik 2017; Nguyen et al. 2017). These variance reduction techniques achieve linear convergence rates in strongly convex settings, significantly improving optimization performance for tasks like regularized logistic regression (Nguyen et al. 2017). Although SVRG has shown superior performance in non-convex neural networks (Shang et al. 2018; Reddi et al. 2016), applying these methods to more complex and deeper neural networks remains a critical area for future research.

Finally, a valuable resource for the SO research community is the SimOpt testbed introduced by Pasupathy and Henderson (2006), found at https://simopt.org or https://github.com/simopt-admin/simopt; see also Pasupathy and Henderson (2011; Dong et al. 2017; Eckman et al. 2019), and the Winter Simulation Conference (WSC) program archives at https://informs-sim.org for regular updates and the most current research developments in the simulation field.
