本章从回归、分类讲到神经网络和强化学习。关键是分清：是在预测结果，还是在学习如何行动。

回归与分类
线性回归、逻辑回归、支持向量机和决策树是基础工具。
神经网络
通过多层变换学习复杂关系，训练时用梯度方法更新参数。
不同数据，不同结构
CNN 处理图像；RNN、Transformer 等处理序列信息。
强化学习
智能体通过状态、动作和奖励学习策略，Q-learning 是入门方法。

训练表现和实际效果要分开看：冻结策略后，用新的随机样本评估。

本章动手实验

学习库存补货策略通过 Q-learning 学习策略，并用独立需求序列评估。
展开完整原文（英文）

This chapter overviews major methodologies used in AI from the areas of statistical regression models and machine learning, beginning with linear regression and logistic regression, which form the basis for many predictive models. ML methods covered are support vector machines, decision trees, reinforcement learning, and artificial neural networks – convolutional neural networks used for computer vision tasks such as image recognition and object detection, followed by recurrent neural networks and Transformers used for natural language processing applications such as sentiment analysis and chatbots.

3.1 Linear Regression and Logistic Regression

Linear regression and logistic regression are two of the most widely-used statistical methods for modeling the relationship between a dependent variable (target) and one or more independent variables (features), the difference between the two being the function used to model the relationship. The two models can also be viewed as representative of two classes of regression models distinguished by the range of the dependent variable: unbounded versus bounded.

Linear regression aims to find the best-fitting linear model, although transformations enable significant extension of its applicability.

The simplest linear regression has one independent variable \(x\), with the relationship between \(x\) and the dependent variable \(y\) modeled as a straight line, i.e., \[\begin{equation*} y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \epsilon, \end{equation*}\] where \(y\) is the dependent variable, \(\beta_0\) is the y-intercept, \(\beta_1\) is the slope, \(x\) is the independent variable, and \(\epsilon\) is the error term (residuals).

When there are multiple independent variables, the model extends to multiple linear regression:

\[\begin{equation*} y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_1 + \beta_2 x_2 + \dots + \beta_p x_p + \epsilon, \end{equation*}\] where \(x_1, x_2, \dots, x_p\) are the independent variables \(x_i=(x_{i1},\dots,x_{ip})\) with corresponding coefficients \(\beta_1, \beta_2, \dots, \beta_p\),

estimated using the method of ordinary least squares (OLS), which minimizes the sum of the squared differences between the observed values \(y=(y_1,\dots,y_n)\) and the predicted values \(\hat{y}=(\hat{y}_1,\dots,\hat{y}_n)\) from the model: \[\begin{equation*} J(\beta) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( y_i - (\beta_0 + \beta_1 x_{i1} + \dots + \beta_p x_{ip}) \right)^2. \end{equation*}\]

Defining the vector of coefficients as \(\beta=(\beta_1,\dots,\beta_p)\) and the matrix of input features as \(X=(x_1,\dots,x_n)\), the solution to this minimization problem is given by \[\begin{equation*} \beta = (X^T X)^{-1} X^T y, \end{equation*}\] where \(T\) denotes the transpose operator.

Standard assumptions for linear regression are as follows:

  • Linearity: The relationship between the predictors and the outcome is linear.

  • Independence: Observations are independent of each other.

  • Homoscedasticity: The variance of the error terms is constant across all levels of the independent variables.

  • Normality: The error terms are normally distributed.

Common performance metrics for linear regression model fit include the coefficient of determination (denoted by \(R^2\)), which measures the proportion of variance in the dependent variable that is predictable from the independent variables (higher values indicating better fit), and root mean-squared error (RMSE):

\[\begin{equation*} R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \bar{y})^2}, \end{equation*}\]

\[\begin{equation*} RMSE = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}. \end{equation*}\]

Logistic regression is a statistical method that uses the logistic function (also known as the sigmoid function) for the input-output model: \[\begin{equation*} y = \sigma(\beta_0 + \beta_1 x_1 + \beta_2 x_2 + \dots + \beta_p x_p), \sigma(z) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-z}}, \end{equation*}\]

where \(\sigma(\cdot)\) denotes the sigmoid function. Since the range of the output is between 0 and 1, logistic regression can be used to model the probability of a binary outcome, i.e., \({\mathcal P}(y = 1 \mid x)\).

Thus, in AI/ML applications, logistic regression is used for binary classification problems, where the outcome is a categorical variable with two possible values (e.g., success/failure, yes/no, 0/1).

To predict the binary outcome, a threshold is typically set at 0.5. If the predicted probability is greater than or equal to 0.5, the model predicts one class; else, it predicts the other.

The coefficients in logistic regression are estimated using maximum likelihood estimation (MLE).

Standard assumptions for logistic regression are as follows:

  • Linearity of the Logit: The logit (log-odds) of the outcome is a linear combination of the predictor variables.

  • Independence: Observations are independent of each other.

  • Absence of Multicollinearity: Predictors should not be highly correlated with each other.

Logistic regression models are evaluated using metrics appropriate for classification tasks, such as accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and AUC-ROC, defined as follows:

  • Accuracy is the proportion of correctly predicted instances: \[\begin{equation*} \text{Accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}, \end{equation*}\] where \(TP\), \(TN\), \(FP\), and \(FN\) stand for true positives, true negatives, false positives, and false negatives, respectively.

  • Precision is the proportion of true positive predictions out of all positive predictions: \[\begin{equation*} \text{Precision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP}. \end{equation*}\]

  • Recall (Sensitivity) is the proportion of true positive predictions out of all actual positives: \[\begin{equation*} \text{Recall} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}. \end{equation*}\]

  • F1-Score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall: \[\begin{equation*} \text{F1-Score} = 2 \times \frac{\text{Precision} \times \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}. \end{equation*}\]

  • AUC-ROC is the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve, which plots the true positive rate against the false positive rate at various threshold settings. The AUC-ROC value ranges from 0 to 1, with a value closer to 1 indicating better model performance.

3.2 Support Vector Machines (SVMs) and Decision Trees

Support Vector Machines are a form of supervised learning used for both classification and regression tasks, although more widely used for the former. SVMs aim to find the hyperplane or set of hyperplanes that best separates the data points of different classes, most often in a high-dimensional space. Here we consider two classes, known as the binary classification problem, in which case the hyperplane maximizes the margin between the closest data point from each class, known as the support vector, where the margin is defined as the distance between the hyperplane and the support vectors. The larger the margin, the better the generalization and separation between classes. Figure 3.2 provides an illustrative example in two-dimensional space, where the optimal hyperplane (solid line) is centered between the two support vectors (dotted lines) that maximize this margin.

For a binary classification problem, the decision boundary is defined by the equation: \[\begin{equation*} w \cdot x + b = 0, \end{equation*}\] where \(w\) is the weight vector, \(x\) is the feature vector (with features X and Y in Figure 3.2), and \(b\) is the bias term.

If the data is linearly separable, a linear SVM can be found by solving the following optimization problem to find the hyperplane that maximizes the margin: \[\begin{equation*} \min_{w, b} \frac{1}{2} \|w\|^2 \end{equation*}\] subject to the constraints: \[\begin{equation*} y_i (w \cdot x_i + b) \geq 1, \end{equation*}\] where \(y_i \in \{ \pm 1\}\) is the class label of the \(i\)th data point with feature \(x_i\).

Real-world data is often not linearly separable. To handle nonlinearly separable data, SVM uses a technique called the “kernel trick,” which transforms the original feature space into a higher-dimensional space where the data becomes linearly separable. The selection of the kernel function is crucial to effectively capture the underlying relationships within the data.

The simplest kernel is the linear kernel given by \[\begin{equation*} K(x, x') = x \cdot x', \end{equation*}\]

which maps features directly into a higher-dimensional space without modifying their relationships, effectively finding an optimal hyperplane in the original feature space. This kernel is typically used when the data is linearly separable and has relatively low computational complexity, making it the best choice for simple linear problems.

When data exhibits nonlinear relationships, kernels can be used in SVM to capture complex interactions by transforming the input space. The polynomial kernel is defined as \[\begin{equation*} K(x, x') = (x \cdot x' + c)^d, \end{equation*}\] where \(d\) is the degree and \(c\) is an offset. This kernel is useful for capturing polynomial relationships between features and targets. The radial basis function (RBF) kernel, or Gaussian kernel, is widely used for its ability to map data into higher-dimensional space: \[\begin{equation*} K(x, x') = \exp(-\gamma \|x - x'\|^2), \end{equation*}\] where \(\gamma\) controls the width of the Gaussian and affects the model’s complexity; higher \(\gamma\) values create more complex decision boundaries. Lastly, the sigmoid kernel, similar to neural network activation, is given by \[\begin{equation*} K(x, x') = \tanh(\alpha x \cdot x' + c), \end{equation*}\] with \(\alpha\) and \(c\) controlling the slope and offset. The sigmoid kernel approximates nonlinear boundaries, functioning like a shallow neural network.

The choice of kernel function depends on the characteristics of the data and the complexity of the relationships that need to be modeled. For linearly separable data, the linear kernel provides a simple and effective solution. For problems where the relationship between features and target is more intricate, the polynomial kernel offers the flexibility to capture polynomial interactions, while the RBF kernel is ideal for capturing complex, highly nonlinear patterns. The sigmoid kernel, on the other hand, is effective at mimicking the behavior of neural networks.

In practice, it is often beneficial to experiment with multiple kernel functions and perform hyperparameter tuning to select the one that yields the best performance for a given dataset. Each kernel function brings its own strengths in capturing relationships, thus providing flexibility in addressing various ML problems effectively.

In cases where perfect separation is not achievable due to noise or overlapping data, the soft-margin SVM approach is used. This method allows some misclassification for a better balance between maximizing the margin and minimizing classification errors. The objective function for a soft-margin SVM is \[\begin{equation*} \min_{w, b, \xi} \frac{1}{2} \|w\|^2 + C \sum_{i=1}^{n} \xi_i \end{equation*}\] subject to \[\begin{equation*} y_i (w \cdot x_i + b) \geq 1 - \xi_i, \xi_i \geq 0, \end{equation*}\] where \(\xi_i\) are slack variables allowing misclassifications, and \(C\) is a regularization parameter controlling the trade-off between maximizing the margin and minimizing misclassification errors. The choice of \(C\) determines the model’s flexibility: a larger value of \(C\) aims for fewer misclassifications, while a smaller value allows a wider margin at the cost of some errors.

SVM models are evaluated using common classification metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and AUC-ROC. These metrics help assess the model’s performance and its ability to correctly classify new data points.

Decision trees are another widely utilized and intuitive method for both classification and regression tasks. A decision tree models the decision-making process by recursively partitioning the data into subsets based on feature values, ultimately forming a hierarchical, tree-like structure where each node represents a decision criterion. This recursive process continues until a stopping condition is met, such as reaching a predefined maximum depth or when further splitting does not significantly reduce impurity.

A decision tree is composed of three primary types of nodes:

  • root node – the topmost node, representing the entire dataset, from which the decision tree begins to partition the data.

  • internal nodes – nodes that represent decisions based on feature values, leading to subsequent splits; these nodes act as intermediate steps that guide the data down different branches of the tree based on specific criteria.

  • leaf nodes – terminal nodes that represent the final predicted output or class label; these nodes contain the majority class (for classification) or the average value (for regression) of the samples that reach them.

The construction of an effective decision tree relies on selecting the optimal feature to split the data at each node. This selection is driven by a criterion that evaluates the quality of a split. Common criteria for determining the best split include the following:

  • Gini Impurity measures the likelihood of incorrectly classifying a randomly chosen element if it were assigned a label according to the observed distribution within a subset: \[\begin{equation*} Gini = 1 - \sum_{i=1}^{c} p_i^2, \end{equation*}\] where \(p_i\) represents the probability of selecting an element from class \(i\). A lower Gini Impurity value indicates a purer node, which means that most of the data points belong to a single class.

  • Entropy quantifies the degree of disorder or impurity within the dataset: \[\begin{equation*} Entropy = -\sum_{i=1}^{c} p_i \log_2(p_i). \end{equation*}\] Entropy is particularly useful for capturing uncertainty in the system, where higher entropy indicates greater impurity, and lower entropy suggests a more homogeneous subset. In information theory, entropy is a measure of the information content, and it helps to determine the uncertainty reduction when splitting a node.

  • Information Gain is derived from entropy and measures the reduction in entropy after splitting the dataset based on a particular feature: \[\begin{equation*} Information ~ Gain = Entropy(parent) - \sum_{i=1}^{k} \frac{n_i}{n} Entropy(child_i), \end{equation*}\] where \(n_i\) represents the number of samples in the \(i\)th child node, and \(n\) is the total number of samples. The feature with the highest information gain is selected for the split, as it yields the greatest reduction in impurity, thereby helping to create purer nodes.

The structure of a decision tree is depicted in Figure 3.3, where each decision node represents a condition or test on a feature (e.g., ‘Age >30’). Branches stemming from these nodes represent the possible outcomes of each condition, directing the flow based on whether the condition is met or not. The end points, or leaf nodes, indicate the final predicted outcome or class label (e.g., ‘Yes’ or ‘No’ for classification tasks). This hierarchical structure enables users to trace the sequence of conditions that lead to a specific prediction, providing a clear step-by-step interpretation of how the model reaches its decisions. This interpretability is particularly useful for identifying influential features and understanding the model’s logic.

The process of constructing a decision tree can be framed as an optimization problem, where the objective is to minimize the impurity of the resulting child nodes after each split. For a binary split, the resulting child nodes ideally exhibit high purity, meaning they primarily contain instances of a single class. Let \(D\) represent the dataset at a particular node, and let \(D_1\) and \(D_2\) represent the resulting child nodes after a split. The goal is to determine the feature and splitting value that minimize the weighted average impurity of \(D_1\) and \(D_2\). This can be represented as \[\begin{equation*} \text{Weighted Impurity} = \frac{|D_1|}{|D|} \times Impurity(D_1) + \frac{|D_2|}{|D|} \times Impurity(D_2), \end{equation*}\] where \(|D_1|\) and \(|D_2|\) are the sizes of the child nodes, and \(Impurity(D_1)\) and \(Impurity(D_2)\) are the impurity measures (such as Gini or entropy) of the respective nodes.

One major challenge with decision trees is their propensity to overfit the training data, resulting in poor generalization to unseen data. Overfitting occurs when the tree captures noise or idiosyncrasies in the training set rather than the underlying patterns. To mitigate overfitting, pruning techniques are employed:

1. Pre-pruning (Early Stopping): This approach halts the growth of the tree early based on criteria such as maximum tree depth, minimum samples per leaf, or minimum information gain. Pre-pruning restricts the model’s complexity and helps prevent overfitting by avoiding deep branches that capture noise.

2. Post-pruning: In this approach, branches are removed from a fully grown tree based on their contribution to model performance on a validation set. Post-pruning evaluates each branch and removes those that do not significantly enhance predictive accuracy. This ensures that only meaningful branches are retained, reducing the risk of overfitting while maintaining model accuracy.

3. Cost Complexity Pruning is another widely used pruning method in which a complexity parameter, \(\alpha\), is introduced to penalize the complexity of the tree. The objective is to minimize the following cost function: \[\begin{equation*} C(T) = R(T) + \alpha \cdot |T|, \end{equation*}\] where \(R(T)\) denotes the empirical risk (i.e., training error) of the tree \(T\), and \(|T|\) represents the number of leaf nodes. By adjusting \(\alpha\), we can balance the trade-off between model complexity and training error, leading to a simpler and more generalizable tree. The choice of \(\alpha\) plays a critical role in determining the optimal tree size that minimizes overfitting while retaining sufficient predictive power.

Decision trees are evaluated using metrics common to other classification or regression models, such as accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, and RMSE. One of the main advantages of decision trees is their interpretability; the decision-making process can be easily visualized and understood, providing clear insight into how individual predictions are derived. The ability to trace the path from the root node to a specific leaf node is particularly advantageous in domains that require transparency and justifiability of decisions, such as finance, healthcare, and legal applications.

Beyond their standalone use, decision trees are foundational components in ensemble learning methods such as random forests and gradient boosting machines (GBMs). These ensemble methods combine multiple decision trees to achieve improved predictive performance and reduced overfitting. In random forests, multiple decision trees are trained on bootstrapped samples of the dataset, and their predictions are aggregated (typically using majority voting for classification or averaging for regression) to form a final prediction, thereby increasing robustness and reducing variance. In GBMs, trees are added sequentially, with each tree attempting to correct the errors made by the previous ensemble, leading to a powerful, highly accurate model that is well-suited for a wide range of complex predictive tasks.

An important aspect of decision tree construction is feature selection and its impact on model performance. Features that provide the highest information gain or the greatest reduction in impurity are prioritized during the splitting process. However, in real-world datasets with many irrelevant or redundant features, feature selection methods can be used before training to reduce dimensionality and improve the efficiency of the decision tree model.

3.3 Artificial Neural Networks

Artificial neural networks form the backbone of deep learning, and their basic architecture was inspired by the structure and function of the human brain.

The multilayer perceptron (MLP), a basic form of (deep) feedforward ANN, comprises multiple layers of nodes called neurons, where each neuron is connected to all neurons in the next layer. These connections are often represented as weights, which are the parameters of the model. The general structure of an MLP consists of three types of layers: an input layer, hidden layers, and an output layer. Let \(\tau \geq 3\) denote the total number of layers (including the input and output layers and at least one hidden layer) in the MLP. A deep neural network simply means that \(\tau\) is relatively large (initially just four, i.e., at least two hidden layers, but now significantly more than that). Figure 3.4 illustrates an MLP for \(\tau=4\). The input layer contains neurons that represent features of the dataset. These inputs are passed to the hidden layers, which contain multiple neurons that process the inputs using weights, biases, and activation functions. Bias neurons are added in the hidden layers to adjust the output. The hidden layers are responsible for learning complex patterns in the data. The final layer is the output layer, which produces the network’s predictions or classifications.

Mathematically, an MLP is a composition of functions of weighted sums of inputs propagated as outputs from one layer of neurons to the next layer. The components of an individual neuron are depicted in Figure 3.5, for input vector \(x^{(t)}(n):= \{ x_0^{(t)}(n),x_1^{(t)}(n),\ldots, x_{m_t}^{(t)}(n) \}\) producing output \(x^{(t+1)}(n)\) of the \(i\)th neuron at layer \(t+1\), capturing the following equations (for data point \(n = 1,\ldots,N,\) for a dataset of size \(N\)): \[\begin{eqnarray} x_i^{(t+1)}(n) &=& \varphi\big(v_i^{(t)}(n)\big), \\ v_i^{(t)}(n) &=& \sum_{j=0}^{m_t} \theta_{ij}^{(t)} x_j^{(t)}(n), \end{eqnarray}\]

where \(m_t+1\) is the number of neurons in the \(t\)-th layer (\(t=1,\ldots,\tau\)), \(\varphi\) is the activation function, and \(v_i^{(t)}(n)\) is the weighted input signal to neuron \(i\) at layer \(t\), with \(\theta_{ij}^{(t)}\) the synaptic weights that are the parameters to be tuned based on the dataset.

Common choices of activation functions, which introduce nonlinearity into the model, are as follows:

  • sigmoid \(\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}}\);

  • rectified linear unit ReLU(\(x\)) = \(x^+ := \max(0, x)\);

  • hyperbolic tangent \(\tanh(x) = \frac{e^{x} - e^{-x}}{e^{x} + e^{-x}}.\)

Gradient Estimation

Training a neural network usually means adjusting the synaptic weights to minimize a loss function \(L(x,y)\) quantifying the difference between the predicted output \(x\) and actual target output \(y\). For an MLP, this is carried out using a gradient-based search such as stochastic gradient descent (SGD), typically involving the backpropagation (BP) algorithm for computing the gradient of \(L\) with respect to each weight \(\theta_{ij}\).

The BP calculation consists of a forward pass that propagates input data through the network, computing the predicted output and storing intermediate activations for the backward pass, which applies the chain rule to compute gradients layer-by-layer from output back to input, assuming differentiability of both the activation function \(\varphi\) and loss function \(L\).

Denoting the output observation from the \(n\)th data point as \(O(n)\), which has \(x^{(\tau)}(n)\) as the corresponding output prediction from the \(n\)th data point, the BP algorithm uses (3.1) and (3.2) to derive the gradient estimate \(\nabla_{\theta} L\) \[\frac{\partial L(x^{(\tau)}(n), O(n))}{\partial \theta_{i,j}^{(t)}} = \delta_{i}^{(t)}(n) x_{j}^{(t)}(n),\] where \[\delta_{i}^{(t)}(n) = \begin{cases} \left. \frac{\partial L(x,O(n))}{\partial x} \right|_{x=x_i^{(t)}(n)} \varphi'(v_i^{(t)}(n)) & \text{if } t = \tau, \\[8pt] \varphi'(v_i^{(t)}(n)) \sum_{j=1}^{m_{t+1}} \delta_{j}^{(t+1)}(n) & \text{if } t < \tau. \end{cases}\]

The validity of the BP gradient estimate relies on the activation function \(\varphi\) and the loss function \(L\) being well behaved, mainly in terms of continuity and differentiability. The BP algorithm can be viewed as an application of automatic differentiation and as a special case of the stochastic gradient estimation technique of IPA introduced in Section 2.5. An approach that avoids the backward pass is introduced in the next chapter, in Section 4.2, based on a version of the LR stochastic gradient estimation also introduced in Section 2.5.

Optimization

After computing the gradients, the ANN’s weights are updated using a stochastic approximation algorithm (cf. Section 2.3), i.e., SGD or one of its variants. Traditional gradient descent calculates the gradient over the entire dataset before updating the model parameters, which can be computationally expensive and slow for large datasets. To alleviate this issue, SGD updates the model parameters by computing the gradient on small mini-batches of data taken from the much larger dataset. The gradient is stochastic, because the mini-batches are generally chosen by random sampling (akin to bootstrapping or sampling from the empirical distribution, although the goal is reversed – using a smaller sample size rather than generating a larger one).

For training ANNs, the concept of momentum has been found to be practically useful in improving the stability of SGD and accelerating convergence. Momentum accumulates gradients in the direction of the steepest descent, allowing the optimization search algorithm to build velocity. Momentum is realized using a velocity term \(V_n\), updated as follows (along with the corresponding parameter update): \[\begin{eqnarray*} V_n &=& \gamma V_{n-1} + \eta \nabla_{\theta} L(\theta_{n}), \\ \theta_{n+1} &=& \theta_{n} - V_n, \end{eqnarray*}\] where \(\gamma\) is the momentum coefficient (typically between 0.9 and 0.99), and \(\eta\) is the learning rate. The velocity term helps the algorithm accelerate in the direction of consistent gradients, while also damping oscillations in regions where gradients change directions.

It can also improve the optimization process in traversing flat regions of the loss landscape more quickly and avoid excessive fluctuations in steep, narrow valleys, so that momentum-based gradient descent is particularly effective in deep networks, where gradients can sometimes be inconsistent or change directions rapidly between layers.

One commonly used method is RMSProp, which was developed to deal with the problem of large gradients and oscillations. RMSProp scales the learning rate by the root mean square of the gradients, allowing it to handle nonstationary objectives more effectively. The update rules for RMSProp are \[\begin{eqnarray*} V_n &=& \beta V_{n-1} + (1 - \beta) (\nabla_{\theta} L(\theta_{n}))^2, \\ \theta_{n+1} &=& \theta_{n} - \frac{\eta}{\sqrt{V_n} + \epsilon} \nabla_{\theta} L(\theta_{n}), \end{eqnarray*}\] where \(V_n\) is the exponentially decaying average of the squared gradients, \(\epsilon\) is a small number, and \(\beta\) is typically set to 0.9. This method helps to mitigate large gradient updates and prevents the learning process from overshooting the optimal solution.

Another widely used optimization method is Adam (adaptive moment estimation), which combines the advantages of momentum and adaptive learning rates, making it robust and efficient for a wide range of DL tasks. Instead of using a single learning rate for all parameters, Adam computes individual adaptive learning rates for each parameter based on estimates of the first and second moments of the gradient.

The Adam update rule is \[\begin{eqnarray*} m_n &=& \beta_1 m_{n-1} + (1 - \beta_1) \nabla_{\theta} L(\theta_{n}), ~~~\hat{m}_n = \frac{m_n}{1 - \beta_1^n}, \\ v_n &=& \beta_2 v_{n-1} + (1 - \beta_2) (\nabla_{\theta} L(\theta_{n}))^2, ~~~\hat{v}_n = \frac{v_n}{1 - \beta_2^n}, \\ \theta_{n+1} &=& \theta_n - \eta \frac{\hat{m}_n}{\sqrt{\hat{v}_n} + \epsilon}, \end{eqnarray*}\] where \(m_n\) represents the exponentially moving average of the gradients (first moment), and \(v_n\) represents the exponentially moving average of the squared gradients (second moment). The variables \(\beta_1\) and \(\beta_2\) are decay rates, typically set to 0.9 and 0.999, respectively. The term \(\hat{m}_t\) is the bias-corrected estimate of the first moment, and \(\hat{v}_t\) is the bias-corrected estimate of the second moment.

Adam’s key strength lies in its ability to adapt the learning rate for each parameter individually, based on the estimated gradient statistics. This makes it particularly effective for dealing with sparse gradients and noisy datasets, as the algorithm can adjust the step size accordingly. Additionally, Adam has built-in bias correction, which helps ensure that the estimates for the moments are unbiased, especially during the early stages of training. Adam has largely replaced RMSProp in many applications, but RMSProp remains a strong choice for recurrent neural networks and other specialized DL models.

Initialization, Regularization and Normalization

However, even with advanced gradient descent variants, the performance of deep networks can still be significantly influenced by the initial values of the weights. This brings us to another major challenge in DL optimization: vanishing gradients and exploding gradients, which are closely tied to how weights are initialized before training begins.

Vanishing Gradients: This issue arises when gradients become very small as they propagate back through the network during BP, especially in deep networks with many layers. Activation functions like sigmoid and tanh, which compress their inputs into a small range, can exacerbate this issue. As a result, the gradients of earlier layers become close to zero, leading to extremely slow weight updates. For the sigmoid activation function \[\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}},\] the derivative \(\sigma'(x) = \sigma(x)(1 - \sigma(x))\) is always between 0 and 0.25, meaning that as the backpropagated gradients move through the layers, they become progressively smaller, particularly when the network is deep.

Exploding Gradients: The opposite of vanishing gradients occurs when gradients grow excessively large as they are propagated backward, destabilizing the training process. This can result in very large updates to the weights, causing the model to diverge. This issue is particularly common in deep networks where small errors at the output layer result in disproportionately large weight updates in the earlier layers.

Both vanishing and exploding gradients significantly hinder the training process, especially in deep architectures. To prevent such problems, various strategies have been developed, one of which is weight initialization, which aims to keep the gradients within a manageable range throughout the network. These strategies maintain a balance in the flow of gradients during forward and backward propagation, preventing them from either vanishing or exploding.

A commonly used method is the Xavier initialization (also called Glorot initialization), which is designed to ensure that the variance of the activations is the same across every layer, thus preventing the gradients from shrinking or growing as they propagate through the network.

This initialization works well for activation functions such as the sigmoid and tanh functions, ensuring that the variance of the activations does not diminish or explode as they pass through each layer. By initializing the weights based on the number of input and output units, Xavier initialization helps to maintain the flow of gradients, reducing the likelihood of vanishing or exploding gradients.

However, Xavier initialization may not be optimal for ReLU activations, which have different properties from the sigmoid and tanh functions. ReLU activations introduce sparsity by setting all negative activations to zero, which can lead to a loss of variance in the gradients as they propagate through the network, since the gradients are also zero for negative activations. To address this shortcoming, He initialization was developed for ReLU-based networks, ensuring that the variance of the activations remains consistent across layers, even when ReLU is applied. Because ReLU can introduce zeros into the gradients, it’s crucial to initialize the weights in such a way that the variance of the activations doesn’t diminish across layers. He initialization ensures that the activation variance is kept in check, preventing vanishing gradients even in deep networks.

Another extension of these strategies involves LeCun initialization, which is often used for Leaky ReLU and Selu activations, particularly in self-normalizing networks. This initialization is designed to match the variance of the weights to the particular characteristics of self-normalizing networks, ensuring the gradients remain in a manageable range.

Regularization techniques play a crucial role in preventing overfitting by introducing constraints or modifications to the learning process. Overfitting occurs when a model captures not only the underlying patterns in the data but also the noise, leading to poor generalization on unseen data. Regularization aims to create models that are more robust and generalizable.

One of the simplest and most effective regularization methods involves adding a penalty term to the loss function, which discourages large weights. By penalizing large weights, the model is forced to learn simpler representations, which are less likely to overfit.

\(\mathbb L^2\) regularization (also known as ridge regularization) adds a penalty proportional to the sum of the squared weights. The total loss function becomes \[\mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \mathcal{L} + \frac{\lambda}{2} \|\mathbf{W}\|_2^2,\] where \(\lambda\) is the regularization strength, which controls the trade-off between minimizing the original loss and keeping the weights small. \(\mathbb L^2\) regularization encourages the weights to stay close to zero without completely eliminating them, helping the model learn smoother decision boundaries and reducing overfitting.

In contrast, \(\mathbb L^1\) regularization (also known as Lasso regularization) adds a penalty proportional to the absolute values of the weights: \[\mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \mathcal{L} + \lambda \|\mathbf{W}\|_1,\] \(\mathbb L^1\) regularization encourages sparsity in the weights, meaning that many weights are driven to exactly zero. This can effectively perform feature selection by pruning unnecessary connections in the network.

These two techniques can be combined into elastic net regularization, where both \(\mathbb L^1\) and \(\mathbb L^2\) penalties are applied: \[\mathcal{L}_{\text{total}} = \mathcal{L} + \lambda_1 \|\mathbf{W}\|_1 + \lambda_2 \|\mathbf{W}\|_2^2,\] which allows the model to benefit from both the sparsity of \(\mathbb L^1\) and the weight shrinkage of \(\mathbb L^2\).

Another effective regularization method is dropout, which prevents overfitting by randomly dropping out a subset of neurons during training. For each training iteration, certain neurons are temporarily removed from the network along with their connections. This forces the network to not rely too heavily on specific neurons, encouraging redundancy and more robust feature learning.

During testing, dropout is turned off, and the activations are scaled to ensure the same expected output as during training, so that the total signal remains consistent between training and testing phases.

A simple yet powerful method is early stopping. During training, the model’s performance is monitored on a validation set. If the validation error starts to increase after a certain number of iterations, indicating overfitting, early stopping halts the training process at this point.

Another powerful technique is batch normalization, which normalizes the inputs of each layer to maintain consistent activation distributions during training. This method addresses the issue of internal covariate shift, where the distribution of activations changes as the network learns. By normalizing the inputs, batch normalization stabilizes the learning process and allows for higher learning rates, accelerating convergence.

For a given layer, the normalized activation is computed as \[\hat{x} = \frac{x - \mu_B}{\sqrt{\sigma_B^2 + \epsilon}},\] where \(\mu_B\) and \(\sigma_B^2\) are the mean and variance of the mini-batch, and \(\epsilon\) is a small constant added for numerical stability. Batch normalization also introduces learnable scale \(\gamma\) and shift \(\beta\) parameters: \[y = \gamma \hat{x} + \beta.\] These parameters allow the network to learn the appropriate scale and mean for the normalized inputs, making the model more flexible. Moreover, batch normalization can reduce the need for other regularization techniques like dropout, especially in very deep networks, where it helps to reduce the internal covariate shift.

Finally, another effective approach to prevent overfitting is data augmentation, particularly in tasks such as image classification. Data augmentation artificially expands the training set by applying transformations like cropping, flipping, rotation, and color jittering to the input data. These transformations create new, slightly altered training examples that help the model generalize better to unseen data.

Computer Vision

Computer vision is one of the most rapidly advancing fields in AI, driven by applications such as autonomous vehicles, medical imaging, surveillance, and augmented reality. The aim is to enable machines to interpret and make decisions based on visual data, much like humans do. The development of powerful DL models has significantly boosted the performance of computer vision systems, making tasks such as object detection, image segmentation, and facial recognition more accurate and efficient than ever before.

Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) are specialized deep neural networks designed to process data with grid-like topologies, such as images. Their architecture is inspired by the visual cortex and is particularly adept at automatically learning hierarchical representations, revolutionizing tasks such as image classification, object detection, and segmentation. The architecture of a CNN comprises the following types of layers, each playing a distinct role in the feature extraction process, as illustrated in Figure 3.9.

  1. Convolutional Layers: At the heart of a CNN, the convolutional layer applies numerous filters to the input to create feature maps. These filters detect spatial hierarchies of features by sliding across the input image, computing dot products, and producing maps that emphasize features like edges or textures.

  2. Pooling Layers: Following the convolutional layer, a pooling layer is typically used to reduce the spatial dimensions of the feature maps, thus reducing the number of parameters and computation in the network.

  3. Fully Connected Layers: Towards the end of the network, fully connected layers combine all features from the previous layers to classify the image into labels. The output from the final fully connected layer is often passed through a softmax function to classify the inputs into probabilistic outputs for each class.

  4. ReLU Activation Function: After each convolutional and fully connected layer, a ReLU function is applied to introduce nonlinearity, enhancing the network’s ability to learn complex patterns.

While CNNs have dramatically improved the field of computer vision, they come with challenges such as high data requirements, significant computational resources, and susceptibility to adversarial attacks. Future research in CNNs and related architectures such as Stable Diffusion models continues to focus on making these networks more efficient, interpretable, and robust against attacks, ensuring their applicability in a wider range of real-world scenarios.

CNNs have become the cornerstone of modern computer vision, enabling breakthroughs in tasks such as image recognition, object detection, and segmentation. Each of these tasks involves understanding visual information at different levels of complexity, and CNNs are well-suited for learning these hierarchical patterns from data.

In image recognition, the goal is to assign a single label to an entire image, which is typically formulated as a multi-class classification problem. The task involves predicting a probability distribution over a fixed set of categories. Given an input image \(I\), the CNN processes it through a series of convolutional and pooling layers to extract relevant features, which are then passed to fully connected layers for classification. The final output is a probability distribution over \(C\) classes, computed using the softmax function. To train the model, a loss function such as cross-entropy is minimized. This task serves as the foundation for many computer vision applications, and CNNs excel at automatically learning the hierarchical features necessary for accurate classification.

In contrast, object detection goes beyond recognizing the entire image by identifying and localizing multiple objects within the image. The task not only involves classifying each object but also predicting its location in the form of bounding boxes. Object detection models output both class labels and the coordinates of bounding boxes surrounding the objects. The output of a CNN-based object detection model includes a set of bounding boxes. In addition to the bounding box coordinates, the model also predicts class probabilities for each box. A commonly used approach is the region-based CNN (R-CNN) family, where regions of interest (RoIs) are first proposed and then classified into object categories. For each region, the CNN extracts features and predicts the class probabilities and bounding box offsets: \[\Delta B = ( \Delta x, \Delta y, \Delta w, \Delta h ).\] The loss function in object detection typically combines two components: a classification loss (e.g., cross-entropy) and a localization loss (e.g., smooth \(\mathbb L^1\) loss) to predict accurate bounding box coordinates: \[\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_{\text{cls}} + \lambda \mathcal{L}_{\text{loc}},\] where \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{cls}}\) is the classification loss, \(\mathcal{L}_{\text{loc}}\) is the localization loss, and \(\lambda\) is a balancing factor. Popular object detection models include Faster R-CNN, YOLO (You Only Look Once), and SSD (Single Shot Multibox Detector), which differ in their speed and accuracy trade-offs but are all based on CNN architectures.

Finally, image segmentation is a more fine-grained task that goes beyond detection and recognition by assigning a label to each pixel in the image. There are two main types of segmentation: semantic segmentation and instance segmentation. Semantic segmentation assigns a label to every pixel, grouping pixels that belong to the same object or category. This results in a pixel-wise classification where each pixel is associated with a class label. Instance segmentation extends semantic segmentation by differentiating between multiple objects of the same category, assigning unique labels to individual object instances.

The goal of segmentation is to learn a mapping from an input image to a segmentation mask. The CNN-based segmentation network typically consists of two stages: an encoder and a decoder. The encoder is similar to a CNN used for image recognition, where convolutional layers progressively reduce the spatial resolution while capturing higher-level features. The decoder upsamples the feature maps to reconstruct the original spatial resolution, allowing the network to predict pixel-level labels.

One of the most prominent architectures for image segmentation is the U-Net model, which consists of an encoder-decoder structure with skip connections to retain fine-grained spatial information. The output is a probability map, where each pixel is assigned a probability for each class using a softmax function. The loss function for segmentation is typically a pixel-wise cross-entropy loss, which sums over all pixels in the image.

3.3.1 Natural Language Processing (NLP)

Natural language processing is a branch of AI focused on understanding, interpreting, and generating human language. It combines computational linguistics, ML, and DL techniques to process text and speech data. NLP applications include machine translation, sentiment analysis, chatbots, and text summarization, aiming to bridge the gap between human communication and computer understanding.

NLP involves building models that can understand, generate, and manipulate human language. Traditional ML approaches to NLP often required extensive feature engineering and domain knowledge. However, the rise of DL, particularly models such as recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and Transformer architectures, has significantly improved the performance on a wide range of NLP tasks by automatically learning features from raw data.

Recurrent Neural Networks

RNNs are a class of neural networks designed to handle sequential data by maintaining a hidden state that is updated at each time step. This hidden state allows RNNs to capture temporal dependencies in the data, making them particularly well-suited for tasks involving sequences, such as language modeling, machine translation, and speech recognition.

The architecture of an RNN differs from feedforward networks in that the connections between neurons form directed cycles. At each time step \(t\), the RNN takes an input \(x_t\), updates the hidden state \(h_t\), and outputs a prediction \(\hat{y}_t\). This process can be described by the following equations: \[h_t = f(W_h h_{t-1} + W_x x_t + b_h) , \hat{y}_t = g(W_y h_t + b_y),\] where \(h_t\) represents the hidden state at time step \(t\), \(W_h\) is the weight matrix for the hidden state, \(W_x\) is the weight matrix for the input, and \(W_y\) is the weight matrix for the output. The functions \(f\) and \(g\) are usually nonlinear activation functions such as Tanh or ReLU. The recursive nature of RNNs allows them to maintain context over time, which is essential for understanding sequences of words or characters in NLP tasks.

As shown in Figure 3.11, an RNN can be unfolded over time, allowing it to process sequences of inputs \(X = (x_{t-1}, x_t, x_{t+1}, \dots)\) and update the hidden state \(h_t\) at each time step. The hidden state \(h_t\) carries the memory of previous inputs, enabling the model to capture dependencies across time steps.

Despite their ability to handle sequential data, RNNs face significant challenges when dealing with long sequences due to the problem of vanishing and exploding gradients. As the gradients are backpropagated through time, they may either shrink or grow exponentially, making it difficult for the model to learn long-term dependencies.

To address these issues, more advanced variants of RNNs, such as long short-term memory (LSTM) networks and gated recurrent units (GRUs), have been developed. LSTMs introduce a gating mechanism that controls the flow of information through the network, allowing it to retain relevant information over longer time periods. The LSTM architecture can be described by the following equations:

\[f_t = \sigma(W_f [h_{t-1}, x_t] + b_f) \quad \text{(forget gate)}\] \[i_t = \sigma(W_i [h_{t-1}, x_t] + b_i) \quad \text{(input gate)}\] \[\tilde{C}_t = \tanh(W_C [h_{t-1}, x_t] + b_C) \quad \text{(cell state candidate)}\] \[C_t = f_t \odot C_{t-1} + i_t \odot \tilde{C}_t \quad \text{(cell state update)}\] \[o_t = \sigma(W_o [h_{t-1}, x_t] + b_o) \quad \text{(output gate)}\] \[h_t = o_t \odot \tanh(C_t) \quad \text{(hidden state update)},\] where \(f_t\), \(i_t\), and \(o_t\) represent the forget, input, and output gates, respectively. The cell state \(C_t\) stores long-term memory, while the hidden state \(h_t\) carries the current step’s information. The gates control what information is added, removed, or retained from the cell state, which enables LSTMs to overcome the limitations of standard RNNs in long-term dependency learning.

While RNNs and their variants excel at processing sequences, they suffer from limitations in parallelization and long-term dependencies. The Transformer architecture was developed to address these shortcomings. The Transformer architecture has since become the dominant model for many NLP tasks, including machine translation, text summarization, and question answering.

Transformers

The Transformer relies entirely on self-attention mechanisms to model dependencies between tokens, allowing it to process all tokens in a sequence simultaneously rather than step-by-step as in RNNs. This parallelization makes Transformers more computationally efficient and capable of handling much longer sequences.

The self-attention mechanism computes a weighted sum of the values from the input sequence based on learned attention weights. The input to the self-attention mechanism consists of three components: the query, the key, and the value. The attention weights are computed as \[\text{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right) V,\] where \(Q\), \(K\), and \(V\) are matrices representing the query, key, and value vectors for each token in the input sequence, and \(d_k\) is the dimensionality of the key vectors. The dot products of vectors in \(Q\) and \(K\) give a similarity score between the tokens, and the softmax function ensures that the weights sum to 1. These weights are then applied to the value vectors \(V\) to produce the final output of the attention layer.

As shown in Figure 3.16, the Transformer model consists of an encoder and a decoder, both of which are built using layers of multi-head self-attention and feedforward neural networks. Multi-head attention allows the model to focus on different parts of the sequence simultaneously, capturing various aspects of the input. Each attention head computes self-attention independently, and the results are concatenated and linearly transformed. The process can be mathematically defined as \[\text{MultiHead}(Q, K, V) = \text{Concat}(\text{head}_1, \dots, \text{head}_h) W^O,\] where each attention head is computed as \[\text{head}_i = \text{Attention}(Q W_i^Q, K W_i^K, V W_i^V),\] and \(W_i^Q\), \(W_i^K\), \(W_i^V\) are learned projection matrices for the queries, keys, and values for head \(i\). The outputs of all heads are concatenated and projected to the output space using the matrix \(W^O\).

Unlike RNNs, which process data sequentially, Transformers can attend to all positions in the input sequence simultaneously, which makes them highly effective for capturing long-range dependencies without the vanishing gradient problems that can plague RNNs.

The encoder and decoder blocks in the Transformer are composed of multiple layers, each containing a multi-head self-attention mechanism followed by a position-wise feedforward neural network. Additionally, residual connections and layer normalization are applied after each sub-layer to stabilize and improve training. This can be expressed as \[\text{Output}_{\text{Layer}} = \text{LayerNorm}(X + \text{SubLayer}(X)),\] where \(X\) is the input to the sub-layer, and \(\text{SubLayer}(X)\) can be either a multi-head self-attention or a feedforward layer.

The feedforward network consists of two linear transformations with a ReLU activation in between: \[\text{FFN}(x) = \text{max}(0, xW_1 + b_1)W_2 + b_2,\] where \(W_1\), \(W_2\), \(b_1\), and \(b_2\) are learned parameters.

The decoder incorporates an additional cross-attention mechanism to attend to the encoder’s output, enabling the model to generate predictions based on both the input sequence and the previously generated tokens. The Transformer architecture has been extended to create models like BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) and GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer), which are pre-trained on large text corpora and fine-tuned for specific tasks. These models have achieved state-of-the-art performance on a wide range of NLP benchmarks.

NLP is a broad field that encompasses various applications aimed at enabling machines to understand, interpret, and generate human language. Among the most important NLP tasks are sentiment analysis, chatbot systems, and the development of powerful language models. These applications rely on DL architectures to model complex linguistic patterns and interactions.

Sentiment analysis, also known as opinion mining, is a task in NLP that aims to determine the emotional tone behind a piece of text. It is often used to identify the sentiment (positive, negative, or neutral) expressed in social media posts, reviews, or any form of text data. The main challenge in sentiment analysis lies in understanding the subtle nuances of language, such as sarcasm, context, and negations.

The process typically starts by encoding text data into numerical representations using methods such as word embeddings (e.g., Word2Vec, GloVe) or more recent techniques like BERT. These embeddings capture semantic relationships between words and are fed into models such as RNNs, LSTM networks, or Transformers to model the sequential nature of the text.

Given an input text, an LSTM or Transformer processes this sequence to generate a hidden representation of the entire text. The final layer of the model outputs a probability distribution over the sentiment classes (positive, negative, neutral) using a softmax function. The model is trained to minimize the cross-entropy loss. The rise of pre-trained language models like BERT and GPT has significantly improved the accuracy of sentiment analysis by capturing contextual information and long-range dependencies in the text.

Chatbots are AI-driven systems designed to simulate human conversation. They are widely used in customer service, personal assistants, and interactive applications. The goal of a chatbot is to understand user queries and provide appropriate responses, often in real-time. Chatbots typically employ a combination of natural language understanding (NLU) and natural language generation (NLG). NLU enables the chatbot to interpret the user’s input by identifying the intent (what the user wants) and extracting entities (important information such as dates, names). NLG generates a coherent response based on the user’s query and the system’s underlying logic.

Modern chatbots are built using DL models, particularly sequence-to-sequence (Seq2Seq) architectures, which map input sequences to output sequences. A common approach is to use an encoder-decoder architecture, often built with RNNs or Transformers. Given a user input, the encoder maps the input to a hidden representation. The decoder then generates a response by predicting one token at a time. The generation of each token \(y_t\) is conditioned on the previously generated token \(y_{t-1}\) and the context provided by the encoder’s hidden states \(h_t\).

In more advanced chatbots, attention mechanisms are incorporated into the model, allowing the decoder to focus on specific parts of the input sequence when generating a response. This is particularly useful for handling longer conversations or context-dependent queries. Pre-trained models such as GPT-4o have further revolutionized chatbot systems by enabling more fluid, human-like conversations. These models are fine-tuned on conversational datasets and can generate contextually relevant responses without requiring large amounts of task-specific data.

Language models are fundamental to many NLP tasks, including machine translation, text generation, and speech recognition. A language model assigns probabilities to sequences of words, helping to predict the likelihood of a given word or sequence based on its context. Traditional \(n\)-gram models estimate the probability of a word based on the previous \(n-1\) words. However, these models suffer from sparsity issues and cannot capture long-range dependencies. DL-based language models, particularly RNNs, LSTMs, and Transformers, overcome these limitations by learning contextual representations of words.

The objective of a language model is to maximize the likelihood of a sequence of words \(X = (x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n)\). The probability of the sequence can be factorized as \[P(X) = \prod_{t=1}^{n} P(x_t \mid x_1, x_2, \dots, x_{t-1}).\] In RNN-based language models, the hidden state \(h_t\) at each time step \(t\) is updated based on the previous hidden state and the current input word embedding \(x_t\): \[h_t = f(W_h h_{t-1} + W_x x_t + b_h).\] The output is then used to predict the next word in the sequence: \[P(x_{t+1} \mid x_1, \dots, x_t) = \text{softmax}(W_y h_t + b_y).\]

The introduction of the Transformer model, specifically BERT and GPT, has dramatically improved the performance of language models by enabling them to capture bidirectional or unidirectional context, respectively. BERT is trained to predict masked words in a sentence (masked language modeling), allowing it to learn bidirectional representations of text, while GPT is trained to predict the next word in a sequence (causal language modeling). The loss function used for training language models is typically the negative log-likelihood: \[\mathcal{L} = -\sum_{t=1}^{n} \log P(x_t \mid x_1, x_2, \dots, x_{t-1}).\] Pre-trained language models like BERT and GPT are fine-tuned on specific tasks, such as question answering or text classification, by adding task-specific output layers.

Mamba

More recently, state space models (SSMs) have re-emerged as a promising alternative to attention-based architectures for long-sequence modeling. Unlike self-attention, which explicitly computes pairwise interactions among all tokens, SSMs represent sequence dynamics through a hidden state that evolves over time. A simplified discrete-time SSM can be written as \[h_t = \bar{A}h_{t-1} + \bar{B}x_t, \qquad y_t = Ch_t + Dx_t,\] where \(x_t\) is the input token representation, \(h_t\) is the latent state, and \(y_t\) is the output representation. This formulation resembles recurrent models in that it updates a hidden state sequentially, but modern SSMs are designed with structured parameterizations and efficient parallel algorithms, allowing them to process long sequences more efficiently than standard RNNs.

Mamba further improves SSM-based sequence modeling by introducing a selective state space mechanism. The key idea is to make certain state-space parameters input-dependent, so that the model can selectively retain, update, or forget information according to the content of the current token. In a simplified form, this can be viewed as \[h_t = \bar{A}(x_t)h_{t-1} + \bar{B}(x_t)x_t, \qquad y_t = C(x_t)h_t,\] where the transition and output mechanisms depend on the input. This selectivity helps address a major limitation of earlier SSMs: fixed dynamics are efficient but may be less capable of content-based reasoning, which is crucial in language modeling.

Compared with Transformers, Mamba has two important advantages. First, it scales approximately linearly with sequence length, whereas standard self-attention has quadratic complexity in the sequence length. Second, it supports efficient autoregressive inference because the hidden state can be updated recurrently without recomputing attention over all previous tokens. Empirical studies show that Mamba achieves competitive performance on language modeling and other sequence modeling tasks, especially in settings involving long contexts. Therefore, Mamba can be viewed as a bridge between the sequential recurrence of RNNs and the content-aware representation learning of Transformers: it retains a compact recurrent state while introducing input-dependent selection to improve expressiveness.

3.4 Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement learning is a core area of ML that studies how agents learn to make sequential decisions by interacting with uncertain and dynamic environments. Unlike supervised learning, where models learn from labeled datasets, RL agents acquire optimal behaviors through trial and error, guided by feedback signals in the form of rewards or penalties. This learning paradigm has been widely applied in various domains, including robotics, game playing, and autonomous systems.

MDPs, introduced in Section 2.4, provide a useful formal mathematical framework for RL, since an MDP is a discrete-time process modeling sequential decision-making under uncertainty. An MDP captures environments where outcomes are influenced both by stochastic transitions and the agent’s actions. In the RL setting, the MDP is defined by a tuple \((S, A, P, R)\), where \(S\) is a finite set of states, \(A\) is a finite set of actions, \(P(s' \mid s, a)\) denotes the transition probability of moving from state \(s\) to state \(s'\) after taking action \(a\), and \(R(s, a)\) is the reward function that specifies the immediate (one-period) reward received for performing action \(a\) in state \(s\).

Recall that a policy \(\pi: S \rightarrow A\) is a mapping from states to actions, specifying what action to take in a given state, or a mapping from states to a probability distribution over the set of actions in the case of a randomized policy, written as \(\pi(a|s)\). The objective is to maximize cumulative discounted rewards given by \[\sum_{t=0}^T \gamma^t R(s_t,a_t),\] where \(s_t\) and \(a_t\) are the state and action taken at time \(t\), with \(a_t = \pi(s_t)\) or \(a_t \sim \pi(\cdot|s_t)\) for a randomized policy, and \(\gamma \in [0,1]\) is the discount factor.

RL often works with the Q-function, which gives the expected return of taking action \(a\) in state \(s\), and then following the optimal policy thereafter: \[Q(s,a) = R(s,a) + \sum_{s^\prime \in S} P(s^\prime \mid s,a) \gamma V^*(s^\prime), ~\forall s \in S,\] where recall that \(V^*\) is the optimal value function.

RL methods are designed to work without explicit knowledge of the underlying MDP, i.e., transition probabilities and reward functions may not be explicitly known but are learned from sampled data gathered through trial and error, experience, or simulation. One major category of classical RL methods is value-based approaches, which focus on learning the value function or action-value function representing expected cumulative rewards for states or state-action pairs. These methods derive policies indirectly from learned value functions without explicitly maintaining policies. A well-known example is Q-learning, an off-policy algorithm that learns the Q-function using the following update rule: \[Q(s_t,a_t) \gets Q(s_t,a_t) + \alpha \left[ R(s_t,a_t) + \gamma \max_a Q(s_{t+1},a) - Q(s_t,a_t) \right],\] where \(\alpha\) is the learning rate. The form of the update rule is the same as that of stochastic approximation methods treated in Section 2.3.

Q-learning guarantees convergence to the optimal Q-function as long as all state-action pairs are visited infinitely often and the learning rate diminishes appropriately. However, its main limitation is that it must store a value for each possible state-action pair, making it impractical if the state space or action space is too large, which is often the case in practical applications due to the curse of dimensionality.

DRL integrates RL with DL to handle large or continuous state spaces by approximating value functions or policies with neural networks, enabling learning from high-dimensional raw inputs such as images or audio.

A prominent DRL method is the Deep Q-Network (DQN), which extends Q-learning by using a neural network parameterized by \(\theta\) to approximate the Q-function \(Q(s,a;\theta)\). Instead of a Q-lookup table, the network takes state \(s\) as input and is able to output Q-values for all actions, allowing generalization across similar states and scalability to large spaces.

Training the Q-network involves minimizing the Bellman error: \[L(\theta) = \mathbb{E} \left[ \left( y_t - Q(s_t, a_t; \theta) \right)^2 \right],\] where the target \[y_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma \max_a Q(s_{t+1}, a; \theta^-),\] where \(r_{t+1} := R(s_{t+1},a_{t+1})\) and \(\theta^-\) denotes the parameters of a periodically updated target network used for stabilization. The network weights are updated via SGD to reduce this loss.

This framework enables DRL agents to effectively learn optimal policies in complex, high-dimensional environments beyond the reach of traditional tabular RL methods.

The key components of Deep Q-Learning (DQL) are the following:

  1. Experience Replay: One of the challenges in training neural networks in RL is the correlation between consecutive experiences, which can lead to instability during training. To address this, DQL uses an experience replay buffer. During training, the agent stores its experiences \((s_t, a_t, r_{t+1}, s_{t+1})\) in a replay buffer. When updating the Q-network, a mini-batch of past experiences is sampled randomly from this buffer, breaking the correlation between consecutive experiences and stabilizing training by providing a more diverse set of experiences.

  2. Target Network: Another innovation in DQL is the use of a target network to improve training stability. Instead of using the same network to compute both the current and target Q-values, DQL maintains a separate target network with parameters \(\theta^-\). This target network is used to compute the target Q-values \(\max_a Q(s_{t+1}, a; \theta^-)\), and its parameters are periodically updated to match the parameters of the Q-network \(\theta\). This prevents the network from chasing a moving target during training, which can lead to divergence.

  3. Epsilon-Greedy Exploration: In RL, there is a trade-off between exploration (trying new actions to discover more about the environment) and exploitation (choosing actions that are known to give high rewards). DQL typically uses an epsilon-greedy strategy for action selection. With probability \(\epsilon\), the agent chooses a random action to explore the environment, and with probability \(1 - \epsilon\), it selects the action with the highest Q-value (i.e., it exploits its current knowledge). The value of \(\epsilon\) is often decayed over time to encourage more exploration early in training and more exploitation as the agent learns.

Convergence of tabular Q-learning.

We next state a classical convergence result for tabular Q-learning, following the stochastic-approximation approach of Tsitsiklis (1994).

Consider a finite discounted MDP with discount factor \(\gamma\in(0,1)\). For each state–action pair \(x=(s,a)\), define the Bellman optimality operator \[(FQ)(s,a) := r(s,a)+\gamma \sum_{s'\in S} P(s' \mid s,a)\max_{a'\in A} Q(s',a').\] Then the optimal action-value function \(Q^*\) is the unique fixed point of \(F\), i.e., \[Q^* = FQ^*.\]

The tabular Q-learning update can be written as \[Q_{t+1}(s_t,a_t) = Q_t(s_t,a_t) + \alpha_t(s_t,a_t) \Bigl( r_t+\gamma \max_{a'}Q_t(s_{t+1},a')-Q_t(s_t,a_t) \Bigr),\] while \(Q_{t+1}(s,a)=Q_t(s,a)\) for \((s,a)\neq (s_t,a_t)\). Equivalently, for each \(x=(s,a)\), \[Q_{t+1}(x) = Q_t(x)+\alpha_t(x)\Bigl((FQ_t)(x)-Q_t(x)+M_{t+1}(x)\Bigr),\] where \[M_{t+1}(x) = \mathbf{1}\{x=(s_t,a_t)\} \Bigl( r_t+\gamma \max_{a'}Q_t(s_{t+1},a')-(FQ_t)(x) \Bigr)\] is a martingale-difference noise term conditional on the past.

Theorem (Almost sure convergence of Q-learning). Assume that:

  1. the state space and action space are finite;

  2. every state–action pair is visited infinitely often almost surely;

  3. for every \((s,a)\), the stepsizes satisfy \[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty} \alpha_t(s,a)=\infty, \qquad \sum_{t=0}^{\infty} \alpha_t(s,a)^2<\infty;\]

  4. rewards have bounded second moments.

Then \[Q_t(s,a)\to Q^*(s,a) \qquad\text{almost surely for every }(s,a)\in S\times A.\]

Proof (Proof sketch based on Tsitsiklis (1994)). The proof has three main steps.

Step 1: \(F\) is a contraction. For any two action-value functions \(Q\) and \(\widetilde Q\), \[\begin{aligned} |(FQ)(s,a)-(F\widetilde Q)(s,a)| &= \gamma\left| \sum_{s'}P(s' \mid s,a) \Bigl( \max_{a'}Q(s',a')-\max_{a'}\widetilde Q(s',a') \Bigr) \right| \\ &\le \gamma \sum_{s'}P(s' \mid s,a) \max_{a'}|Q(s',a')-\widetilde Q(s',a')| \\ &\le \gamma \|Q-\widetilde Q\|_\infty . \end{aligned}\] Taking the supremum over \((s,a)\) yields \[\|FQ-F\widetilde Q\|_\infty \le \gamma \|Q-\widetilde Q\|_\infty.\] Since \(\gamma<1\), \(F\) is a contraction in the sup norm. Hence, by Banach’s fixed-point theorem, it admits a unique fixed point \(Q^*\).

Step 2: Q-learning is an asynchronous stochastic approximation scheme. The update above has the form \[Q_{t+1}(x)=Q_t(x)+\alpha_t(x)\bigl((FQ_t)(x)-Q_t(x)+M_{t+1}(x)\bigr),\] where only the currently sampled component is updated. Moreover, conditional on the filtration generated by the past, \[\mathbb{E}[M_{t+1}(x)\mid \mathcal{F}_t]=0,\] so the noise is unbiased, and the bounded-second-moment assumption implies that its conditional variance is controlled.

Step 3: apply the asynchronous stochastic approximation theorem. Because \(F\) is a contraction, each component is updated infinitely often, and the stepsizes satisfy the Robbins–Monro conditions, the general convergence theorem for asynchronous stochastic approximation applies. Therefore, \[Q_t \to Q^* \qquad\text{almost surely}.\] This proves the result.
