展开完整原文（英文）

In this chapter, we present several applications in settings that highlight the synergy of simulation optimization and AI/ML models, spanning domains such as production planning, logistics and supply chain management, business strategy, and creative industries, where simulation provides an environment in which complex dynamics can be replicated, hypotheses can be tested, and learning algorithms can adapt through iterative feedback. Specifically, in multi-echelon inventory and revenue management, simulation captures demand variability and lead-time uncertainty, enabling reinforcement learning agents to experiment with replenishment and pricing strategies without incurring real-world costs. In production planning and scheduling, simulation allows the modeling of stochastic demand and resource constraints, supporting the evaluation of sequential optimization methods under realistic operating conditions. Simulated business environments recreate market dynamics so that policies for inventory, pricing, and recommendations can be trained and validated before deployment, and custom-designed business simulation games provide a controlled testbed for examining how AI entities interact with humans in strategic decision-making. In AI-driven fashion design, simulation captures multi-agent workflows that enable iterative refinement, allowing virtual agents to align designs with market feedback. Finally, in social media marketing, user-interaction simulations provide the feedback loops required for reinforcement learning algorithms to optimize targeting, bidding, and engagement strategies.

This chapter presents the following applications and case studies:

  • Section 5.1 presents a periodic-review serial supply chain system with multiple intermediate echelons between a manufacturer and customers. This work is based on Liu, Hu, et al. (2025; Zheng et al. 2024; Jiang et al. 2025).

  • Section 5.2 presents an example from supply chain production planning and scheduling, based on Liu, Alexopoulos, et al. (2025).

  • Section 5.3 presents the large management model (LMM) from Wang et al. (2022, 2024), including the Decision Transformer framework, and Section 5.4 presents generative agents and team cognition, based on Krol and Peng (2024).

  • Sections 5.5 and 5.6 conclude with futuristic AI use of simulation optimization for real-world business practice in fashion design and social media marketing, respectively.

The coordinated treatment spanning Sections 5.1–5.3 also draws on the integrated control framework and Pretrained Coordination Model in Anonymous authors (n.d.).

5.1 Inventory and Supply Chain Revenue Management

Inventory management is central to supply chain operations, with AI playing an increasingly important role in improving demand forecasting, inventory replenishment, and operational efficiency. RL, especially DRL, has emerged as a powerful tool for optimizing complex multi-echelon inventory management problems by modeling them as MDPs. Algorithms such as PPO and Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic (A3C) enable dynamic adaptation to uncertain and time-varying demand environments.

Within the MDP framework, each actor in the supply chain, such as manufacturers, distributors, and retailers, is represented as an intelligent agent that interacts with its environment to make optimal decisions. The goal for each agent is to minimize its costs related to inventory management, such as holding costs for excess stock, backlog costs for unmet demand, and ordering costs for replenishment. To achieve this, the RL agents learn from their environment by receiving feedback in the form of rewards, which they use to adjust their decision-making policies over time. In this context, the state of the system includes key variables such as the agent’s current inventory levels, outstanding orders, and in-transit shipments, whereas the action taken by each agent is the quantity of goods they order in the next period.

To account for the decentralized nature of decision-making in supply chains, where each actor has only partial visibility into the global system, the problem is modeled as a multi-agent system. In such a system, multiple agents must learn to coordinate their actions without direct access to the decisions of others. Multi-agent deep reinforcement learning is employed to facilitate this coordination, particularly through centralized training and decentralized execution. During training, the agents have access to global information about the entire supply chain, allowing them to learn from the collective experiences of all actors. However, once training is complete, each agent makes decisions based only on its local observations, such as its own inventory and customer demand. This approach mimics real-world supply chains, where constant information sharing is often impractical or too costly.

The specific algorithm used to train the agents in this framework is heterogeneous-agent PPO, which is a variant of PPO designed to accommodate the heterogeneous nature of agents in supply chains, where each actor may have different roles, objectives, and constraints. Heterogeneous-agent PPO ensures that each agent’s policy is updated sequentially, allowing them to adapt and improve their strategies as they learn from the dynamic environment of the supply chain. The algorithm adjusts the agents’ policies based on feedback from their own actions and those of other agents, ensuring that decisions are not only locally optimal but also beneficial for the overall system.

A key aspect of the RL approach is the design of the reward function, which balances the individual costs incurred by each agent with the overall costs of the system. The reward for each agent is a combination of its own costs (such as holding and backlog costs) and the total costs of the supply chain. This encourages each agent to act in a way that is beneficial to both itself and the broader system, fostering cooperation even in a decentralized setting. Interestingly, research indicates that the best performance is achieved when agents strike a balance between self-interest and consideration for the system as a whole, rather than solely focusing on minimizing the total system costs or their own costs. This balance ensures that the agents coordinate their actions effectively, leading to lower overall costs and improved supply chain performance.

Once trained, the agents execute their policies in a decentralized manner, making independent decisions based on local information without the need for constant communication. Despite acting independently, the agents’ learned policies are highly coordinated, allowing them to respond to demand fluctuations and inventory changes in a way that minimizes inefficiencies such as the bullwhip effect, where small variations in customer demand lead to increasingly larger fluctuations up the supply chain. By training the agents to make informed, coordinated decisions, multi-agent DRL helps smooth out these demand fluctuations, leading to more stable inventory levels, fewer stockouts, and reduced excess inventory.

The application of multi-agent DRL to multi-echelon inventory management provides a dynamic, adaptive approach to optimizing supply chain performance. By modeling the system as a set of interacting agents who learn from experience and adapt their decisions, the RL approach allows for better coordination across the supply chain. Ultimately, the RL-based solution reduces the costs of inventory management, alleviates the bullwhip effect, and leads to more resilient and efficient supply chains.

The general large-scale multi-product multi-echelon supply chain inventory management problem is formulated as an optimization problem, and the proposed solution approach comprises two phases: supervised pretraining and RL fine-tuning. In the DL pretraining phase, the objective is to build a model that emulates a target policy by using historical data or simulations to generate a suite of target actions, providing a robust foundation for what lies ahead. Throughout this process, the model learns to anticipate optimal ordering decisions, informed by the state of the inventory system. The target actions, derived from an ex-post optimal policy, serve as guiding labels, allowing the model to effectively discern the intricate relationships between states and actions. This type of training not only accelerates the forthcoming learning process but also captures essential inventory dynamics and demand patterns, setting the stage for a more adept RL agent.

In the RL fine-tuning phase, the focus shifts towards optimizing the pretrained model, refining its policy through interactions marked by trial and error within the simulation environment. Here, the pretrained DL model acts as a launchpad for the RL model, which embarks on a quest to explore a myriad of actions aimed at maximizing cumulative rewards dictated by a carefully defined reward function, one that often mirrors the costs incurred. In this phase, the RL agent draws lessons from the feedback received post-action, adjusting its policy in response to the rewards observed and the transitions between states. To balance between exploration of new actions and exploitation of established policies, strategies like epsilon-greedy or softmax are incorporated. This critical phase allows the model to deepen its understanding of the underlying inventory dynamics, culminating in the formulation of a more sophisticated and effective ordering policy. Ultimately, the interplay between DL pretraining and RL fine-tuning enables the method to surpass the capabilities of either approach alone.

Joint pricing and replenishment: Next we consider the joint pricing and replenishment problem, where at the beginning of each period of a finite-horizon problem, both the replenishment quantity and price for a product must be decided. Overall demand is inferred by modeling the probability of each individual consumer’s choice based on real-world data, but demand for a particular product each period is determined by our offer price, the competitor’s price, and the current reference price.

The sales profit is a function of actual sales minus holding, backlogging, and ordering costs.

Traditional retail practices often struggle with balancing the need to maintain inventory levels while maximizing profits through dynamic pricing. The challenge lies in the fact that decisions about pricing and replenishment are not always made at the same frequency and must consider various market factors, including competitive pricing and fluctuating demand. The fast-slow dual-agent DRL (FSDA DRL) approach for dynamic pricing and replenishment is a natural solution for the complex interplay between pricing strategies and inventory management.

The FSDA DRL algorithm introduces a two-time-scale SGD algorithm designed to handle the asynchronous nature of pricing and replenishment decisions and to be able to handle disparate settings in terms of the frequency of such decisions. The core of FSDA DRL is a dual-agent system, where one agent is responsible for pricing decisions and the other for replenishment. When a fast time-scale is used for pricing decisions and a slower time-scale for replenishment decisions, this allows the system to respond quickly to market changes by dynamically changing prices while also considering long-term inventory needs. On the other hand, when price changes are made on a slower time-scale compared to frequent replenishment decisions, the system is able to operate more like a lean just-in-time system to maintain profitability and market competitiveness by meeting dynamically changing demand.

Each agent is equipped with deep neural networks that approximate value and policy functions, enabling them to process continuous and high-dimensional features and actions. The agents operate within a Markov game framework, where each agent has an actor network that proposes actions and a critic network that evaluates the proposed actions, enabling the agents to learn from the environment and adapt their strategies over time. The algorithm can be proved to converge to high-quality solutions under appropriate conditions.

Using data-driven demand modeling, a decision tree-based ML model is trained using real market datasets to forecast market demand. The ML model learns patterns and relationships directly from the data, enabling more accurate demand forecasts and supporting more scientific decision-making. The process begins with the collection of a wide range of data points that could influence consumer demand, such as sales history, pricing trends, inventory levels, and external factors like competitor actions and market conditions. Each piece of data is then preprocessed to ensure its quality and suitability for analysis, including cleaning, normalization, and transformation into a format that can be effectively used by ML models.

The next phase involves the strategic engineering of features from the preprocessed data, which are critical indicators that can influence demand patterns. These features, such as price rank relative to competitors, the gap between current and reference prices, and the number of active competitors, are carefully selected to provide the most insightful view of the market dynamics. With these features, a sophisticated demand forecasting model, such as LightGBM, is trained to predict future demand based on historical patterns and trends. The choice of LightGBM is particularly strategic due to its efficiency and effectiveness in handling large datasets, making it an ideal candidate for real-time, high-stakes decision-making scenarios.

Integration of this demand forecasting model with the DRL agents is critical to the success of FSDA DRL, as the agents operating on different time scales use these forecasts to inform their pricing and replenishment strategies, ensuring that their actions are always aligned with the most current predictions of market demand. This real-time adaptability is a hallmark of the data-driven approach, allowing the system to respond swiftly to any changes in market conditions and consumer behavior. The adaptability of the data-driven approach is further underscored by its continuous learning capability. As new data becomes available, the demand forecasting model updates its predictions, feeding the latest insights into the DRL agents. This ongoing process of learning and adaptation ensures that the system remains agile and responsive, keeping pace with market changes and maintaining a competitive edge.

The data-driven approach also excels in its ability to eliminate the need for preset assumptions about market behavior, which can often be limiting and sometimes misleading. By learning directly from the data, the system can develop a more nuanced and accurate understanding of the market, leading to more effective pricing and replenishment strategies. This is particularly evident in the experimental validation of the FSDA DRL model, where the data-driven approach consistently outperforms traditional baseline policies, delivering higher rewards and profits across various scenarios. Moreover, the data-driven approach provides a solid foundation for scientific decision-making, transforming the process into one that is grounded in empirical evidence and data-driven insights. This not only enhances the performance of the DRL model but also establishes a new benchmark for decision-making in the retail industry, where accuracy, adaptability, and responsiveness are paramount.

Recommendation and inventory management: By formulating the coordination of the inventory management and recommendation system as an MDP, DRL can be used to jointly consider the two problems, which is carried out using a recommendation system that adjusts the exposure situations of products to influence customers’ ratings among them.

A standard multi-product single-echelon inventory system with lost sales is considered, where unmet demand is lost.

To solve the MDP, the decision policy is parameterized as a conditional distribution over the action space using a neural network or a cluster of neural networks, which is then trained via policy-based DRL algorithms, e.g., PPO.

Integrated Inventory, Pricing, and Recommendation Control

The preceding models coordinate selected pairs of decisions. A broader formulation treats production, inventory, pricing, and recommendation as levers of one finite-horizon control problem (Anonymous authors, n.d.). The important distinction is not merely the larger action vector. Production and replenishment determine future availability, price changes the total level of demand, and recommendation reallocates demand across products. These decisions therefore share both the inventory transition and the system-level profit function.

Consider \(n\) products and a one-period replenishment lead time. Before the decision in period \(t\), the joint state is \[s_t=\bigl(q_{t-1},d_{t-1},I_t\bigr),\] where \(q_{t-1}\) is the vector of production orders in transit, \(d_{t-1}\) is the most recent demand signal, and \(I_t\) is on-hand inventory. The joint action \[a_t=\bigl(q_t,p_t,k_t\bigr)\] contains production or replenishment quantities, prices, and recommendation intensities. For product \(i\), inventory and lost sales evolve as \[I_{i,t+1}=\bigl[I_{i,t}+q_{i,t-1}-d_{i,t}\bigr]^+, \qquad \ell_{i,t}=\bigl[d_{i,t}-I_{i,t}-q_{i,t-1}\bigr]^+.\] The delayed arrival of \(q_{i,t-1}\) makes a demand-side action today relevant to production decisions made both earlier and later.

One transparent demand model begins with the price-sensitive potential \[g_i(p_{i,t})=D_i^{\max}-\alpha_i p_{i,t}.\] If \(k_{i,t}\) denotes recommendation intensity, its share of exposure can be represented by \[\rho_i(k_t)= \frac{\exp(\beta_1 k_{i,t})} {\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp(\beta_1 k_{j,t})}.\] The resulting mean demand used in the integrated model is \[\bar d_{i,t}(p_t,k_t) =g_i(p_{i,t}) \bigl(1+\beta_2\rho_i(k_t)\bigr) \left(1+\beta_3\frac{k_{i,t}-1}{4}\right),\] with realized demand generated stochastically around this mean. Price thus controls market volume, whereas the softmax term couples all products by allocating a limited amount of customer attention.

A common reward makes the economic trade-offs explicit: \[r_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n} \left[ p_{i,t}d_{i,t}-c_i^q q_{i,t}-h_i I_{i,t} -c_i^{\ell}\ell_{i,t}-c_i^a(k_{i,t}-1) \right].\] Here \(c_i^q\), \(h_i\), \(c_i^{\ell}\), and \(c_i^a\) denote production, holding, lost-sales, and recommendation costs. Under the potential-revenue convention used in the reference model, \(c_i^{\ell}\) includes the margin and service consequences of unfulfilled demand. The objective is to maximize \(\mathbb{E}[\sum_{t=0}^{T}\gamma^t r_t]\).

This representation clarifies when joint control is valuable. If transitions, feasible actions, and rewards separate by process and product, independent controllers can be sufficient. When capacity, inventory, or demand shaping couples the modules, a pricing engine that cannot observe inventory or a recommendation engine that ignores availability can incur a genuine information-structure loss. The computational study in Anonymous authors (n.d.) finds the largest and most stable gains in the more strongly coupled and nonstationary cases. It also identifies a boundary: under sufficiently high demand volatility, return-conditioned offline learning can mistake favorable noise for a good action, and an online RL policy may have a higher mean return. Independent stress testing across demand regimes is therefore part of the control design, not merely a final reporting step.

5.2 Supply Chain Production Planning and Scheduling

Integration of AI into modern production planning and scheduling has facilitated more efficient and adaptive manufacturing. Here, we consider the stochastic multi-level lot-sizing problem (SMLP), which includes strategic decision-making for production volumes across various levels of a supply chain, where the volatility of external demands adds a layer of complexity. At the core of the methodology is the general production function, a versatile tool that encapsulates the dynamics linking production decisions to actual output. This function is essential in formulating the problem, providing a mathematical representation of the production process that can accommodate diverse production scenarios and constraints.

The SMLP aims to manage production and inventory across a network of products where demands are uncertain. The SMLP is formulated as an MDP, where the state includes all environmental factors affecting production, and the action is the current production decision. The immediate reward in this context is the production quantity, a function of the production decision. The objective is to find a production policy that minimizes the total expected discounted cost over an infinite planning horizon, where the cost structure includes costs associated with holding inventory, backorders, and setup expenses. The production function governs the quantity produced based on the production decision, adjusting inventory levels to meet expected demand while considering production feasibility.

The Markovian demand model posits that the external demand for each final product depends solely on a demand state, which encapsulates all environmental factors affecting demands and evolves according to a homogeneous Markov chain. This probabilistic model of demand is crucial for capturing the stochastic nature of market demands and its impact on production planning. Individual product states, production states, and demand states are modeled into a comprehensive system state, a key step in formulating the dynamic program. This aggregated state provides a holistic view of the system, enabling the development of policies that consider the interdependencies between products and time periods.

Given the complexity of managing a joint production policy across multiple products, the problem employs sequential policy optimization (SPO) to make it computationally feasible. Instead of solving a single policy for all products at once, this approach iteratively optimizes each product’s policy while keeping the others fixed. In each iteration, the policy for one product is updated to minimize the discounted cost over an infinite horizon under the current policies of other products. Sequential optimization continues until there is no further improvement, indicating a Nash equilibrium, where no single product’s policy can improve without adjusting others. This structured approach simplifies policy development within an MDP framework, allowing a more manageable optimization of the objective function over the long term.

The optimal policy is identified as a stationary policy that minimizes the expected total cost, derived from the dynamic program. This policy is characterized by a mapping from the system state to a production decision that minimizes the immediate cost plus the discounted expected future cost, given the current state. The SPO procedure breaks down the complex task of optimizing the joint policy for all products into a series of simpler, separate optimization problems for each product. The policy for each product is updated iteratively while keeping the policies of other products fixed, based on their latest iterations. This method is particularly effective in large-scale problems, where joint optimization of all products’ policies would be computationally intractable.

DRL is applied to implement the SPO procedure. DRL is adept at solving optimization problems with complex state spaces and unknown transition probabilities, making it an ideal tool for our method. Applying DRL to each separate optimization problem can effectively learn the optimal policy for each product, considering the uncertainties and interdependencies inherent in the production process.

Production Planning within a Coordinated Control System

The integrated formulation above gives production planning a system-wide role rather than treating it as an isolated quantity decision (Anonymous authors, n.d.). Let \(q_{i,t}\) be the quantity initiated for product \(i\) in period \(t\). A representative shared-capacity constraint is \[\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i,t}\leq Q^{\max}, \qquad q_{i,t}\geq0,\] and the corresponding production cost is \[C_t^{\mathrm{prod}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_i^q q_{i,t}.\] With a positive lead time, the production vector enters the pipeline rather than on-hand inventory immediately. Hence the planner must allocate current capacity using anticipated margins, price-sensitive demand, recommendation exposure, on-hand stock, and arrivals already in transit. The resulting cross-product competition for capacity is precisely what a collection of independently optimized product policies can miss.

At the aggregate level, the coordinated MDP determines quantities and their timing. In a practical planning hierarchy, those quantities become targets for a lower-level scheduling model that assigns jobs to machines, shifts, and production lines. Feedback should run in both directions: detailed scheduling returns feasible completion times and effective capacity to the aggregate controller, while the aggregate controller supplies product priorities derived from inventory risk and demand-side value. This separation keeps the action space manageable without discarding the economic coupling between production and revenue management.

The offline-learning pipeline in Anonymous authors (n.d.) uses two complementary trajectory sources. An interpretable traditional controller combines an \((s,S)\)-type replenishment rule, a price obtained from an estimated linear demand model, and a margin-based recommendation rule. PPO supplies a second set of trajectories generated by adaptive online interaction with the simulator. Pooling these logs exposes the learner to both stable operating rules and responses to stochastic cross-process effects. A return-conditioned sequence model can then distill a joint production policy without repeating costly online exploration at deployment.

Several diagnostics are especially relevant for production planning. First, capacity sensitivity should be evaluated by changing \(Q^{\max}\) and unit production costs, rather than reporting performance at one nominal setting. Second, shock-response experiments should record how capacity is reallocated across products and how long inventories take to return to their pre-shock range. In the reference experiments, a step demand shock causes the learned policy to differentiate recovery speeds according to unit cost and price elasticity instead of increasing every product uniformly. Third, nonstationary tests should combine trend, seasonality, and temporal correlation. These experiments showed that learning-based policies could substantially improve on base-stock and \((s,S)\) rules while reducing profit variability, although the results are mechanism evidence from simulation and not a universal dominance claim.

The main modeling lesson is that detailed scheduling efficiency and economic coordination answer different questions. A schedule can be locally efficient yet produce the wrong product mix, whereas a profitable aggregate plan can be operationally infeasible. A simulation-based interface between the two levels is needed to evaluate both feasibility and end-to-end profit.

5.3 Large Management Model (LMM)

The large management model (LMM) provides a general framework for decision making in the realm of business management, representing an innovative synthesis of DRL and AI. LMM is a theoretical construct and a practical framework designed to address the multifaceted and interconnected challenges inherent in today’s volatile business environments.

The necessity for such a model is underscored by the complexities of industries such as the fashion industry, where supply and demand fluctuations, seasonal trends, and the unpredictability of global supply chains generate managerial challenges not readily addressed using conventional approaches. LMM seeks to meet these challenges by creating an adaptive, intelligent system capable of making sophisticated, real-time decisions that are informed by a deep understanding of historical patterns and current market dynamics.

At the core of the LMM is its foundational decision model, which employs an autoregressive architecture to analyze and learn from vast datasets. This model is inspired by the success of LLMs, which have demonstrated an uncanny ability to generate human-like text based on contextual cues. Similarly, the LMM’s model is designed to assimilate and optimize past decision trajectories, allowing it to make informed predictions and strategic choices that are tailored to the unique demands of each business scenario.

One of the key strengths of the LMM is its ability to integrate what were once considered separate domains of business management into a unified framework. Inventory management, dynamic pricing, and recommendation systems no longer operate in isolation but are instead treated as interconnected components of a larger system. This integration, illustrated in Figure 5.1, allows for a more holistic approach to decision-making, where actions in one area can be intelligently coordinated with those in another, leading to synergies that enhance overall operational efficiency and profitability.

The LMM’s application of DRL is what sets it apart from traditional management models. DRL enables the model to interact with its environment, learning and adapting through a process of trial and error. This dynamic capability allows the LMM to respond to new information and changing conditions with agility, making it a versatile tool for navigating the uncertainties of the business world.

The potential of the LMM is vast, and its development represents a significant step towards the realization of artificial general intelligence (AGI) in the field of business management. As the model continues to evolve, it is expected to encompass an ever-widening array of management functions, further enhancing its capabilities and solidifying its role as a pioneering force in the convergence of AI and business strategy. The LMM represents a paradigm shift in the way businesses approach management decision-making. Its integration of advanced AI techniques, its ability to learn and adapt, and its holistic approach to managing the complexities of modern business operations make it a formidable tool for businesses looking to navigate the challenges of the future with confidence and foresight.

A Management World Model for Coordinated Operations

The Pretrained Coordination Model (PCM) provides a concrete operational instantiation of an LMM (Anonymous authors, n.d.). Its purpose is not to reproduce raw observations or to act as a general conversational model. Rather, it is a queryable surrogate for a coupled management system. Given the history \[h_t=(s_{1:t},a_{1:t-1}),\] a management world model is the learned triple \[\mathcal{W}=(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{O},\mathcal{G}),\] where \[\mathcal{D}(h_t,a_t)=\widehat s_{t+1},\qquad \mathcal{O}(h_t,a_t)=\widehat r_t,\qquad \mathcal{G}(h_t,z)=a_t.\] The dynamics operator \(\mathcal{D}\) predicts the next operating state, the outcome operator \(\mathcal{O}\) evaluates the consequence of a contemplated joint action, and the generation operator \(\mathcal{G}\) produces an action for managerial target \(z\).

These operators define a capability ladder. A behavioral model uses \(\mathcal{G}\) to imitate or synthesize actions from logged trajectories. A predictive model adds \(\mathcal{D}\) and \(\mathcal{O}\) so that actions not taken in the historical logs can be evaluated. An imaginative model searches over candidate actions by rolling them forward through the learned dynamics and outcome operators. This separation is useful because each capability has a different data requirement and can be validated independently.

Return-conditioned policy generation.

PCM casts a logged trajectory as a sequence of returns, states, and actions. At period \(t\), the return-to-go target is \[z_t=\sum_{u=t}^{T}\gamma^{u-t}r_u.\] After embedding the sequence, the Transformer represents the conditional joint-action distribution \[\pi_\theta\bigl(a_t\mid z_{1:t},s_{1:t},a_{1:t-1}\bigr).\] With discretized action components, training minimizes the negative log-likelihood of the logged actions. This shifts much of the estimation burden from online policy gradients and bootstrapping to offline supervised learning. Pooling trajectories from different incumbent controllers is coherent only when the return target remains inside the support of the data; conditioning above the maximum supported return is an extrapolation rather than a justified request.

Product-token architecture.

A flat network ties its input and output dimensions to the number of products. PCM instead creates one token per product and period, combining production in transit, lagged demand, inventory, production quantity, price, recommendation, and return-to-go. Shared weights impose the useful prior that products are exchangeable up to their operational state. Two attention pathways then represent the main couplings:

  1. cross-product attention captures shared production capacity and the reallocation of demand caused by recommendation;

  2. temporal attention captures inventory evolution, delayed production, and demand persistence within each product.

The reported implementation consequently uses 294,659 policy parameters for assortments ranging from 2 to 100 products. This does not make the decision problem independent of size, but it prevents the model architecture itself from growing with every additional product.

Prediction, coverage, and guarded planning.

The same product-token backbone supports a dynamics head for state increments and an outcome head for system profit. The policy head and the predictive heads should not be trained on exactly the same data distribution. High-return expert logs are appropriate for action generation, whereas counterfactual prediction requires coverage around actions that the expert did not take. PCM therefore augments the dynamics and outcome data with perturbed exploratory actions while keeping the behavioral policy focused on strong demonstrations.

At deployment, the planner begins with actions proposed by the behavioral PCM and an incumbent heuristic, adds local perturbations, and evaluates short candidate sequences in the learned model. Let \(\widehat V_H(a)\) denote the predicted return of a candidate over planning horizon \(H\), and let \(\Delta_H\) bound accumulated model error. The planner departs from the better base sequence only when \[\max_{a\in\mathcal{C}(s_t)}\widehat V_H(a) \geq \widehat V_H(a^{\mathrm{base}})+2\Delta_H.\] In the deterministic, uniformly bounded-error setting, this guard prevents a model-based change from falling below the base comparison beyond the stated error allowance. Since \(\Delta_H\) grows with rollout depth, the result favors short receding-horizon plans anchored to strong operating policies. Under stochastic demand, the no-regression property must be checked empirically rather than assumed.

Evidence and deployment implications.

The experiments in Anonymous authors (n.d.) are designed as mechanism evidence. With 512 logged trajectories and two products, the product-token policy matched the full-information heuristic reference. As the assortment grew to 100 products, it recovered roughly 50–64% of the reference profit while retaining a constant parameter count and sub-5-ms behavioral latency. At ten products, increasing the log volume to 1,024 trajectories raised recovery to 85.5%, indicating that action and state coverage, rather than network width alone, was limiting performance. Adding predictive heads and guarded planning increased profit over the heuristic reference by 19.1% at two products and by 3.0% at ten products, the latter lying within experimental dispersion, with approximately 4–6 ms of planning time per decision.

These results suggest a practical deployment sequence: diagnose whether cross-process coupling is material before replacing modular controllers; retain high-return logs for policy distillation; collect safe local action variation for counterfactual modeling; validate state and reward predictions off-policy; and enable planning only behind an error-calibrated guard. The world-model view therefore adds an auditable decision process to the broader LMM vision: generate, predict, validate, and only then plan.

Sequential Modeling for Management Problems

The Decision Transformer is a framework that abstracts RL problems into a sequence modeling challenge, harnessing the prowess of the Transformer architecture. This innovative approach allows the Decision Transformer to tackle a wide array of management problems by processing and learning from extensive datasets. The true potential of the Decision Transformer lies in its scalability and the ability to generalize across different decision-making scenarios. As the model is exposed to a larger volume of diverse data, it refines its understanding and predictive capabilities, thereby enhancing its decision-making prowess. This large-scale learning capability is crucial, as it equips the Decision Transformer with the flexibility to adapt to new situations and generalize solutions across various contexts, providing a robust decision-making paradigm for a multitude of management challenges.

A key feature of the Decision Transformer is its use of ‘Return-to-Go’ (RTG) conditioning. In this framework, RTG represents the cumulative reward that the agent aims to achieve from any given point onward in a trajectory. By conditioning on RTG, the model effectively adapts its action predictions based on desired outcomes, treating RL as a goal-conditioned task. During training, sequences of (state, action, RTG) tuples are input to the model, where each element is embedded similarly to tokens in a standard Transformer. Through this setup, the model conditions its behavior on both the trajectory’s historical context and the specified target reward. The RTG mechanism also enables conditional decision-making, allowing the Decision Transformer to flexibly pursue different reward levels without needing retraining for each target, thus achieving a level of goal adaptation that would otherwise require separate policies in traditional RL.

The training process of the Decision Transformer diverges from conventional RL algorithms by employing supervised learning instead of a reward-driven objective. Specifically, it minimizes the MSE between predicted and actual actions from recorded trajectories, avoiding the challenges of estimating a value function or handling sparse rewards. This approach not only simplifies the training pipeline but also allows the model to learn effectively from offline RL data, where a diverse dataset of past experiences informs its behavior without requiring active interaction with the environment.

By transforming RL into a sequence modeling problem, the Decision Transformer captures long-term dependencies more effectively than traditional methods. This setup is particularly beneficial in tasks with delayed rewards or intricate dependencies across time steps, where traditional RL algorithms may struggle. Additionally, by training on a range of RTG conditions, the Decision Transformer achieves a degree of zero-shot generalization, adapting to new reward levels without additional training, a significant advantage in settings where flexibility is essential. Its reliance on offline data also positions it as an ideal solution for environments where interaction is costly, such as robotics or healthcare, and it has demonstrated notable success across both discrete and continuous action spaces, including standard RL benchmarks like Atari and OpenAI Gym.

In redefining RL as sequence modeling, the Decision Transformer overcomes several challenges that conventional RL methods face, such as the need for extensive exploration and sensitivity to reward sparsity. By leveraging the Transformer’s ability to model complex temporal dependencies and integrating RTG for flexible goal achievement, the Decision Transformer offers an efficient, adaptable, and scalable approach to decision-making, opening new avenues in fields where data constraints and operational costs limit the feasibility of traditional RL.

When applied to inventory management, the Decision Transformer excels by amalgamating a vast array of data points, such as historical sales data, supplier information, and market trends, to anticipate future demand and optimize inventory levels. It forecasts demand accurately, facilitating strategic decisions on stock replenishment and reducing the likelihood of stockouts or overstocking. The model’s ability to simulate various supply chain scenarios aids in the dynamic optimization of ordering quantities and timings, ensuring cost-effective inventory management that is responsive to fluctuating market demands.

In the realm of pricing strategies, the application of the Decision Transformer enables businesses to implement dynamic pricing that is sensitive to real-time market conditions and consumer behaviors. It can analyze competitor pricing strategies and assess demand elasticity to inform decisions that are both competitive and profitable. Furthermore, the Decision Transformer assists in planning promotional activities by identifying optimal timing and pricing strategies to maximize profitability.

For recommendation systems, the Decision Transformer facilitates the generation of personalized and sequenced product recommendations, thereby enhancing customer engagement. By analyzing customer preferences and behaviors over time, the Decision Transformer can identify patterns and trends to provide the most relevant and timely recommendations. This approach not only improves customer satisfaction but also aligns promotional efforts with inventory levels, ensuring that recommendations are both desirable and feasible.

The implementation of the Decision Transformer in these domains underscores the importance of high-quality data and continuous learning to refine the model’s predictions and recommendations. As the model encounters new data, it evolves, improving its decision-making capabilities. Integration with existing systems can lead to automated and streamlined decision-making processes, while maintaining transparency in the model’s decision-making process ensures alignment with broader business objectives and ethical standards. The scalability and generalizability of the Decision Transformer make it a formidable tool for acquiring robust decision-making abilities across a wide spectrum of management problems.

5.4 Generative Agents and Team Cognition

Generative agents, capable of mimicking human-like behaviors such as decision-making, communication, and task coordination, have emerged as potential active contributors to team environments. Unlike passive AI tools, these agents can participate dynamically in group tasks, demonstrating autonomy in their interactions and adaptability to evolving scenarios. Despite advancements in AI integration at individual levels – such as improving personal productivity or creativity – there remains a significant gap in understanding the collective impact of generative agents within teams.

The integration of AI agents into teams is not just a trend but a necessity. Integrating generative agents as full team members into teams, however, might have consequences for team dynamics as well as team cognition. To evaluate the deployability and effects of generative agents on team cognitions, the research reported here investigates the transformative role of generative agents – AI entities powered by LLMs such as GPT-4 – within team-based environments. The core objective is to explore how the replacement of a human team member with a generative agent impacts the development of a transactive memory system (TMS: how team members collectively encode, store, and retrieve knowledge through coordination, specialization, and credibility) and team performance. How team members’ attitudes toward AI moderate these dynamics offers both theoretical and practical insights into the integration of AI into collaborative processes.

Participants are organized into teams engaged in a custom-designed business simulation game. This simulation replicates the pressures and complexities of real-world strategic decision-making within a fictional startup environment. Teams are divided into two groups: a control group composed entirely of human members and a treatment group where a generative agent replaces one human team member in either a strategist or data analyst role. This substitution mirrors realistic organizational scenarios, providing a controlled context to assess the agent’s integration and impact.

The business simulation game requires teams to allocate resources, develop strategies, and respond to market feedback to maximize profitability over ten periods. The presence of generative agents in the treatment group introduces an additional dynamic, where agents contribute knowledge, coordinate tasks, and interact with team members through a simulated chat interface modeled after corporate communication tools like Slack or Microsoft Teams.

For organizations considering the integration of AI into team structures, actionable insights highlight the importance of fostering positive attitudes toward AI, emphasizing the role of trust, transparency, and effective communication in successful human-AI collaboration, in addition to offering guidance on the strategic deployment of generative agents in roles that align with their strengths, such as information analysis and structured planning.

In strategic decision-making scenarios, the divergence between generative agents and human decision-making becomes particularly pronounced, given the high stakes and the complexity inherent in shaping an organization’s path. Generative agents, with their ability to process and analyze vast datasets, can identify patterns and trends that might inform strategic decisions. For instance, an AI might analyze market data to predict future trends, assess the viability of new product lines, or evaluate the potential impact of a merger or acquisition based on historical precedents and financial metrics.

Generative agents can also simulate various scenarios to predict outcomes and risks associated with different strategic choices, providing a quantitative foundation for decision-makers. They can run thousands of simulations to determine the most effective marketing strategy, optimize supply chain logistics, or forecast the financial implications of entering new markets. This level of analysis can be incredibly valuable in making informed, strategic decisions that are grounded in data.

However, while generative agents excel in quantitative analysis, human decision-makers bring qualitative insights to the table. Humans can consider the cultural fit of a potential merger, the ethical implications of a new technology, or the long-term vision and values of the organization. They can weigh the intangible aspects of a decision, such as employee morale, brand reputation, and customer loyalty, which are often critical in strategic planning but are difficult to quantify.

Moreover, humans possess the ability to recognize and adapt to emerging trends that may not yet be reflected in the data. They can intuit opportunities and threats that arise from political, social, or technological shifts, and adjust strategies accordingly. This adaptability is crucial in a rapidly changing business environment where the success of a strategy may hinge on the ability to respond quickly to unforeseen circumstances. Additionally, human decision-makers can engage in a process of collaborative sense-making with stakeholders, incorporating diverse perspectives and building consensus around a shared vision. This is particularly important in strategic decision-making, where alignment across the organization is essential for successful implementation. Generative agents, while capable of facilitating communication through platforms, do not yet possess the emotional intelligence or the ability to build genuine relationships that are necessary for this type of collaborative process.

Generative agents can support human decision-makers by providing them with the data and analysis they need to make informed choices, while also freeing them from the burden of processing large volumes of information manually. By working together, generative agents and humans can create a synergistic approach to strategic decision-making, where the strengths of both are leveraged to develop comprehensive strategies that consider both quantitative data and qualitative insights.

Generative agents offer a powerful tool for strategic decision-making, providing data-driven insights and enabling the exploration of complex scenarios. Yet, human decision-makers remain indispensable for their ability to navigate the uncharted territories of business strategy, where creativity, intuition, and an understanding of human dynamics are paramount. The most effective strategic decisions are likely to be those that harness the precision of AI analysis and the depth of human judgment.

5.5 AI for Fashion Design

The traditional fashion industry is characterized by a complex and time-consuming design process that begins with extensive market research. Designers often rely on a combination of field studies, social media analytics, and feedback from retailers to gather this information, which can be slow and may not always be comprehensive or up-to-date.

The limitations of the traditional fashion design process are multifaceted and deeply rooted in the industry’s historical practices and structures. One of the primary constraints is the inherently manual and time-intensive nature of creating new designs. Designers must conduct extensive market research to identify trends, which is a process that often relies on intuition and personal experience rather than data-driven insights. This can result in a lack of objectivity and potential oversight of emerging trends.

Moreover, the design phase itself is laborious, involving the collection of inspiration, selection of colors and materials, and the creation of patterns and decorations. This process requires a significant investment of time and resources, with designers often needing to iterate on their ideas multiple times to perfect a collection. The iterative nature of design means that it can take months to move from concept to a final product, during which time trends may shift and consumer preferences may evolve.

Another limitation is the subjectivity inherent in the design process. Fashion design is an art form, and as such, it is influenced by the personal tastes and vision of the designer. While this creativity is a cornerstone of the industry, it can also lead to designs that do not resonate with the broader market or that fail to meet the diverse needs and preferences of consumers.

The industry also faces challenges in terms of collaboration and communication. Designers, pattern makers, and production teams often work in silos, which can lead to miscommunication and inefficiencies. This lack of integration between different stages of the design and production process can result in delays and increased costs.

Furthermore, the traditional fashion design process can struggle to keep pace with the rapid changes in consumer behavior and market demands. The long lead times from concept to market mean that by the time a product is ready for sale, it may already be out of step with current trends. This can lead to overproduction and waste, as unsold inventory accumulates and becomes outdated.

In terms of intellectual property, the fashion industry is also fraught with challenges. With the rise of fast fashion, there is an increased risk of design piracy and the erosion of creative rights. Designers must navigate a landscape where their work can be quickly copied and mass-produced by larger, more cost-effective brands.

Finally, the traditional fashion design process often lacks the flexibility to respond to real-time market feedback. Designers may not have immediate access to consumer insights or the ability to quickly pivot their designs based on changing preferences. This can result in a disconnect between what is being created and what consumers actually want to purchase.

Multi-Agent Fashion Design

The multi-agent AI workflow for fashion design is an advanced and dynamic approach that harnesses the power of collaboration among various specialized AI agents. This system is designed to mimic the way human teams work, with each agent contributing unique insights and skills to the design process.

An example is depicted in Figure 5.2, where the workflow begins with a user request for a girl’s purple unpatterned hoodie, which initiates the AI system’s collaborative process. The system assembles an Agent Group consisting of specialized agents, each playing a unique role. The Market Analyst Agent Group is the first to engage, analyzing current market trends and consumer preferences using sophisticated tools. These agents employ forward reasoning to predict how these trends will evolve and how the new design might align with the changing market landscape. Simultaneously, the Designer Agent Group begins conceptualizing the hoodie, leveraging their design expertise to explore various possibilities, such as fabric choices, silhouettes, and style details. Their forward reasoning also considers how these design elements will resonate with market trends identified by the Market Analyst Group. As the initial design concepts start to take shape, the Web Celebrity and Internet Celebrity Agent Groups provide feedback on the design from a promotional and consumer perspective. They ensure that the design has the potential to succeed in the digital space, considering its appeal and marketability. Throughout this process, a continuous feedback loop is established, where each agent group’s input informs the next iteration of the design. The feedback is not only used to refine the design but also to train the system in an RL framework. This training process helps the AI model improve its predictions and decisions over time, with each iteration informed by the rewards and penalties derived from previous design outcomes.

The RL aspect of the workflow allows the system to learn from the interactions and feedback of the different agent groups, improving its ability to predict consumer preferences, fine-tune the design, and optimize market positioning. The iterative refinement process ensures that the Market Analyst Group continuously adjusts its predictions, the Designer Group enhances the design details, and the Web Celebrity and Internet Celebrity Groups maintain the design’s relevance in the promotional landscape. The outcome is a design that is aesthetically compelling and strategically aligned with market demands.

Once the design has undergone multiple cycles of feedback and refinement, the final product emerges, ready for output. This iterative, RL-powered approach ensures that the AI system not only delivers a design that meets the user’s request but also creates a product that is primed for success in the market. By continuously learning from past projects, the system becomes more adept at navigating the complexities of fashion design, making it a powerful tool for future innovation in the fashion industry.

5.6 AI for Social Media Marketing

Integrating AI into social media marketing is driven by the dynamic and data-intensive nature of these platforms. AI’s ability to process and analyze large volumes of data in real-time provides a significant advantage in understanding and predicting consumer behavior. By leveraging ML algorithms, AI can identify patterns and trends that might be imperceptible to human analysts, allowing marketers to create highly targeted campaigns that resonate with specific audience segments. This level of personalization not only improves the effectiveness of marketing messages but also enhances user experience by delivering content that is genuinely relevant and engaging.

To incorporate an RL perspective into the social media marketing framework, we can view the marketing process as an ongoing interaction with the environment, composed of the audience and their behaviors. In this context, RL can enhance the personalization and optimization processes by treating each interaction as a ‘state’ and each marketing action, such as post creation, ad targeting, or customer engagement, as an ‘action’. The goal is to maximize long-term rewards, such as engagement rates, conversions, or brand loyalty, through the refinement of strategies over time.

For instance, RL algorithms can optimize content delivery by continuously learning from user interactions. By tracking how users engage with different types of content, the system can adjust its content strategy to target segments more effectively, learning from feedback to improve over time. This iterative learning process enables marketers to fine-tune their campaigns based on real-time data, identifying not just what works, but also predicting what will work next.

Moreover, RL can be used to dynamically adjust marketing budgets and bids for ads in real-time, based on observed outcomes. By treating each interaction as a ‘reward’, RL systems can learn optimal bidding strategies that maximize the return on ad spend. In addition, RL agents can personalize the timing of social media posts by learning the best times to post for maximum audience engagement, adjusting the schedule based on evolving user behavior.

Sentiment analysis, combined with RL, can be further enhanced by adapting responses to customer feedback in real-time. The system can adjust its approach to customer service and brand messaging based on sentiment trends, evolving strategies in response to negative or positive feedback, and fine-tuning communication strategies over time.

The workflow for social media marketing involves a series of interconnected steps, each managed by a specialized role or intelligent agent within a system designed to create and distribute engaging content. A detailed description of the workflow is as follows, depicted in Figure 5.3 for the example of generating an online short video:

1. Market Expert: The process begins with a market expert who has a deep understanding of current affairs and the ability to identify trending topics that are relevant to the target audience. This expert is responsible for the initial selection of hot topics that can be leveraged for marketing content.

2. Theater Designer: Once a topic is selected, the theater designer steps in. This role involves someone with a high aesthetic sense who can plan and construct unique scenes. They take the input of the hot topic and the target customer group description to create a setting that will be visually appealing and contextually relevant for the content.

3. Copywriter: With the scene set, the copywriter then takes the story text and crafts the accompanying narrative. This includes creating the title, description, and hashtags that will resonate with the audience and encourage engagement.

4. Photographer: After the narrative is established, the photographer, who is skilled in various styles of photography and has a keen sense of aesthetics, takes over. They are responsible for capturing the scenes with the clothing, models, and locations as specified, producing a series of photos that will be used in the final content.

5. Video Editor: The photos and the story are then handed over to the video editor. This role involves someone who can efficiently combine various video elements, including the photos, subtitles, title, description, and background music, to create a compelling video that captures the audience’s attention.

6. Account Operator: Finally, the completed video and its accompanying text are given to the account operator. This individual is meticulous and follows a careful process to manage the social media accounts, ensuring that the video is posted with the correct title, description, and hashtags, and that it reaches the intended audience.

7. Reflection and Optimization: A unique aspect of this workflow is the built-in reflection and learning phase after each task is completed. Each intelligent agent in the workflow is designed to reflect on their work and learn from it, continuously optimizing their approach for future tasks.

This workflow is designed to be efficient and adaptable, with each role contributing to a cohesive end product that is a social media post or campaign. It leverages the strengths of different experts and uses technology to enhance the process, ensuring that the content is both engaging and effective in reaching the target audience.

The integration of AI into social media marketing brings a multitude of strengths that are reshaping the industry. One of the most notable advantages is the efficiency with which AI can produce content, as evidenced by the ability to generate a piece in just 15 minutes, a pace that far surpasses traditional human-led processes. This rapid content creation not only ensures a consistent flow of fresh material but also allows for a swift response to trending topics, capitalizing on the ephemeral nature of social media engagement.

Moreover, the consistency of AI in producing content is exemplified by the stable volume of output, which is crucial for maintaining a regular brand presence. This reliability is further underscored by the AI’s capability to avoid the pitfalls of content scarcity that can plague human operators, such as a lack of material or themes. The AI’s resourcefulness ensures a continuous stream of relevant and engaging content, which is essential for keeping the audience’s interest and driving meaningful interactions.

The performance of AI-operated content, as measured by average playback volume, also stands out, with the data suggesting that AI can outperform human operations. This superior performance indicates that AI-driven content resonates well with audiences, likely due to its ability to tailor content to align with specific brand styles and requirements. The customization of content to fit the brand’s aesthetic, including the styling of models and the selection of shooting scenes, further enhances the brand’s message and appeal.

Scalability is another significant benefit of AI in social media marketing. As demand increases or as brands look to expand their reach across various platforms, AI can scale up content production without a corresponding increase in labor or resources. This scalability is particularly valuable for brands with global audiences or those looking to quickly ramp up their social media presence.

Furthermore, AI’s ability to optimize content over time through learning and data analysis is a key strength. By leveraging insights from consumer behavior and feedback, AI can fine-tune marketing strategies to improve engagement and better meet audience expectations. This data-driven approach to content and strategy optimization is a powerful tool for enhancing marketing effectiveness.

While not explicitly highlighted in the image, the cost-effectiveness of AI is an inherent advantage. By reducing the labor costs associated with content creation and management, AI can offer significant savings, especially when considering the volume and speed of content production it enables.

上一章下一章 · 结论

结论

第 06 章 · 章节速读

仿真提供试验环境，优化寻找更好的方案，AI 学习复杂关系。三者结合，能够支持更灵活的决策。

建立反馈循环
把建模、学习、决策和评估连接起来。
面向动态环境
让策略随新数据和环境变化持续调整。
用实验验证价值
在新样本和真实约束下，检验性能、稳定性与适用范围。

回到四个实验：练习建模、学习、比较与验证这条完整路径。

展开完整原文（英文）

This review underscores the potential transformative synergy between AI and SO for addressing the growing complexity of decision-making in modern dynamic and uncertain environments. The integration of advanced AI/ML techniques, such as DRL, multi-agent systems, and ANN-based predictive models, with detailed simulation models and state-of-the-art optimization algorithms enhances operational efficiency, predictive accuracy, and adaptability across various domains, including production planning, supply chain management, financial planning, and strategic marketing.

The synergistic relationship between AI and SO supports a robust, data-driven framework that not only manages large-scale datasets and high-dimensional complexities but also dynamically adapts to the reality of constantly changing environments. Harnessing AI’s predictive power and learning capabilities facilitates a more efficient and agile decision-making process, one that continuously evolves through real-time feedback and iterative SO learning algorithms, leading to the realization of truly intelligent digital twins.
